2.
Generalities

In this section, we first collect some basic facts about coherent sheaves on
\mathds{P}^{2}
and

K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}
, Bridgeland stability conditions
\Stab\mathcal{C}

on the derived category
\mathcal{C}=D^{b}(\Coh_{c}K_{\mathds{P}^{2}})
of compactly supported
sheaves, and identify the slice
\Pi\subset\Stab\mathcal{C}
of
physical stability conditions.

2.1.
Gieseker-stable sheaves on
\mathds{P}^{2}

Given a coherent sheaf
E
on
\mathds{P}^{2}
, we denote its rank by
r(E)
,
its degree by
d(E)=\int_{\mathds{P}^{2}}c_{1}(E)\cdot H
(where
H
is the hyperplane section generating
H^{2}(\mathds{P}^{2},\mathds{Z})
), its second Chern character by
\textnormal{ch}_{2}(E)
, and by
\gamma(E)
the
Chern vector
[r,d,\textnormal{ch}_{2}]
valued in
\mathds{Z}\oplus\mathds{Z}\oplus\frac{1}{2}\mathds{Z}
. We denote by
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathds{P}^{2}}(0)
the structure sheaf, with Chern vector
[1,0,0]
, and by

\mathcal{O}_{C}
the structure sheaf of the rational curve
C
in the hyperplane class,
with Chern vector
[0,1,-\frac{1}{2}]
.

For any pair of coherent sheaves,
the Euler form
\chi(E,E^{\prime})
is given by the Riemann–Roch formula

(2.1)

\displaystyle\chi(E,E^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\dim\Hom(E,E^{\prime})-\dim\Ext^{1}(E,E^{\prime})+\dim\Ext^{2}(E,E^{\prime})

\displaystyle=rr^{\prime}+\frac{3}{2}(rd^{\prime}-r^{\prime}d)+r\operatorname{ch}^{\prime}_{2}+r^{\prime}\textnormal{ch}_{2}-dd^{\prime}

In particular for
E=\mathcal{O}
, the Euler characteristic

(2.2)

\chi(E)\coloneqq\chi(\mathcal{O},E)=\dim H^{0}(E)-\dim H^{1}(E)+\dim H^{2}(E)=r(E)+\frac{3}{2}d(E)+\textnormal{ch}_{2}(E)

is an integer. With some abuse of notation, we also denote by
\gamma(E)
the vector

[r,d,\chi)
, valued in
\mathds{Z}^{3}
(note the round closing bracket, to distinguish it from the vector

[r,d,\textnormal{ch}_{2}]
). We denote the antisymmetrized Euler form
also known as Dirac-Schwinger-Zwanzinger pairing) by
12
12

12

Our convention for the
antisymmetrized Euler form is consistent with

[
29
,
20
]
and opposite to that in

[
13
,
14
,
23
,
22
]

(2.3)

\langle\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\rangle\coloneqq\chi(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})-\chi(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma)=3\left(rd^{\prime}-r^{\prime}d\right)

For a coherent sheaf
E
with
\rk(E)\neq 0
, we define the slope
\mu(E)

and discriminant
\Delta(E)
by

(2.4)

\mu(E)\coloneqq\frac{d(E)}{r(E)},\quad\Delta(E)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\mu(E)^{2}-\frac{\textnormal{ch}_{2}(E)}{r(E)}

and denote them by
\mu_{\gamma}
and
\Delta_{\gamma}

(when
r(E)=0
and
d(E)\neq 0
, we set
\mu(E)=+\infty
).
Under tensoring with the
m
-th power of the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{C}
, the Chern vector transforms
as

(2.5)

\gamma\mapsto\gamma(m)\coloneqq[r,d+mr,\textnormal{ch}_{2}+md+\frac{r}{2}m^{2}]

such that
\mu\mapsto\mu+m
while
\Delta
is invariant. In particular, the ‘fluxed D4-brane’

\mathcal{O}(m)
has Chern vector
[1,m,\frac{1}{2}m^{2}]
, slope
m
and vanishing discriminant.
A sheaf with
r\neq 0
is said to be normalized if its slope
\mu
lies in the interval
(-1,0]
.

A coherent sheaf
E
on
\mathds{P}^{2}
is said to be of pure dimension
n
if the dimension of the
support of any non-zero subsheaf (including
E
itself) is of complex dimension
n
.
A torsion-free sheaf
E
is said to be slope-semistable if it is of pure dimension 2 and if for any
subsheaf
F\subset E
one has
\mu(F)\leq\mu(E)
. It is Gieseker-semistable
if it is of pure dimension 2 and if for any
subsheaf
F\subset E
one has
\mu(F)\leq\mu(E)
, with
\Delta(F)\geq\Delta(E)
in
case of equality. Gieseker stability is defined by requiring
\Delta(F)>\Delta(E)
in
case
\mu(F)=\mu(E)
, and slope stability by requiring
\mu(F)<\mu(E)
for any proper subsheaf. In particular, slope-stability implies Gieseker stability, which implies Gieseker semistability, which implies slope-semistability.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma)
be the moduli space of Gieseker-semistable sheaves
with Chern vector
\gamma=[r,d,\textnormal{ch}_{2}]=[r,d,\chi)
(the rationale for the notation
\infty
will become apparent in §
4.1
). If
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma)
is not empty, then it
is a normal, irreducible, factorial projective variety of dimension

(2.6)

\dim_{\mathds{C}}\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma)=\dim\Ext^{1}(E,E)=r^{2}(2\Delta-1)+1

Moreover, it is smooth whenever
[r,d,\chi)
is a primitive vector in
\mathds{Z}^{3}
, such that semi-stable sheaves are automatically stable.

In order to state the condition for
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma)
to be non-empty, we consider
exceptional stable sheaves, defined as those for which
\Hom(E,E)=\mathds{C},\Ext^{1}(E,E)=\Ext^{2}(E,E)=0
.
Such sheaves are then necessarily homogenous stable vector bundles, and have
a trivial moduli space.
They are entirely specified by their slope
\mu
, which can take value in an infinite set
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathds{Q}
, called the set of exceptional slopes. For
\mu=\frac{p}{r}\in\mathcal{E}
with
r>0
and
(p,r)
coprime, the exceptional bundle of slope
\mu
has rank
r
and discriminant
\Delta_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{1}{r^{2}})
. The set

\mathcal{E}
is the union of an increasing family
\mathcal{E}_{n}\subset\mathcal{E}_{n+1}
obtained by the following recursive construction:
\mathcal{E}_{0}=\mathds{Z}
and
\mathcal{E}_{n+1}
is obtained from
\mathcal{E}_{n}
by adjoining the slopes

(2.7)

\mu=\frac{1}{2}(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2})+\frac{\Delta_{\mu_{2}}-\Delta_{\mu_{1}}}{3+\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}

between any consecutive slopes
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
in
\mathcal{E}_{n}
.
For example, after four steps one gets

(2.8)

\mathcal{E}\supset\mathcal{E}_{4}\cap[0,1]=\left\{0,\frac{13}{34},\frac{5}{13},\frac{75}{194},\frac{2}{5},\frac{179}{433},\frac{12}{29},\frac{70}{169},\frac{1}{2},\frac{99}{169},\frac{17}{29},\frac{254}{433},\frac{3}{5},\frac{119}{194},\frac{8}{13},\frac{21}{34},1\right\}

The exceptional bundles of integer slope are the structure sheaves
\mathcal{O}(m)
, while the
exceptional bundle of half-integer slope
m-\frac{3}{2}
is
the twisted cotangent bundle
\Omega(m)
, defined by the exact sequence

(2.9)

0\rightarrow\Omega(m)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(m-1)^{\oplus 3}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(m)\rightarrow 0

with Chern vector
\gamma=[2,2m-3,m^{2}-3m+\frac{3}{2}]
.

For any Chern vector
\gamma=[r,d,\chi)
with
r>0
,
d\in\mathds{Z}
,
\chi\in\mathds{Z}
,
the condition for
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma)\neq\emptyset
is then
[
41
]

(2.10)

\Delta(\gamma)\geq\delta_{\rm LP}(\mu(\gamma))\qquad\mbox{or}\quad(\mu(\gamma)\in\mathcal{E}\ \mbox{and}\ \Delta(\gamma)=\Delta_{\mu(\gamma)})

where
\delta_{\rm LP}(\mu)
is the ‘Drézet–Le Potier curve’

(2.11)

\delta_{\rm LP}(\mu)\coloneqq\sup_{\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E},\,|\mu^{\prime}-\mu|<3}\left[P(-|\mu^{\prime}-\mu|)-\Delta_{\mu^{\prime}}\right]

where
P(x)=\frac{1}{2}(x^{2}+3x+2)
(see Figure
12
). In particular,
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma)

is empty unless
the Bogomolov bound
\Delta(\gamma)\geq 0

is satisfied.

Figure 12.
Drézet–Le Potier curve
\delta_{\rm LP}(\mu)
(in blue) and discriminants
\Delta_{\mu}
of exceptional sheaves (in red).

We define the refined Gieseker index
\Omega_{\infty}(\gamma)
as the Poincaré-Laurent polynomial for the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma)
,

(2.12)

\Omega_{\infty}(\gamma)=\sum_{p\geq 0}b_{p}(\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma))(-y)^{p-\dim_{\mathds{C}}\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma)}

In the limit
y\to 1
,
\Omega_{\infty}(\gamma)
reduces to the signed Euler characteristic

(-1)^{\dim_{\mathds{C}}\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma)}e(\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma))
.
When the inequality (
2.10
) is saturated,
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma)

has Picard rank
b_{2}(\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma))=1
, or
2
when the inequality is strict.
The Gieseker index for sheaves on
\mathds{P}^{2}
with arbitrary rank was determined in
[
42
]
, by relating it to the Gieseker index for sheaves on the Hirzebruch surface

\mathds{F}_{1}
(which coincides with the blow-up of
\mathds{P}^{2}
at one point) and using wall-crossing arguments.
Quite remarkably, the generating series of Gieseker indices with fixed rank
r
and degree
d

are conjectured to transform as mock Jacobi forms of the parameters
(\tau,w)
conjugate to the
second Chern class
\textnormal{ch}_{2}
and Betti degree
p
in (
2.12
)
[
43
]
. The simplest case is for
rank
1
, where the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}([1,0,1-n))
coincides with the Hilbert scheme of
n
points on
\mathds{P}^{2}
, and the
generating series is an actual Jacobi form
[
35
]
,

(2.13)

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 0}\Omega_{\infty}([1,0,1-n))\,q^{n}=\frac{\mathrm{i}q^{1/8}(y-1/y)}{\theta_{1}(q,y^{2})}

\displaystyle=1+(y^{2}+1+1/y^{2})q+(y^{2}+1+1/y^{2})^{2}q^{2}+(y^{6}+2y^{4}+5y^{2}+6+\dots)q^{3}+\dots

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle y\to 1}}{{\to}}1+3q+9q^{2}+22q^{3}+51q^{4}+108q^{5}+221q^{6}+429q^{7}+810q^{8}+1479q^{9}+\dots

where
\theta_{1}(q,y)=\mathrm{i}\sum_{r\in\mathds{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}(-1)^{r-\frac{1}{2}}q^{r^{2}/2}y^{r}
is the Jacobi theta series (the dots in the middle line indicate the obvious additional terms required for invariance under
y\mapsto 1/y
).
Further examples of generating series of Gieseker indices of rank
r>1
can be found in
[
23
, §A]
.

The notion of Gieseker semi-stable sheaves extends to vanishing rank as follows.
A torsion sheaf
E
is Gieseker-semistable if it is of pure dimension 1 (hence
d(E)\neq 0
) and if for any proper subsheaf
F\subset E
one has
\nu(F)\leq\nu(E)
, with
\nu(E)=\frac{\chi(E)}{d(E)}=\frac{\textnormal{ch}_{2}(E)}{d(E)}+\frac{3}{2}
.
The moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma)
for
\gamma=[0,d,\chi)
is non-empty for any
d>0
, and is invariant under
(d,\chi)\mapsto(d,d+\chi)
and
(d,\chi)\mapsto(d,-\chi)
. As in the torsion-free case, it is a normal, irreducible, factorial projective variety of dimension

(2.14)

\dim_{\mathds{C}}\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma)=\dim\Ext^{1}(E,E)=d^{2}+1

and it is smooth whenever
(d,\chi)
are coprime.
For
\gamma=[0,1,1)
, corresponding to a D2-brane wrapped on a curve
C
in the linear system

|H|
, one has
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma)=\mathds{P}^{2}
. The Gieseker index, defined in the same way as
in (
2.12
), turns out to be completely independent of
\chi

[
44
]
, and
related to the refined Gopakumar-Vafa invariants

N_{d}^{(j_{L},j_{R})}

[
36
,
37
]
via

(2.15)

\Omega_{\infty}([0,d,\chi))=\sum_{j_{L},j_{R}}(-1)^{2j_{L}+2j_{R}}\chi_{j_{L}}(y)\chi_{j_{R}}(y)N_{d}^{(j_{L},j_{R})}

where
\chi_{j}(y)=\sum_{m=-j}^{j}y^{-2m}=\frac{y^{2j+1}-y^{-2j-1}}{y-1/y}
is the character of the spin-
j
representation of
SU(2)
. The identification of the two fugacities
y_{L}
and
y_{R}
conjugate to

j_{L}
and
j_{R}
is natural in the Nekrasov-Shatashvilii limit of the refined topological string amplitude.
Using the refined BPS invariants in
[
45
, Table 2 p.61]
, we find for degree up to 6,

(2.16)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\infty}([0,1,\chi))

\displaystyle=y^{2}+1+1/y^{2}

\displaystyle\Omega_{\infty}([0,2,\chi))

\displaystyle=-y^{5}-y^{3}-y-1/y-1/y^{3}-1/y^{5}

\displaystyle\Omega_{\infty}([0,3,\chi))

\displaystyle=y^{10}+2y^{8}+3y^{6}+3y^{4}+3y^{2}+3+\dots

\displaystyle\Omega_{\infty}([0,4,\chi))

\displaystyle=-y^{17}-2y^{15}-6y^{13}-10y^{11}-14y^{9}-15y^{7}-16y^{5}-16y^{3}-16y-\dots

\displaystyle\Omega_{\infty}([0,5,\chi))

\displaystyle=y^{26}+2y^{24}+6y^{22}+13y^{20}+26y^{18}+45y^{16}+68y^{14}+87y^{12}

\displaystyle+100y^{10}+107y^{8}+111y^{6}+112y^{4}+113y^{2}+113+\dots

\displaystyle\Omega_{\infty}([0,6,\chi))

\displaystyle=-y^{37}-2y^{35}-6y^{33}-13y^{31}-29y^{29}-54y^{27}-101y^{25}

\displaystyle-169y^{23}-273y^{21}-401y^{19}-547y^{17}-675y^{15}-779y^{13}

\displaystyle-847y^{11}-894y^{9}-919y^{7}-935y^{5}-942y^{3}-945y+\dots

In §
4.3
, §
4.4
and §
5.4
we reproduce some of the invariants (
2.13
) and (
2.16
) from attractor flow trees in the large volume and orbifold scattering diagrams.

2.2.
Derived category of coherent sheaves on
K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}

The total space
X=K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}
of the canonical bundle over
\mathds{P}^{2}
is a non-compact, smooth
Calabi-Yau threefold, obtained as a crepant resolution of the quotient
\mathds{C}^{3}/\mathds{Z}_{3}
with diagonal action
z_{i}\mapsto e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}/3}z_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
. We denote by
\Coh_{c}X
the Abelian category
of compactly supported coherent sheaves on
X
.
A compactly supported coherent sheaf
E

is equivalent to a pair
(F,\phi)
where
F=\pi_{*}(E)\in\Coh\mathds{P}^{2}

is the push forward of
E
, and
\phi\colon F\to F\otimes K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}
is a morphism
(see e.g.
[
46
, §8.1]
). We abuse notation
and denote by
\operatorname{ch}(E)=\operatorname{ch}(F)=[r,d,\chi)
the Chern character of
F
.
Physically,
F
describes the gauge field on a stack of
r
D4-branes wrapped on
\mathds{P}^{2}
, while
\phi
is the Higgs field describing fluctuations in the fiber direction
K_{X}
. Given a sheaf
F

on
\mathds{P}^{2}
, we denote by
i_{\star}(F)=(F,0)
its embedding along the zero section.

As explained for example in
[
47
]
,
the category of BPS states in type IIA string theory
on
\mathds{R}^{3,1}\times X
coincides with the bounded derived category
\mathcal{C}=D^{b}(\Coh_{c}X)
of coherent sheaves on
X
with compact support.
An object

E\in\mathcal{C}
is a complex of coherent sheaves
\dots\to E^{-1}\to E^{0}\to E^{1}\to\dots
of arbitrary (but finite) length, where the component
E^{k}
in cohomological degree
k
is a coherent sheaf
with compact support on
X
.
We denote by
\mathcal{H}^{k}(E)
the
cohomology of the complex at the
k
-th place,
and by
\operatorname{ch}(E)\coloneqq\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\operatorname{ch}(E^{k})
the Chern character of the complex.
The homological shift
E\mapsto E[1]
taking the complex
(E^{k})_{k\in\mathds{Z}}
to
(E^{k-1})_{k\in\mathds{Z}}
maps D-branes with charge
\gamma=\operatorname{ch}(E)=[r,d,\chi)

to anti-D-branes with opposite charge
-\gamma
.

By Serre duality, the Euler form on

X
coincides with the antisymmetrized Euler form (
2.3
) on
\mathds{P}^{2}

[
46
, Corollary 8.2]
,

(2.17)

\chi_{X}(E,E^{\prime})\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{3}(-1)^{k}\dim\Ext^{k}_{X}(E,E^{\prime})=3(rd^{\prime}-r^{\prime}d)

where
\operatorname{ch}(E)=[r,d,\chi)
and
\operatorname{ch}(E^{\prime})=[r^{\prime},d^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})]
.
For
E=i_{*}(F)
and
E^{\prime}=i_{*}(F^{\prime})
, the extension groups on
X
can be computed
from those on
\mathds{P}^{2}
via (
2.18
),

(2.18)

\Ext_{X}^{k}(i_{*}F,i_{*}F^{\prime})=\Ext^{k}_{\mathds{P}^{2}}(F,F^{\prime})\oplus\Ext^{3-k}_{\mathds{P}^{2}}(F^{\prime},F)

In particular, an exceptional sheaf
F
on
\mathds{P}^{2}
lifts to a spherical object
S=i_{*}(F)
in
\mathcal{C}
, i.e. an object such that
\Ext_{X}^{k}(S,S)\simeq\mathds{C}
for
k=0,3
and zero otherwise.

Starting from the
Ext-exceptional collection on
\mathds{P}^{2}
given by

(2.19)

F_{1}=\mathcal{O}[-1],\quad F_{2}=\Omega(1),\quad F_{3}=\mathcal{O}(-1)[1]

and embedding it along the zero section,
one obtains a tilting sequence
\mathcal{S}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{3}E_{i}
of objects
E_{i}=i_{*}(F_{i})
on
X

which generate the category
\mathcal{C}
and such that
\Ext^{k}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S})=0
for
k\neq 0
.
Physically, these objects correspond to the fractional branes of the superconformal field theory
on the orbifold
\mathds{C}^{3}/\mathds{Z}_{3}
.
The category
\mathcal{C}
is then equivalent to the derived category of representations of the Jacobian algebra
13
13

13

Recall that the Jacobian algebra
J(Q,W)
is the quotient of the path algebra
by the ideal generated by relations
\{\partial_{a}W:a\in Q_{1}\}
.

J(Q,W)
for a quiver with potential
(Q,W)
associated to
\mathcal{S}
.
The corresponding quiver (shown in Figure
4
) in has 3 nodes corresponding to
each fractional brane, with
3
arrows
a_{i}\colon E_{2}\to E_{1}
,
b_{j}\colon E_{3}\to E_{2}
,
c_{k}\colon E_{1}\to E_{3}
in agreement with

\Ext^{1}(E_{i},E_{i-1})=\mathds{Z}^{3}
(with index
i
identified modulo
3
).
The potential
W=\sum_{i,j,k}\epsilon_{ijk}\mbox{Tr}(a_{i}b_{j}c_{k})
can be determined by studying
the
A_{\infty}
structure on the derived category of coherent sheaves
[
48
]
, or
read off from the associated brane tiling
[
49
]
.

The derived category
\mathcal{C}
admits a large group of auto-equivalences
\Aut(\mathcal{C})
, generated by the
homological shift
E\mapsto E[1]
, by the translation
E\mapsto E(1)=E\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(1)
,
by spherical twists
\ST_{\mathcal{O}}
with respect to the spherical object
\mathcal{O}
,
and by automorphisms of
X

itself (or rather, its formal completion at
\mathds{P}^{2}
). For any spherical object
S
, the spherical twist

\ST_{S}
acts on
E\in\mathcal{C}
via
[
50
]

(2.20)

\ST_{S}\colon E\mapsto\Cone\left(\Hom^{\bullet}_{K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}}(S,E)\otimes S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm ev}}{{\to}}E\right)

where
\Cone(f)

is defined by the exact triangle
A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}B\to\Cone(f)\to A[1]
. As a result

\ST_{S}
maps the Chern vector

(2.21)

\operatorname{ch}E\mapsto\operatorname{ch}E-\langle\operatorname{ch}S,\operatorname{ch}E\rangle\operatorname{ch}S

As shown in
[
31
]
, the translation
E\mapsto E(1)
and the spherical twist
\ST_{\mathcal{O}}

generate
\Gamma_{1}(3)
, the subgroup of
SL(2,\mathds{Z})
matrices defined below (
1.4
).

2.3.
Stability conditions and Donaldson-Thomas invariants

A stability condition on a triangulated category

\mathcal{C}
with Grothendiek group
\Gamma
consists of a pair
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
such that
[
4
]

i)

\mathcal{A}
is the heart of a bounded
t
-structure, in particular it is an Abelian subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
;

ii)

Z\colon\Gamma\to\mathds{C}
is a linear map, called the central charge;

iii)

For any
0\neq E\in\mathcal{A}
,
Z(E)=\rho(E)e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\phi(E)}
where
\rho(E)>0

and
0<\phi(E)\leq 1
; in other words,
Z(E)
is contained in
\mathds{H}_{B}=\mathds{H}\cup(-\infty,0)
;

iv)

(Harder-Narasimhan property)
Every
0\neq E\in\mathcal{A}
admits a finite filtration
0\subset E_{0}\subset E_{1}\cdots\subset E_{n}=E
by objects
E_{i}
in
\mathcal{A}
, such that each factor
F_{i}\coloneqq E_{i}/E_{i-1}
is
\sigma
-semistable
and
\phi(F_{1})>\phi(F_{2})>\ldots>\phi(F_{n})
;

v)

(Support property)
There exists a quadratic form
Q
on
\Gamma\otimes\mathds{R}
such that the kernel of
Z
in

\Gamma\otimes\mathds{R}
is negative definite with respect to
Q
, and moreover, for any

\sigma
-semistable object
E\in\mathcal{A}
,
Q(\gamma(E))\geq 0
. Equivalently
[
6
]
,
there exists a non-negative constant
C
such that, for all
\sigma
-semistable object
E\in\mathcal{A}
,

(2.22)

\|\gamma(E)\|\leq C\,|Z(E)|

where
\|\cdot\|
is a fixed Euclidean norm on
\Gamma\otimes\mathds{R}
.

In the last two items above, we define
\sigma
-semistability of an object
F\in\mathcal{A}

by requiring that
\phi(F^{\prime})\leq\phi(F)

for every non-zero subobject of
F
. Unlike common practice, we do not declare
that homological shifts
F[k]
of a
\sigma
-semistable object
F
are also stable,
but we compensate for this in the definition of the DT invariants below.

According to
[
4
]
, the space of stability conditions
\Stab(\mathcal{C})

is a complex manifold of dimension
\rk\Gamma
, such that the map
\Stab(\mathcal{C})\to\Hom(\Gamma,\mathds{C})
which sends
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})\mapsto Z
is a local homeomorphism of
complex manifolds. Moreover, it admits an action of
\widetilde{GL^{+}}(2,\mathds{R})\times\Aut(\mathcal{C})

[
4
, Lemma 8.2]
,
where
\widetilde{GL^{+}}(2,\mathds{R})
is the universal cover of the group of
2\times 2
real matrices with
positive determinant. The group
GL(2,\mathds{R})^{+}
acts on the central charge
Z
via

(2.23)

\begin{pmatrix}\Re Z\\
\Im Z\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Re Z\\
\Im Z\end{pmatrix}\ ,\quad ad-bc>0

preserving the orientation on
\mathds{R}^{2}
. Its universal cover acts on the stability condition
(Z,\mathcal{A})

by suitably tilting the heart
\mathcal{A}
. The subgroup
\mathds{C}^{\times}
of matrices of the form

\lambda(\begin{smallmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{smallmatrix})

with
\lambda>0
preserves the complex structure and has trivial stabilizers, so the quotient

\Stab(\mathcal{C})/\mathds{C}^{*}
is still a complex manifold. When
\mathcal{C}
is a category of coherent sheaves, there is a notion of derived duality
14
14

14

Physically,
\gamma\mapsto\gamma^{\vee}
corresponds
to time reversal
T
, which reverses the sign of the Dirac pairing, while
\gamma\mapsto-\gamma

corresponds to CPT symmetry.

E\mapsto E^{\vee}
,
and therefore an
involution sending
(Z,\mathcal{A})\mapsto(Z^{\vee},\mathcal{A}^{\vee})
where

\mathcal{A}^{\vee}
is the derived dual of
\mathcal{A}
(up to tilt) and
Z^{\vee}(E):=-\overline{Z(E^{\vee})}
.
This involution allows to extend
\widetilde{GL^{+}}(2,\mathds{R})
to the full group
\widetilde{GL}(2,\mathds{R})
.

For any class
\gamma\in\Gamma
and stability condition
\sigma\in\Stab(\mathcal{C})
,
we denote by
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma)
the moduli stack of
\sigma
-semistable objects of charge

\pm\gamma
in
\mathcal{A}
. We further denote by

\Omega_{\sigma}(\gamma)
the motivic Donaldson-Thomas invariant of

\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma)
.
Informally, one can think of
\Omega_{\sigma}(\gamma)
as the Poincaré-Laurent polynomial of the cohomology with compact support
of
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma)
,
with a cohomological shift ensuring that

(2.24)

\Omega_{\sigma}(\gamma)=\sum_{p\geq 0}b_{p}(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma))(-y)^{p-\virdim_{\mathds{C}}\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma)}

where
\virdim_{\mathds{C}}
is the virtual dimension and
y^{2}=\mathds{L}
is the motive of the affine line (see Appendix
D
for a more precise mathematical definition).
We further define the rational DT invariant
\bar{\Omega}_{\sigma}(\gamma)
via (
1.1
).
By construction, both
\Omega_{\sigma}(\gamma)
and
\bar{\Omega}_{\sigma}(\gamma)
are
invariant under the action of
\widetilde{GL^{+}}(2,\mathds{R})\times\Aut(\mathcal{C})
, in particular
under
\gamma\mapsto-\gamma
, as well as derived duality acting on
(\sigma,\gamma)
.

These invariants are locally constant on
\Stab\mathcal{C}
but may jump when some object
E\in\mathcal{A}

of charge
\gamma
goes from being stable to unstable. This may happen when the central charge
Z(\gamma^{\prime})
of a subobject
E^{\prime}\subset E
of charge
\gamma^{\prime}
becomes aligned with

Z(\gamma)
, therefore along the
real-codimension one
wall of marginal stability

(2.25)

\mathcal{W}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\coloneqq\{\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})\in\Stab\mathcal{C}:\Im(Z(\gamma^{\prime})\overline{Z(\gamma)})=0\}

The discontinuity across
\mathcal{W}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
is determined from the invariants on either side of the wall by the wall-crossing formulae of
[
6
,
7
]
.

2.4.
Stability conditions on
K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}
and
\Pi
-stability

The space of Bridgeland stability conditions
\Stab(\mathcal{C})
on
\mathcal{C}=D^{b}(\Coh_{c}K_{\mathds{P}^{2}})

was studied in
[
30
,
31
]
. After fixing the
\mathds{C}^{\times}
action
such that skyscraper sheaves have central charge
Z_{[0,0,1]}=-1
, the central charge
can be parametrized by the two complex coefficients
(T,T_{D})
in (
1.3
),

(2.26)

Z(\gamma)=-rT_{D}+dT-\textnormal{ch}_{2}

We define
s=\frac{\Im T_{D}}{\Im T}
, in such a way that
\Im Z(\gamma)=\Im T(d-rs)
.
We then denote by

•

\Coh_{c}^{\leq s}
the subcategory of
\Coh_{c}(X)
generated (under extensions) by slope-semistable torsion-free
sheaves of slope
\frac{d}{r}\leq s

•

\Coh_{c}^{>s}
the subcategory of
\Coh_{c}(X)
generated by slope-semistable torsion-free
sheaves of slope
\frac{d}{r}>s
and by torsion sheaves

•

\Coh_{c}^{\sharp s}
the subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
of objects
E
such that
\mathcal{H}^{i}(E)=0

for
i\neq-1,0
,
\mathcal{H}^{-1}(E)\in\Coh_{c}^{\leq s}
,
\mathcal{H}^{0}(E)\in\Coh_{c}^{>s}

The Abelian category
\mathcal{A}(s)=\Coh_{c}^{\sharp s}
is obtained by the standard tilt
procedure from the torsion pair

(\Coh_{c}^{\leq s},\Coh_{c}^{>s})
and is the heart of a bounded
t
-structure on

\mathcal{C}
.
For
\Im T>0
, the construction ensures that
\Im Z(\gamma)\geq 0

for any
E\in\mathcal{A}(s)
. In addition, if
\Im Z(\gamma)=0
, namely

s=\mu
, we get

(2.27)

\Re Z(\gamma)=r\left(w-\frac{\textnormal{ch}_{2}}{r}\right)=r\left(w-\frac{1}{2}s^{2}+\Delta\right)

where we defined
w=-\Re T_{D}+s\Re T
as in (
1.7
).
The coordinates
(s,w)
in fact parametrize the orbits of the action
of
\widetilde{GL^{+}}(2,\mathds{R})
on
\Stab(\mathcal{C})
in the region
\Im T>0
,
such that the central charge (
2.26
)
is in the same orbit as the function used in
[
51
]

(2.28)

Z^{LJ}_{(s,w)}(\gamma)=(w-\mathrm{i}s)r+\mathrm{i}d-\textnormal{ch}_{2}=(rw-\textnormal{ch}_{2})+\mathrm{i}(d-sr)

The virtue of these coordinates is that walls of marginal stability become straight lines, given by the
vanishing of

(2.29)

\Im\left[Z^{LJ}_{(s,w)}(\gamma^{\prime})\overline{Z^{LJ}_{(s,w)}(\gamma)}\right]=(rd^{\prime}-r^{\prime}d)w+(r^{\prime}\textnormal{ch}_{2}-r\textnormal{ch}_{2}^{\prime})s+(d\textnormal{ch}_{2}^{\prime}-d^{\prime}\textnormal{ch}_{2})

Returning to (
2.27
), the Drézet–Le Potier bound
\Delta(E)\geq\delta_{\rm LP}(\mu(E))
in (
2.10
) (which also holds for slope-semi-stable objects)
implies that
\Re Z(\gamma)>0
(and therefore
\Re Z(-\gamma)<0
for the
homologically shifted object
E[-1]
) in the region

(2.30)

\Im T>0,\qquad w>\frac{1}{2}s^{2}-\hat{\delta}_{\rm LP}(s)

where we define
\hat{\delta}_{\rm LP}(\mu)=\delta_{\rm LP}(\mu)
when
\mu\notin\mathcal{E}
,

\hat{\delta}_{\rm LP}(\mu)=\Delta_{\mu}
otherwise.
The region
w>\frac{1}{2}s^{2}-\hat{\delta}_{\rm LP}(s)
is the region above the blue jagged curve and red points
in Figure
13
(the black and purple lines will be discussed momentarily). One can show that the remaining axioms for Bridgeland stability conditions (HN filtration and support property) are indeed satisfied by the pair
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A}(s))
in this region. Moreover, the resulting stability condition turns out to exhaust the subset
U\subset\Stab(\mathcal{C})
of geometric stability conditions, defined as those stability conditions for which all skyscraper sheaves
\mathcal{O}_{x}
with
x\in\mathds{P}^{2}
are
\sigma
-stable with the same phase. The connected component of
\Stab(\mathcal{C})
containing
U
,
denoted by
\Stab^{\dagger}(\mathcal{C})
, is the union of the images of (the closure of)
U
under the group
\Gamma_{1}(3)
of auto-equivalences
of
\mathcal{C}
generated by spherical twists, and is simply connected
[
31
]
. It is unknown whether
\Stab(\mathcal{C})
might have other connected components.

Figure 13.
Image in the
(s,w)
plane of the fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}_{C}
and some of its translates.
The dotted line correspond to the parabola
w\geq\frac{1}{2}s^{2}
. The jagged blue curve corresponds
to
w=\frac{1}{2}s^{2}-\delta_{\rm LP}(s)
, and
the red dots indicate the points
(\mu_{\alpha},\frac{1}{2}\mu_{\alpha}^{2}-\Delta_{\alpha})
corresponding to exceptional slopes. The vertical segments in red interpolate between the conifold point at
\tau=n
and the orbifold point at
\tau=\tau_{o}+n
. The segments in purple are the images of infinitesimal arcs around the orbifold point
\tau_{o}
(from
(-1,\frac{1}{6})
to
(0,-\frac{1}{3})
), around
\tau_{o^{\prime}}
(from
(0,-\frac{1}{3})
to
(1,\frac{1}{6})
), etc. These segments are connected at points with
s
integer along the parabola
w=\frac{1}{2}s^{2}-\frac{1}{3}
.
The vertical lines in black correspond to straight
\tau_{1}=n+\frac{1}{2}
lines connecting the orbifold point
\tau_{o}+n
and conifold point
n-\frac{1}{2}
in the
\tau
plane.

Having identified the space of stability conditions
\Stab^{\dagger}\mathcal{C}
inside the two-dimensional space of central charges parametrized by
(T,T_{D})
, it remains to determine which part of it is covered by the physical slice of
\Pi
-stability conditions. As mentioned in the introduction and further detailed in Appendix
A
, the slice
\Pi
is isomorphic to the universal cover of the modular curve
X_{1}(3)
, and conveniently parametrized by
\tau
in the Poincaré upper half-plane
\mathds{H}
.
As shown in

[
31
]
, there is an embedding
\mathds{H}\hookrightarrow\Stab^{\dagger}\mathcal{C}
sending

\tau\mapsto(Z_{\tau},\mathcal{A}_{\tau})
which is equivariant with respect to the action of
\Gamma_{1}(3)
on both sides. The central charge function
Z_{\tau}
(
1.4
) is determined by the
representation (
1.5
) for the periods,

(2.31)

T(\tau)=-\frac{1}{2}+\int_{\tau_{o}}^{\tau}C(u)\mathrm{d}u\ ,\quad T_{D}(\tau)=\frac{1}{3}+\int_{\tau_{o}}^{\tau}C(u)\,u\mathrm{d}u

where
\tau_{o}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}e^{5\pi\mathrm{i}/6}
is a preimage of the orbifold point, and
C(\tau)=\frac{\eta(\tau)^{9}}{\eta(3\tau)^{3}}
is a weight 3 modular form for
\Gamma_{1}(3)
.
Indeed, one checks that the period vector
\Pi=(1,T,T_{D})
transforms under
\tau\mapsto\tau+1

and
\tau\mapsto-\frac{\tau}{3\tau-1}
with the same monodromy matrices
M_{\rm LV},M_{C}
(see
(
A.54
)) as predicted by the translation
E\mapsto E(1)
and spherical twist

E\mapsto\ST_{\mathcal{O}}E
on the derived category side.
It follows from the reality properties
(
A.18
) of the periods
T
and
T_{D}
that
the action of derived duality preserves the slice of
\Pi
-stability conditions,

(2.32)

Z_{-\bar{\tau}}(\gamma^{\vee})=-\overline{Z_{\tau}(\gamma)}

where
[r,d,\textnormal{ch}_{2}]^{\vee}=[-r,d,-\textnormal{ch}_{2}]=[-r,d,3d-\chi)
. Moreover, one can show
that the periods

T(\tau),T_{D}(\tau)
satisfy the geometric stability conditions (
2.30
)
in the fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}_{C}
and its translates
[
31
, §C]
.

In the limit
\tau\to\mathrm{i}\infty
,
C(\tau)=1+\mathcal{O}(q)
with
q=e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\tau}
, the central charge becomes a quadratic polynomial in
\tau
,

(2.33)

Z_{\tau}(\gamma)=-\frac{r}{2}\left(\tau^{2}+\tfrac{1}{8}\right)+d\tau-\textnormal{ch}_{2}+\mathcal{O}\bigl(|\tau|q\bigr)

In fact, in the domain
\mathds{H}^{\rm LV}
defined below (
1.7
) as the
preimage of the region above the the curve
w=\frac{1}{2}s^{2}
inside
the fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}_{C}
and its translates
(see Figure
1
),
the
\mathcal{O}(|\tau|q)
corrections in (
2.33
) as well as the constant
\frac{1}{8}

can be absorbed by
an action of
(\begin{smallmatrix}1&(s-\Re T)/\Im T\\
0&t/\Im T\end{smallmatrix})\in GL(2,\mathds{R})^{+}
with
t=\sqrt{2w-s^{2}}

on
Z_{\tau}(\gamma)
(note that
\Im T>0
throughout
\mathds{H}^{\rm LV}
).
The new central charge is then
Z_{(s,t)}^{\rm LV}(\gamma)
in
(
1.6
).

The heart
\mathcal{A}_{\tau}
is more subtle
[
31
]
.
In the fundamendal domain
\mathcal{F}_{C}
, we define the heart as
\mathcal{A}_{\tau}=\mathcal{A}(s(\tau))
where
s(\tau)
is defined as usual by
s=\frac{\Im T_{D}}{\Im T}
and
\mathcal{A}(s)=\Coh_{c}^{\sharp s}
, and extend this definition to the full upper half-plane by

\Gamma_{1}(3)
equivariance. In particular, in the translates
\mathcal{F}_{C}(m)
under
\tau\mapsto\tau+m

(which includes the region
\mathds{H}^{\rm LV}
and the wedge of angle
2\pi/3
above the orbifold point), the definition
\mathcal{A}_{\tau}=\mathcal{A}(s(\tau))

continues to hold.
However it is no longer valid below the arcs which connect the conifold points
\tau\in\mathds{Z}

and orbifold points
\tau\in\tau_{o}+\mathds{Z}
. In particular, near the real axis
the objects in
\mathcal{A}_{\tau}
may include complexes of arbitrary length. As a result, the slice
of
\Pi
-stability conditions only covers the part of the space
U
of geometric stability conditions
which lies above the purple line in Figure
13
.
While the image of the region
\Im T>0
in the upper half plane
does extend below this line, the relevant heart no longer coincides with the one appropriate for
geometric stability conditions.

3.
Scattering diagrams and attractor flows

In this section, we recall the construction of stability scattering diagram
for quivers with potential, generalize this notion to arbitrary
triangulated categories, and explain its relation to the Split Attractor Flow
Conjecture in the
case of non-compact CY threefolds with one-dimensional Kähler moduli space.

3.1.
Scattering diagram for quivers with potentials

Let
(Q,W)
be a quiver with potential. A representation
R
of
(Q,W)
is a set of finite dimensional
vector spaces
M_{i}
for every node
i\in Q_{0}
, and linear maps
M_{a}\colon M_{i}\to M_{j}
for every
arrow
(a\colon i\rightarrow j)\in Q_{1}
such that for any arrow
a\in Q_{1}
, the element
\partial W/\partial a
in the path algebra of
Q
evaluates to zero on
R
. The set of representations
R
forms an Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
graded by the lattice
\mathds{Z}^{Q_{0}}
of dimension vectors
\gamma=\dim R=(\dim M_{i})_{i\in Q_{0}}
. For any set
(z_{i})_{i\in Q_{0}}
of points

z_{i}=-\theta_{i}+\mathrm{i}\rho_{i}
in the upper half-plane
\mathds{H}_{B}
,
the pair
\sigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})
with central charge
Z(\gamma)=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}n_{i}z_{i}

with
n_{i}=\dim M_{i}

defines a stability condition in the sense of §
2.3
.
We denote by
\mathcal{M}_{Z}(\gamma)

the moduli space of
\sigma
-semi-stable representations of dimension vector
\gamma
, by

\Omega_{Z}(\gamma)
its motivic Donaldson-Thomas invariant (
2.24
) and by

\bar{\Omega}_{Z}(\gamma)
its rational counterpart (
1.1
).
These invariants vanish unless
\gamma
belongs to the positive quadrant

\Gamma_{+}=\mathds{N}^{Q_{0}}
.

As shown in
[
29
]
, the set of DT invariants is conveniently encoded
in a stability scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}_{Q}
in the space
\mathds{R}^{Q_{0}}
spanned by King stability parameters
\theta=(\theta_{i})_{i\in Q_{0}}
. To define
\mathcal{D}
, we first introduce the hyperplane orthogonal to
\gamma

(3.1)

\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}_{Q}(\gamma)=\{\theta:(\theta,\gamma)=0\}

which we call
geometric ray
15
15

15

The rays are sometimes called walls of second kind or BPS walls.
The word ’ray’ avoids possible confusion with walls of marginal stability (or first kind), but admittedly is most adequate for two-dimensional scattering diagrams, where rays are in fact one-dimensional.
.
At any point along
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}_{Q}(\gamma)
, the
notion of
Z
-semi-stability coincides with the notion of
\theta
-semi-stability (i.e.
R
is

\theta
-semi-stable if and only if

(\theta,\gamma^{\prime})\leq 0
for any subrepresentation
R^{\prime}\subset R
of dimension vector
\gamma^{\prime}
).
In particular, the index
\Omega_{Z}(\gamma)\eqqcolon\Omega_{\theta}(\gamma)
is independent of
\rho
.
We then define the active ray
\mathcal{R}_{Q}(\gamma)
as the subset of
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}_{Q}(\gamma)

(3.2)

\mathcal{R}_{Q}(\gamma)=\{\theta:(\theta,\gamma)=0,\quad\bar{\Omega}_{\theta}(\gamma)\neq 0\}

Since
\bar{\Omega}_{\theta}(\gamma)
is invariant under
rescaling
\theta\to\lambda\theta
with
\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, and since it can only jump on a finite set of hyperplanes
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}_{Q}(\gamma^{\prime})
corresponding to the destabilization by
a subobject of dimension vector
\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma
, the set of rays

\{\mathcal{R}_{Q}(\gamma):\gamma\in\Gamma_{+}\}
decomposes into
a complex of convex rational polyhedral cones in the space
\mathds{R}^{Q_{0}}
of King stability conditions.

Furthermore, to each point
\theta\in\mathcal{R}_{Q}(\gamma)

we associate an automorphism
\mathcal{U}_{\theta}(\gamma)
of the quantum torus algebra

\hat{\mathcal{T}}
defined as follows.
Let
\mathcal{T}
be the algebra
\mathds{C}(y)[[\mathcal{X}_{\Gamma_{+}}]]
generated by
formal variables
\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma_{+}
, with coefficients
in
\mathds{C}(y)
, subject to the relations

(3.3)

\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}\,\mathcal{X}_{\gamma^{\prime}}=(-y)^{\langle\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\rangle}\mathcal{X}_{\gamma+\gamma^{\prime}}

where

(3.4)

\langle\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\rangle=\sum_{a:(i\to j)\in Q_{1}}(n^{\prime}_{i}n_{j}-n_{i}n^{\prime}_{j})

is the antisymmetrization of the
Euler form
\chi_{Q}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}n_{i}n^{\prime}_{i}-\sum_{a:(i\to j)\in Q_{1}}n_{i}n_{j}^{\prime}
.
For any positive integer
M
, we denote by
\mathcal{T}_{M}
the ideal spanned by generators

\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}
with total dimension
\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}n_{i}>M
, and define the
pro-nilpotent algebra
\hat{\mathcal{T}}
as the inverse limit of
\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{T}_{M}
as
M\to\infty
.
We denote by
\hat{\mathcal{G}}=\exp(\hat{\mathcal{T}})
the corresponding pro-unipotent group.
The automorphism
\mathcal{U}_{\theta}(\gamma)
is the element of
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
defined by

(3.5)

\mathcal{U}_{\theta}(\gamma)=\exp\biggl(\frac{\bar{\Omega}_{\theta}(\gamma)\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}}{y^{-1}-y}\biggr)

The scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}_{Q}
can be defined as the set of decorated rays

\{\mathcal{R}_{Q}(\gamma):\gamma\in\Gamma_{+}\}

equipped with the automorphism
\mathcal{U}_{\theta}(\gamma)

at each point. The wall-crossing formula for DT invariants ensures
that
\mathcal{D}_{Q}
is consistent in the following sense
[
29
]
: for any
generic closed path
\mathcal{P}\colon t\in[1,0]\to\mathds{R}^{Q_{0}}
(where generic means that
the intersection of
\mathcal{P}
with a ray
\mathcal{R}(\gamma_{i})
at
t=t_{i}
is transverse
and does not meet any cone of codimension larger than one),
the ordered product of automorphisms associated to each intersection is trivial,

(3.6)

\prod_{i}\mathcal{U}_{\theta(t_{i})}(\gamma_{i})^{\epsilon_{i}}=1\ ,\quad\epsilon_{i}=\sign\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{\mathrm{d}t},\gamma_{i}\right)

This consistency property ensures that
all rays can be deduced from the knowledge of the initial
rays, defined as those rays
\mathcal{R}(\gamma)
which contain the
self-stability condition
\theta_{\star}(\gamma):=\langle-,\gamma\rangle

(such that
(\theta_{\star}(\gamma),\gamma^{\prime})=\langle\gamma^{\prime},\gamma\rangle
for
all
\gamma^{\prime}
). The attractor
tree formula of
[
21
]
and the flow tree formula of
[
22
]

provide an algorithm to compute
\Omega_{\theta}(\gamma)
on any ray in terms
of the attractor invariants
\Omega_{\star}(\gamma)
for the initial rays.

For a general quiver with potential
(Q,W)
, the determination of the initial rays is a difficult problem.
For an acyclic quiver however, it is easy to prove that the only initial rays are those associated to the
simple representations at each node
[
29
, Theorem 1.5]
, with

(3.7)

\Omega_{\star}(\gamma_{i})=1,\quad\Omega_{\star}(k\gamma_{i})=0\ \mbox{for}\ k>1

More generally, one can show that
\Omega_{\star}(\gamma)=0
unless the support of
\gamma
(defined as the set of vertices
i\in Q_{0}
such that
n_{i}\neq 0
) is strongly connected

[
20
, Theorem 3.8]
. For quivers associated to non-compact Calabi-Yau threefolds, it is conjectured in
[
23
,
20
,
24
]
that

\Omega_{\star}(\gamma)=0
unless
\gamma
belongs to the kernel of the antisymmetrized Euler form.

3.2.
Scattering diagrams for Kronecker quivers

As an example which will play a central role later in this paper, let us consider the Kronecker quiver with 2 nodes and
\kappa
arrows
a_{i}\colon 1\to 2
. Since the quiver is acyclic,
the only initial rays are those associated to
\gamma_{1}=(1,0)
and
\gamma_{2}=(0,1)
,
with
\Omega_{\theta}(\gamma_{1})
and
\Omega_{\theta}(\gamma_{2})=1
along the axes

\theta_{1}=0
and
\theta_{2}=0
, respectively. For
other dimension vector
(n_{1},n_{2})
, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\gamma)

has virtual dimension

(3.8)

\virdim_{\mathds{C}}\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\gamma)=\kappa n_{1}n_{2}-n_{1}^{2}-n_{2}^{2}+1

It is empty unless
\theta_{1}>0,\theta_{2}<0
and (
3.8
) is non-negative, in which case it
coincides with the actual dimension.
Note that the formula (
3.8
) is symmetric under the exchange

(n_{1},n_{2})\mapsto(n_{2},n_{1})
, and under

(n_{1},n_{2})\mapsto(n_{1},\kappa n_{2}-n_{1})
, which corresponds to a mutation of the quiver.

For the
A_{2}
quiver (
\kappa=1
, left panel on Figure
14
), the only active ray in the quadrant
\theta_{1}>0,\theta_{2}<0

is associated to
\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}
, with
\Omega_{\theta}(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2})=1

along the diagonal
\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}=0
, and the consistency condition (
3.6
)
reproduces the usual five-term relation

(3.9)

\mathcal{U}_{\gamma_{1}}\mathcal{U}_{\gamma_{2}}=\mathcal{U}_{\gamma_{2}}\mathcal{U}_{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}}\mathcal{U}_{\gamma_{1}}

For the affine
A_{1}
quiver (
\kappa=2
, middel panel on Figure
14
), there is an infinity of active rays of the form
(k,k-1),(k-1,k)
and
(k,k)
with

k\geq 1
, with DT invariant
1,1
and
-y-1/y
, respectively.
For the Kronecker quiver with
\kappa\geq 3
, arrows there is a discrete set of active
rays
(n_{1},n_{2})
with unit DT invariant, obtained
by successive mutations of
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
,
and a dense set of rays in the region
\kappa-\sqrt{\kappa^{2}-4}<\frac{2n_{1}}{n_{2}},\frac{2n_{2}}{n_{1}}<\kappa+\sqrt{\kappa^{2}-4}
. For
\kappa=3
(right panel on Figure
14
), the discrete rays correspond to
(n_{1},n_{2})=(F_{2k},F_{2k\pm 2})
where
F_{2k}
are the even Fibonacci numbers
1,3,8,21,\dots
.
In Table
2
, we tabulate some of the DT indices
K_{\kappa}(n_{1},n_{2})\coloneqq\Omega_{\theta}(n_{1},n_{2})

for low values of
(\kappa,n_{1},n_{2})
,
restricting to the case
\kappa=0\mod 3
which is most relevant for the present work.
It is worth noting that for
(n_{1},n_{2})=(1,1)
, the moduli space of stable representations
is
\mathds{P}^{\kappa-1}
, hence

(3.10)

K_{\kappa}(1,1)=(-1)^{\kappa+1}\frac{(y^{\kappa}-y^{-\kappa})}{y-1/y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle y\to 1}}{{\rightarrow}}(-1)^{\kappa+1}\kappa

More generally, for
(n_{1},n_{2})=(1,n)
or
(n,1)
, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\gamma)

is the Grassmannian
of
n
-dimensional planes in
\mathds{C}^{\kappa}
, hence
K_{\kappa}(1,n)=0
if
n>\kappa
.
From the point of view of the flow tree or attractor tree formulae, this vanishing occurs
as a result of cancellations between numerous different trees.
