4.5.
Generalization to
\psi\neq 0

We now discuss how this picture generalizes to an arbitrary phase
\psi\in(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2})
.
For
r\neq 0
, the geometric ray
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}_{\psi}(\gamma)

is now a branch of hyperbola (degenerating to a straight line for vanishing discriminant
\Delta=0
)

(4.24)

s=\tfrac{d}{r}-t\tan\psi-\frac{\sign r}{\cos\psi}\sqrt{t^{2}+2\Delta\cos^{2}\psi}

with
t>0
,
while for
r=0
, it is the line
s=\frac{\textnormal{ch}_{2}}{d}-t\tan\psi
when
d>0
(or the empty set
when
d\leq 0
). In particular, the electromagnetic analogy of §
4.1
breaks down for
\psi\neq 0
,
since one of the two rays emanating from
s=m
is no longer inside the light-cone (indeed, the angle
between the two rays remains equal to
\frac{\pi}{2}
independently of
\psi
).

Moreover, under the same conditions (
4.6
)
two rays
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}_{\psi}(\gamma)
and
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}_{\psi}(\gamma^{\prime})
still
intersect on the same wall
\mathcal{W}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
, but at the point
(s_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}-R_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}\sin\psi,R_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}\cos\psi)
at an angle
\psi
from the top of the half-circle
\mathcal{C}(s_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}},R_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}})
. In fact, observing that

(4.25)

\Re\left(e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}Z^{\rm LV}_{(s,t)}(\gamma)\right)=\cos\psi\,\Re\left(Z^{\rm LV}_{(s_{\psi},t_{\psi})}(\gamma)\right)

where
(s_{\psi},t_{\psi})
are related to
(s,t)
through

(4.26)

s_{\psi}=s+t\tan\psi,\quad t_{\psi}=\frac{t}{\cos\psi}

we see that the loci
\Re\left(e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}Z(\gamma)\right)=0
in the
(s,t)

upper half-plane are mapped to the loci
\Re(Z(\gamma))=0
in the
(s_{\psi},t_{\psi})
upper
half-plane. The relation for the imaginary parts is however more complicated,

(4.27)

\Im\bigl(e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}Z^{\rm LV}_{(s,t)}(\gamma)\bigr)=\Im\bigl(Z^{\rm LV}_{(s_{\psi},t_{\psi})}(\gamma)\bigr)-\sin\psi\,\Re\bigl(Z^{\rm LV}_{(s_{\psi},t_{\psi})}(\gamma)\bigr)+r(\sin\psi)t_{\psi}^{2}

In particular, the transformation
e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}Z^{\rm LV}_{(s,t)}(\gamma)\rightarrow Z^{\rm LV}_{(s_{\psi},t_{\psi})}(\gamma)
is
not
a
\widetilde{GL^{+}}(2,\mathds{R})
transformation, due to
the last term in (
4.27
). Rather, one should view
(s_{\psi},t_{\psi})

as variants of the affine coordinates
(x,y)
defined in (
1.10
),
indeed
(x,y)=(s_{\psi},\frac{1}{2}(t_{\psi}^{2}-s_{\psi}^{2}))
.

Thus, we conclude that whenever
\psi\in(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2})
,
the scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}_{\psi}
in
the
(s,t)
upper half-plane coincides with the scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}_{0}
in
the
(s_{\psi},t_{\psi})
upper half-plane,
and both have the same image in the
(x,y)
plane.
Thus, the SAFC holds on the large volume slice, as long as
\cos\psi\neq 0
.
For
\psi=\pm\frac{\pi}{2}
,
the scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}_{\psi}

becomes degenerate, as all loci
\Im Z(\gamma)=0
become
either vertical lines
s=d/r
for
r\neq 0
or the horizontal line
t=0
on the boundary.
As we shall see in §
6
, the scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\Pi}_{\psi}
along the slice of

\Pi
-stability conditions is better behaved in this limit.

5.
The orbifold scattering diagram

In this section, we construct the scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}_{Q}
for the
orbifold quiver shown in Figure
4
, following the general construction
of §
3.1
. In §
5.2
, we identify the initial rays,
giving a rigorous proof of the Attractor Conjecture for this case (Theorem
1
in the introduction). In §
5.3
,
we restrict
\mathcal{D}_{Q}
to a two-dimensional slice
\mathcal{D}_{o}
, which we shall identify later in §
6.3

with a subset of the exact scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\Pi}_{\psi}
. In §
5.4

we illustrate the use of the diagram
\mathcal{D}_{o}
for computing the index for small
dimension vectors.

5.1.
Quiver descriptions

As explained for example in
[
20
, §4.2]
, any tilting sequence
\mathcal{S}=(E_{1},E_{2},E_{3})
in the derived category
\mathcal{C}=D^{b}\Coh(K_{\mathds{P}^{2}})
(in the sense that
\mathcal{S}
generates
\mathcal{C}
and
\Ext^{k}(E_{i},E_{j})=0
for
k\neq 0
and all
i,j
) provides an isomorphism
\mathcal{C}\sim D^{b}(\Rep J(Q,W))

with the derived category of representations of the Jacobian algebra
J(Q,W)
of a certain
quiver with potential
(Q,W)
associated to
\mathcal{S}
. A tilting sequence can be obtained
from any strong exceptional collection
(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})
on
\mathds{P}^{2}
by setting

\mathcal{S}=(i_{*}(F_{1}[1]),i_{*}(F_{2}),i_{*}(F_{3})[-1])
.
Starting from the strong exceptional collection
(\mathcal{O}(-1),\Omega(1),\mathcal{O})
, one
arrives at the tilting sequence in (
1.8
), associated to the
quiver with potential depicted in Figure
4
. All other strong exceptional collections are obtained by successive mutations of
(\mathcal{O}(-1),\Omega(1),\Omega)
,
and similarly lead to 3-node quivers
(Q_{a,b,c},W_{a,b,c})
with
a
arrows
from node 1
to node 2,
b
arrows from 2 to 3 and
c
arrows from 3 to 1, with

(a,b,c)
any set of positive integers satisfying the Markov condition
a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2}=abc
.

While the isomorphism
\mathcal{C}\simeq D^{b}(\Rep J(Q,W))
holds for any choice of tilting sequence
\mathcal{S}=(E_{1},E_{2},E_{3})
, the heart
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}
only coincides (up to the action of
\widetilde{GL^{+}}(2,\mathds{R})
) with the Abelian category
\Rep J(Q,W)
in a region
\mathds{H}_{\mathcal{S}}
of
\Stab\mathcal{C}
where the
objects
E_{i}
are stable and the phases of their central charges
Z(E_{i})
lie in a common
half-space (see for example Lemma 3.16 in
[
61
]
). In that case,
the moduli space of
\sigma
-semistable objects
E
of Chern vector
\gamma
in

\mathcal{C}
coincides with the moduli space of semi-stable representations of
J(Q,W)
, with
dimension vector
(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
and King parameters
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3})

determined by

(5.1)

\gamma=\sum_{i=1}^{3}n_{i}\operatorname{ch}E_{i}\ ,\quad\theta_{i}=\lambda\,\Re[e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}Z(E_{i})]

with
\psi=\arg Z(E)-\frac{\pi}{2}
such that
n_{1}\theta_{1}+n_{2}\theta_{2}+n_{3}\theta_{3}=0
, and
\lambda
is an arbitrary non-negative factor. At the boundary of the region
\mathds{H}_{\mathcal{S}}
, one of the objects
E_{i}
exits from the common half-space and one should apply a left or right mutation so as to obtain a new quiver description.

Let us consider the exceptional collection in (
1.8
), corresponding to the fractional
branes on the orbifold
\mathds{C}^{3}/\mathds{Z}_{3}
:

(5.2)

E_{1}=i_{*}(\mathcal{O})[-1],\quad E_{2}=i_{*}(\Omega(1)),\quad E_{3}=i_{*}(\mathcal{O}(-1))[1]

with charges
\gamma_{i}=\operatorname{ch}E_{i}
given by

(5.3)

\gamma_{1}=[-1,0,0],\quad\gamma_{2}=[2,-1,-\frac{1}{2}],\quad\gamma_{3}=[-1,1,-\frac{1}{2}]

The corresponding quiver, shown in Figure
4
, has
3
arrows
E_{i}\to E_{i-1}

for each
i
modulo 3, consistently with
\langle\gamma_{i},\gamma_{i-1}\rangle=-3
.
Using the Eichler integral representation (
1.5
), it is easy to check that the central
charges of the simple objects coincide at the orbifold point
\tau_{o}
, namely
Z_{\tau_{o}}(\gamma_{i})=1/3
. Thus, this quiver description will be appropriate
22
22

22

Note that
the objects
E_{i}
are not stable at
\tau=\tau_{o}
strictly, since their central charge is not in
the half-plane
\mathds{H}_{B}
; the objects
E_{i}[1]
are stable, and lead to the same quiver
but opposite dimension vector.

in a region
\mathds{H}^{o}
around
\tau_{o}

in the physical slice
\Pi
, which we identify in §
6.3
.
The dimension vector associated to an object of charge
\gamma=[r,d,\textnormal{ch}_{2}]
is given by

(5.4)

(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})=\Bigl(-\frac{3}{2}d-\textnormal{ch}_{2}-r,-\frac{1}{2}d-\textnormal{ch}_{2},\frac{1}{2}d-\textnormal{ch}_{2}\Bigr)=(-\chi,r+d-\chi,r+2d-\chi)

or conversely

(5.5)

r=2n_{2}-n_{1}-n_{3},\quad d=n_{3}-n_{2},\quad\textnormal{ch}_{2}=-\frac{1}{2}(n_{2}+n_{3})

Abusing notation once again, we shall write
\gamma=(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
, using round brackets on both sides to distinguish it from the other notations
\gamma=[r,d,\textnormal{ch}_{2}]=[r,d,\chi)
.

A similar quiver description holds true around any image of
\tau_{o}
under an element
g\in\Gamma_{1}(3)
, with the tilting sequence being replaced by its image
(g(E_{1}),g(E_{2}),g(E_{3}))
. In particular, around
\tau=\tau_{o}+k
, the tilting sequence (
1.8
) is shifted to

(5.6)

E_{1}(k)=i_{*}(\mathcal{O}(k))[-1],\quad E_{2}(k)=i_{*}(\Omega(k+1)),\quad E_{3}(k)=i_{*}(\mathcal{O}(k-1))[1]

with Chern vectors
\gamma_{1}(k),\gamma_{2}(k),\gamma_{3}(k)
.
The relevant dimension vector is
then

(5.7)

\begin{cases}n_{1}(k)=n_{1}-\frac{1}{2}k(3n_{1}-4n_{2}+n_{3})+\frac{1}{2}k^{2}(n_{1}-2n_{2}+n_{3})\\
n_{2}(k)=n_{2}-\frac{1}{2}k(n_{1}-n_{3})+\frac{1}{2}k^{2}(n_{1}-2n_{2}+n_{3})\\
n_{3}(k)=n_{3}-\frac{1}{2}k(4n_{2}-n_{1}-3n_{3})+\frac{1}{2}k^{2}(n_{1}-2n_{2}+n_{3})\end{cases}

where
(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
denotes the value for
k=0
, given in (
5.4
). We note that
for fixed
\gamma=[r,d,\textnormal{ch}_{2}]
and large
k
, the entries
n_{i}(k)
grow like
-\frac{1}{2}k^{2}r
for

r\neq 0
, or like
kd
for
r=0
, in particular they all have the same sign for large
k
.

5.2.
Initial rays for the orbifold scattering diagram

In this subsection, we prove Theorem
1
, which states that
for the quiver with potential
(Q,W)

shown in Figure
4
,
the attractor invariant
\Omega_{\star}(\gamma)
vanishes for all dimension vectors
\gamma=(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
except for

(5.8)

\gamma\in\bigl\{(1,0,0),(0,1,0),(0,0,1),(n,n,n):n\geq 1\bigr\}

where it takes the values stated in (
1.9
).
This result was conjectured in
[
23
]
, but the proof outlined in that paper
was not mathematically rigorous. Here we complete the proof, combining ideas from
[
23
, §3.3]
and
[
25
, §3.2]
. We denote by
a_{i}\colon 2\to 1
,
b_{i}\colon 3\to 2
and
c_{i}\colon 1\to 3
the arrows of the quiver.

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Consider a representation
\phi
of
(Q,W)
with dimension vector
(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
,
assumed to be stable for a King stability condition
\theta=(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3})
.
Consider a cycle
w
of the quiver: because
(Q,W)
gives a noncommutative crepant resolution of
\mathds{C}^{3}/\mathds{Z}_{3}

[
62
, Proposition 3.13]
, the cycle
w
is a central element of the Jacobian algebra of
(Q,W)
, hence it defines an endomorphism of
\phi
. Because
\phi
is stable, its only automorphisms are rescalings, hence
w
acts as a scalar on
\phi
. Suppose now that
\phi
is not of dimension vector
(n,n,n)
for
n\in\mathds{N}^{\ast}
:
w
cannot be an automorphism (otherwise all its arrows would be isomorphisms, hence one would have
n_{1}=n_{2}=n_{3}
), hence
w
vanishes on
\phi
.

We suppose now that the King stability parameters satisfy
\theta_{1}<0
, and
\theta_{3}>0

(since
\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}+\theta_{3}=0
, other cases follow by circular permutation of the nodes). We now show by contradiction that
\phi_{c_{j}}
vanishes for every arrow
c_{j}:1\to 3
. Suppose that there exists
x\in V_{1}
and
j\in\{1,2,3\}
such that
\phi_{c_{j}}(x)\in V_{3}
does not vanish. Because
\theta_{1}<0
and
\phi
is stable, one must have
x\in\bigoplus_{k=1}^{3}{\rm Im}(\phi_{a_{k}})
. Because
\theta_{3}>0
and
\phi
is stable,
\langle\phi_{c_{j}}(x)\rangle
cannot be a sub-representation of
\phi
, hence there must be an arrow
b_{i}\colon 3\to 2
such that
\phi_{b_{i}}\phi_{c_{j}}(x)\in V_{2}
does not vanish. Because all the cycles of
Q
vanish on
\phi
, one must have
\phi_{b_{i}}\phi_{c_{j}}(\bigoplus_{k=1}^{3}{\rm Im}(\phi_{a_{k}}))=0
, a contradiction. Hence the
\phi_{c_{j}}
vanish on
\phi
for all
j
. The set
I=\{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\}
of vanishing arrows provides a cut of the potential
W
, in the sense that each cycle of
W
contains exactly one arrow of
I
.

The stable representation
\phi
is then a representation of the quiver with relation
(Q_{I},\partial_{I}W)
, where the arrows
c_{j}\in I
have been traded for relations
\partial_{c_{j}}W=0
for
c_{j}\in I
. From general arguments of geometric invariant theory, the moduli
\mathcal{M}^{\theta,s}_{Q_{I}}
of stable representations of
Q_{I}
is smooth of dimension
3n_{3}n_{2}+3n_{2}n_{1}-n_{1}^{2}-n_{2}^{2}-n_{3}^{2}+1
. The moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{\theta,s}_{Q_{I},\partial_{I}W}
of stable representations of
(Q_{I},\partial_{I}W)
is cut out by
3n_{3}n_{1}
bilinear relations
\partial_{c_{j}}W=0

inside
\mathcal{M}^{\theta,s}_{Q_{I}}
. We denote the infinitesimal versions of these relations on the tangent space of
\phi
by
\delta(\partial_{c_{j}}W)\colon V_{3}\to V_{1}
. A linear dependence between these infinitesimal relations would be given by maps
\tilde{\phi}_{c_{j}}\colon V_{1}\to V_{3}
such that:

(5.9)

\displaystyle\mbox{Tr}(\tilde{\phi}_{c_{j}}\delta(\partial_{c_{j}}W))=0

As shown in
[
25
, §3.2]
, this equation is equivalent to the fact that the representation
\bar{\phi}\coloneqq(\phi_{a},\phi_{b},\tilde{\phi}_{c})
of
Q
(which, like
\phi
, satisfies the relations
\delta_{c_{j}}W=0
) satisfies the relations
\delta_{a_{i}}W=0
and
\delta_{b_{i}}W=0
.
Hence
\tilde{\phi}
is a representation of
(Q,W)
, which is
\theta
-stable (because it has fewer subobjects than
the
\theta
-stable representation
\phi
) of dimension
\gamma\neq(n,n,n)
. By the above arguments
\tilde{\phi}_{c_{j}}=0
, therefore the relations
\partial_{c_{j}}W=0
are transverse, and
\mathcal{M}^{\theta,s}_{Q_{I},\partial_{I}W}
is smooth of dimension
3n_{3}n_{2}+3n_{2}n_{1}-3n_{3}n_{1}-n_{1}^{2}-n_{2}^{2}-n_{3}^{2}+1
. In particular, because this dimension is positive, one has

(5.10)

\displaystyle n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{2}-3n_{3}n_{2}-3n_{2}n_{1}-3n_{1}n_{3}+6n_{3}n_{1}\leq 1

Now the proof proceeds exactly as in
[
23
, §3.3]
. Assume that
\theta
is an attractor stability condition, hence a small deformation of the self-stability condition
\langle-,\gamma\rangle

for some dimension vector
\gamma\neq(n,n,n)
.
Then
\theta_{1}<0
implies that
n_{1}\geq n_{2}
, and
\theta_{3}>0
implies that
n_{3}\geq n_{2}
: hence
6n_{3}n_{1}\geq 2n_{3}n_{2}+2n_{2}n_{1}+2n_{1}n_{3}
, and then, using (
5.10
):

(5.11)

\displaystyle q(\gamma)\coloneqq{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}((n_{1}-n_{2})^{2}+(n_{2}-n_{3})^{2}+(n_{3}-n_{1})^{2})=n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{2}-n_{1}n_{2}-n_{2}n_{3}-n_{3}n_{1}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{2}-3n_{1}n_{2}-3n_{2}n_{3}-3n_{2}n_{3}+6n_{3}n_{1}\leq 1

The kernel of the quadratic form
q(\gamma)
is given by dimensions vectors
(n,n,n)
, but we have assumed that
\gamma
was not of this form. One has
q(\gamma)=1
only for
\gamma=(n+1,n,n)
and circular permutations thereof, but the inequality is strict in the second line of (
5.11
) unless
n=0
. Hence,
\gamma
must be
(1,0,0)
,
(0,1,0)
, or
(0,0,1)
, with attractor invariants equal to
1
. The DT invariants for
\gamma=(n,n,n)
are independent of the stability conditions, and given by
the shifted Poincaré polynomial

(-y)^{-3}P(K_{\mathds{P}^{2}},y)=-y^{3}-y-1/y
by
[
20
, Remark 5.2]
.
∎

Figure 24.
Quiver scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}_{Q}
, showing only the initial rays

\mathcal{R}_{o}(\gamma_{i})
and
\mathcal{R}_{o}(\delta)
and secondary rays

\mathcal{R}_{o}(\gamma_{j}+\gamma_{k})
.

Having determined the initial rays for the orbifold quiver, it is now straightforward at least in principle to construct the stability scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}_{Q}
. The result
is represented in Figure
24
, including only initial and some secondary rays. The full
diagram is dense except in a cone which includes the positive and negative octant, as will become clear shortly.

Figure 25.
Two-dimensional section
\mathcal{D}_{o}
of the orbifold scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}_{Q}
along the hyperplane

\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}+\theta_{3}=1
. We show only the restriction to the triangle
\Delta_{\psi}
(see §
6.4.1
)
bounded by the points
p_{i}(\mathcal{V}_{\psi})
defined in (
6.28
)
(with
\psi=-1.4
for illustration), but the scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}_{o}
in the space of King stability conditions is unbounded.
Regions with a dense set of rays are shown in gray. The rays in red correspond to the discrete set of real roots of the Kronecker quiver with 3 arrows, with dimension vector
(F_{2k},F_{2k+2},0)
or permutations thereof, see §
3.2
.

5.3.
Restricted scattering diagram

Due to the rescaling symmetry in the space of King stability parameters, there is no significant loss
of information
23
23

23

The price to pay is that the ray
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}_{o}(\delta)
is no longer visible, but
this causes no problem since the automorphism
\mathcal{U}^{\rm eff}(\delta)
is central; moreover, the
self-stability condition
\langle-,\gamma\rangle
is now pushed to infinity in the direction
opposite to the vector
\nu(\gamma)
introduced below.

in restricting the scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}_{Q}
to its intersection
\mathcal{D}_{o}
with
the hyperplane
\mathcal{H}=\{\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}+\theta_{3}=1\}
.
It is furthermore convenient to parametrize this hyperplane by
(u,v)\in\mathds{R}^{2}
such that

(5.12)

\theta_{1}=-u+v\sqrt{3}+\tfrac{1}{3},\quad\theta_{2}=2u+\tfrac{1}{3},\quad\theta_{3}=-u-v\sqrt{3}+\tfrac{1}{3}

In these coordinates, the
\mathds{Z}_{3}
symmetry permuting the nodes of the quiver cyclically acts by a rotation of angle
2\pi/3
around the origin
(see Figure
5
). The geometric rays

\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}_{o}(\gamma)=\{(u,v)\in\mathds{R}^{2}:\sum_{i}n_{i}\theta_{i}(u,v)=0\}

are given by straight lines

(5.13)

(n_{1}+n_{3}-2n_{2})u+(n_{3}-n_{1})v\sqrt{3}-\frac{1}{3}(n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3})=0

oriented along the vector
\nu(\gamma)=(\sqrt{3}(n_{3}-n_{1}),2n_{2}-n_{1}-n_{3})

pointing from the attractor stability condition
\theta_{i}=\lambda(n_{i-1}-n_{i+1})
towards
the anti-attractor stability condition
\theta_{i}=\lambda(n_{i+1}-n_{i-1})
, with indices
i=1,2,3

taken modulo 3. In particular,
the initial rays intersect at
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}_{o}(\gamma_{i})\cap\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}_{o}(\gamma_{i+1})=\{p_{i}\}
with

(5.14)

p_{1}=\left(-\frac{1}{6},-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{3}}\right),\quad p_{2}=\left(-\frac{1}{6},\frac{1}{2\sqrt{3}}\right),\quad p_{3}=\left(\frac{1}{3},0\right)

Interpreting the ray as the worldline of a fiducial particle
traveling at point
r=(u,v)
with velocity
\nu(\gamma)
, the condition
(
5.13
) implies that the particle has
angular momentum
r\wedge\nu(\gamma)=-\frac{1}{3}(n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3})
with respect
to the origin
(0,0)
in the
(u,v)
plane, therefore rotates clockwise around the origin
(assuming that
n_{i}\geq 0
). In fact, (
5.13
) implies that the particle has
angular momentum

(5.15)

(r-p_{i})\wedge\nu(\gamma)=-n_{i-1}

with respect to any of the points
p_{i}
,
so the particle rotates clockwise around each
p_{i}
, and can only pass through it
if
n_{i-1}=0
(this generalizes the previous statement since
p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}=0
).

Another consequence of (
5.13
) is that
the linear function on
\Gamma

(5.16)

\varphi_{o}(\gamma)=(n_{3}-n_{1})u\sqrt{3}+(2n_{2}-n_{1}-n_{3})v=(r,\nu(\gamma))

increases monotonically along the ray
and is additive at each vertex, similar to the electric potential (
4.10
)
in the large volume scattering diagram. Unlike the latter however,
the function (
5.16
) is not positive,
in particular the first scattering between
\mathcal{R}_{o}(\gamma_{i})
and

\mathcal{R}_{o}(\gamma_{i+1})
may take place at arbitrary negative values of
\varphi_{o}(\gamma_{i}),\varphi_{o}(\gamma_{i+1})
. Fortunately, we do not need to rely on such a
cost function to define the quiver scattering diagram and enumerate all possible
scattering sequences, instead we can use the fact that only positive dimension vectors support
non-zero DT invariants.
In the next subsection, we use the orbifold scattering diagram to compute DT invariants for some simple dimension vectors.

5.4.
Examples

We first consider the case
\gamma=[1,0,1-n)
, corresponding to the Hilbert scheme of
n
points on
\mathds{P}^{2}
. The corresponding dimension vector is
\gamma=(n-1,n,n)
. As in §
4.3

we find that the index in the anti-attractor chamber, which we denote by

\Omega_{c}(\gamma)\coloneqq\Omega_{\langle\gamma,-\rangle}(\gamma)
,
agrees with the Gieseker index (
2.13
)
for all cases considered, although the set of scattering sequences for the large volume and orbifold
scattering diagrams may differ. As explained in §
6.4.4
, the absence of walls between
the anti-attractor chamber and the large volume chamber is a general property of objects
with slope
\mu\in[-1,0]
.

Figure 26.
Scattering sequences for dimension vector
(n-1,n,n)
corresponding the Hilbert scheme of
n
points on
\mathds{P}^{2}
, with
n=1,2,3,4

•

For
n=1
we find a single scattering sequence

\{\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\}
contributing
K_{3}(1,1)=y^{2}+1+1/y^{2}\to 3
in the anti-attractor chamber.

•

For
n=2
, we find a single scattering sequence

\{\{\gamma_{1},2\gamma_{2}\},2\gamma_{3}\}\}
contributing
K_{3}(1,2)^{2}=(y^{2}+1+1/y^{2})^{2}\to 9
.

•

For
n=3
we find two scattering sequences,

(5.17)

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hskip 5mm}l}\left\{\left\{2\gamma_{1},3\gamma_{2}\right\},3\gamma_{3}\right\}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&K_{3}(1,3)K_{3}(2,3)\to 13\\
\left\{\left\{\left\{2\gamma_{1},\gamma_{3}\right\},3\gamma_{2}\right\},2\gamma_{3}\right\}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&K_{3}(1,2){}^{2}K_{3}(1,3)\to 9\end{array}

for a total index of
y^{6}+2y^{4}+5y^{2}+6+\dots\to 22
.

•

For
n=4
we find 3 scattering sequences,

(5.18)

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hskip 5mm}l}\left\{\left\{\left\{3\gamma_{1},\gamma_{3}\right\},4\gamma_{2}\right\},3\gamma_{3}\right\}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&K_{3}(1,3){}^{2}K_{6}(1,4)\to 15\\
\left\{\left\{\left\{3\gamma_{1},\left\{\gamma_{2},2\gamma_{3}\right\}\right\},3\gamma_{2}\right\},2\gamma_{3}\right\}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&K_{3}(1,2){}^{2}K_{3}(1,3){}^{2}\to 9\\
\{\{\{\{2\gamma_{1},\gamma_{3}\},3\gamma_{2}\},\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\}\},3\gamma_{3}\}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&K_{3}(1,1){}^{2}K_{3}(1,2)K_{3}(1,3){}^{2}\to 27\end{array}

for a total index of
y^{8}+2y^{6}+6y^{4}+10y^{2}+13+\dots\to 51
.

Next we consider
\gamma=[0,1,1-n)
, corresponding to a D2-brane bound to
n
anti-D0-branes.
The corresponding dimension vector is
\gamma=(n,n+1,n+2)
. Unlike the Gieseker index at large volume, the index
\Omega_{c}(\gamma)
in the anti-attractor chamber depends on
n
, though it is
still invariant under
n\mapsto-n-2
. After a circular permutation, the dimension vector becomes

\gamma^{\prime}=(n+1,n+2,n)=[3,-2,-1-n)
, and
\Omega_{c}(\gamma^{\prime})
agrees
with the Gieseker index for rank 3 sheaves with
d=-2
and
\chi=-1-n
.

•

For
n=-1
, the dimension vector
(-1,0,1)
has mixed signs so the index vanishes.

•

For
n=0
, we find a single scattering sequence

\{\gamma_{2},2\gamma_{3}\}
contributing
K_{3}(1,2)=y^{2}+1+1/y^{2}\to 3
.

•

For
n=1
, we find
2
scattering sequences,

(5.19)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\gamma_{1},\{2\gamma_{2},3\gamma_{3}\}\}&K_{3}(2,3)K_{3}(1,1)\to 39\\
\{\{\gamma_{1},2\gamma_{2}\},3\gamma_{3}\}&K_{3}(1,2)K_{3}(1,3)\to 3\end{array}

giving a total index of
y^{8}+2y^{6}+5y^{4}+8y^{2}+10+\dots\to 42
.

•

For
n=2
, we find
3
scattering sequences,

(5.20)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\{2\gamma_{1},\{3\gamma_{2},4\gamma_{3}\}\}&K_{3}(1,2)K_{3}(3,4)\to 204\\
\{\{\{2\gamma_{1},\gamma_{3}\},3\gamma_{2}\},3\gamma_{3}\}&K_{3}(1,2)K_{3}(1,3)^{2}\to 3\\
\{\{\{\gamma_{1},3\gamma_{2}\},4\gamma_{3}\},\gamma_{1}\}&K_{3}(1,1)K_{3}(1,3)K_{6}(1,4)\to 45\\
\{\{\{\gamma_{1},2\gamma_{2}\},3\gamma_{3}\},\{\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\}\},\gamma_{1}\}&K_{3}(1,1)^{3}K_{3}(1,2)K_{3}(1,3)\to 81\end{array}

giving a total index of
333
.

•

For
n=3
, we find
7
scattering sequences,

\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\{3\gamma_{1},\left\{4\gamma_{2},5\gamma_{3}\right\}\right\}&K_{3}(1,3)K_{3}(4,5)\to 399\\
\left\{2\gamma_{1},\left\{\left\{\left\{\gamma_{1},3\gamma_{2}\right\},5\gamma_{3}\right\},\gamma_{2}\right\}\right\}&K_{3}(1,2)K_{3}(1,3)K_{6}(1,5)K_{12}(1,1)\to 216\\
\left\{2\gamma_{1},\{\{\left\{\gamma_{1},3\gamma_{2}\right\},4\gamma_{3}\},\{\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\}\}\right\}&K_{3}(1,1){}^{2}K_{3}(1,2)K_{3}(1,3)K_{6}(1,4)\to 405\\
\left\{2\gamma_{1},\{\{\left\{\gamma_{1},2\gamma_{2}\right\},3\gamma_{3}\},\{2\gamma_{2},2\gamma_{3}\}\}\right\}&K_{3}(2,2,1)K_{3}(1,2){}^{2}K_{3}(1,3)\to 648\\
\left\{\left\{\left\{3\gamma_{1},\left\{\gamma_{2},2\gamma_{3}\right\}\right\},3\gamma_{2}\right\},3\gamma_{3}\right\}&K_{3}(1,2)K_{3}(1,3){}^{3}\to 3\\
\left\{\left\{\left\{2\gamma_{1},4\gamma_{2}\right\},5\gamma_{3}\right\},\gamma_{1}\right\}&K_{6,6,3}(2,2,5)K_{3}(1,1)\to 216\\
\left\{\{\{\left\{\left\{2\gamma_{1},\gamma_{3}\right\},3\gamma_{2}\right\},3\gamma_{3}\},\{\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\}\},\gamma_{1}\right\}&K_{3}(1,1){}^{3}K_{3}(1,2)K_{3}(1,3){}^{2}\to 81\end{array}

giving a total index of
1968
. Similar to the discussion below (
4.23
), the factor
K_{6,6,3}(2,2,5)

for the penultimate sequence is computed by applying the flow tree formula
to a local scattering diagram with two incoming rays of charge
\alpha=\gamma_{1}+2\gamma_{2}

and
\beta=\gamma_{3}
with
\bar{\Omega}^{-}(\alpha)=K_{3}(1,2)=y^{2}+1+1/y^{2},\bar{\Omega}^{-}(2\alpha)=K_{3}(2,4)=-y^{5}-y^{3}-y-1/y-1/y^{3}-1/y^{5}
and
\bar{\Omega}^{-}(k\beta)=\delta_{k,1}
,
selecting the outgoing ray of charge
2\alpha+5\beta
.

6.
The exact scattering diagram

In this section, we determine the scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}_{\psi}^{\Pi}
along the slice of

\Pi
-stability conditions, by combining results on the large volume
and orbifold scattering diagrams constructed in §
4
and §
5
with invariance under
\Gamma_{1}(3)
. We start by analyzing the attractor flow for the exact central
charge (
1.4
), first in the Poincaré upper half-plane (§
6.1
) and then in affine coordinates (§
6.2
).
In §
6.3
we then identify the orbifold scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}_{o}
as a particular subset of
\mathcal{D}_{\psi}^{\Pi}

in a region around the orbifold point.
In §
6.4
we describe the full diagram
\mathcal{D}_{\psi}^{\Pi}
and prove the SAFC (Theorem
3
).

6.1.
Exact attractor flow

In this section, we study the attractor flow (
3.20
) for the central charge (
1.4
)
along the slice of
\Pi
-stability conditions, equipped with an general hermitean metric

\mathrm{d}s^{2}=g_{\tau\bar{\tau}}\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\bar{\tau}
.
Using the Eichler integral representation
(
1.5
) for the coefficients
T,T_{D}
in the central charge,
the attractor flow
{\rm AF}(\gamma)
(
3.20
) reduces to

(6.1)

\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{\mathrm{d}\mu}=-g^{\tau\bar{\tau}}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}|Z_{\tau}(\gamma)|^{2}=-g^{\tau\bar{\tau}}(d-r\bar{\tau})\,\overline{C(\tau)}Z_{\tau}(\gamma)

The flow is only meaningful in the region where the charge
\gamma

is populated,
\bar{\Omega}_{\tau}(\gamma)\neq 0
.
As already noted in §
3.5
, the modulus
|Z_{\tau}(\gamma)|=\Im[e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}Z_{\tau}(\gamma)]
decreases along the flow while the argument of
Z_{\tau}(\gamma)
is
preserved, so the trajectories of (
6.1
) are included in
the active ray
\mathcal{R}_{\psi}(\gamma)
defined in (
3.13
)
as the locus
\{\Re[e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}Z_{\tau}(\gamma)]=0,\Im(e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}Z_{\tau}(\gamma))>0,\bar{\Omega}_{\tau}(\gamma)\neq 0\}

inside
\mathds{H}
, where the phase
\psi
is determined by the argument of
Z_{\tau_{0}}(\gamma)

at the starting point
\tau_{0}
. The attractor flow stops when
either a) the flow crosses a wall of marginal stability, after which

\bar{\Omega}_{\tau}(\gamma)
jumps, b)

|Z_{\tau}(\gamma)|
reaches a local minimum in the interior of the upper half-plane,
or c)
\tau
reaches the boundary
\Im\tau=0
.
In §
C.2

we rule out the possibility of an infinitely long flow.

Case a) arises at a point
\mu_{1}
where the quantity
\tilde{W}_{\tau}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\coloneqq\Im[Z_{\tau}(\gamma^{\prime})\overline{Z_{\tau}(\gamma)}]
vanishes for some charge
\gamma^{\prime}
. Since
\tilde{W}_{\tau}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
varies along the flow according to

(6.2)

\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{W}_{\tau}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})}{\mathrm{d}\mu}=-g^{\tau\bar{\tau}}|C|^{2}\left[2\tau_{2}(rd^{\prime}-r^{\prime}d)|Z_{\tau}(\gamma)|^{2}+\tilde{W}_{\tau}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})|d-r\tau|^{2}\right]

the sign of
(rd^{\prime}-r^{\prime}d)\tilde{W}_{\tau}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
is positive for
\mu<\mu_{1}
and negative for
\mu>\mu_{1}
. Thus, the flow crosses from the side of the wall where a two-particle bound state of charges
\gamma
and
\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}
is stable, into the side where the bound state decays.
In particular, it follows from (
6.2
) that the flow cannot cross the same wall more than once,
and that BPS states do not decay as one follows the attractor flow in reverse
[
9
]
.

As for case b), since
C(\tau)\neq 0
for

\Im\tau>0
,
|Z_{\tau}(\gamma)|
can only reach a local
minimum at a point
\tau_{i}
in the interior of
\mathds{H}
if
Z_{\tau_{i}}(\gamma)=0
, but this
is ruled out by the assumption that
\bar{\Omega}_{\tau}(\gamma)\neq 0
along the flow (and the support property). This leaves
case c) with
\Im\tau_{i}=0
. Consider the image of the attractor flow on the quotient
\mathds{H}/\Gamma_{1}(3)
. Since the quotient can be compactified by adding the large volume and conifold points,
\tau_{i}
is either in the orbit of a large volume point or a conifold point, i.e.
\tau_{i}=p/q
with
(p,q)
coprime,
q=0\mod 3
in the first case and
q\neq 0\mod 3
in the second case. Using suitable bounds on the central charge of semi-stable objects in the large volume region, we show in §
C.1
that
\tau_{i}
can only be a conifold point, where some spherical object
E
of charge
\gamma_{C}
becomes massless,
Z_{\tau_{i}}(\gamma_{C})=0
.

To determine
E
, it suffices to find an element
g\in\Gamma_{1}(3)

which maps
\tau=0
to
\tau_{i}=p/q
, and act with the corresponding
auto-equivalence on the object
\mathcal{O}
which is massless at
\tau=0
.
Since
(T,T_{D})=(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{V},0)
at
\tau=0
, where
\mathcal{V}
is the
quantum volume defined in (
1.12
),
the periods at
\tau_{i}
are then

(6.3)

T(\tau_{i})=m+\mathrm{i}q\mathcal{V},\quad T_{D}(\tau_{i})=m_{D}+\mathrm{i}p\mathcal{V}

where
(m,m_{D})
are the off-diagonal elements of the monodromy matrix
M(g)
in
(
A.50
). The charge of the massless object
E
at
\tau=\tau_{i}

is then
\gamma_{C}=[q,p,pm-qm_{D}]
up to overall sign, see Table
1

for examples with
0\leq p\leq q\leq 5
.

Returning to the active ray
\mathcal{R}_{\psi}(\gamma)
, it follows from (
6.3
) that it can only reach the conifold point
\tau_{i}=p/q
if

(6.4)

0=\Re[e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}Z_{p/q}(\gamma)]=(dm-rm_{D}-\textnormal{ch}_{2})\cos\psi+(dq-pr)\mathcal{V}\sin\psi

For generic
\psi
, this is only possible if
\gamma
is a multiple of
\gamma_{C}
,
in which case
\mathcal{R}_{\psi}(\gamma)
coincides with the ray
\mathcal{R}_{\psi}(\gamma_{C})
originating
from
\tau_{i}=p/q
. For special
values of
\psi
such that
\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\coloneqq\mathcal{V}\tan\psi
is rational, namely

(6.5)

\mathcal{V}_{\psi}=\frac{\textnormal{ch}_{2}+rm_{D}-dm}{dq-pr}

it is possible that a ray
\mathcal{R}_{\psi}(\gamma)

with
\langle\gamma_{C},\gamma\rangle=dq-pr\neq 0
originates from the point

\tau_{i}=\frac{p}{q}
,
even though
Z_{\tau_{i}}(\gamma)\neq 0
. Conversely, for the same critical values of
\psi
there can also be rays which terminate at a conifold point, rather than escaping at a large
volume point, as proven in Proposition
8
(this phenomenon occurs for instance in the
\psi\to\psi^{\rm cr}_{-1/2}
limit of Figure
8
).
In the next subsection, we introduce adapted coordinates where these critical rays become
much easier to detect.

6.2.
Affine coordinates

While the attractor flow
{\rm AF}(\gamma)
is complicated on the
\tau
-plane, it becomes a straight line
in affine coordinates
(x,y)
defined globally by
24
24

24

The affine coordinate
y
should not be confused with the refinement variable
y
in the definition of motivic DT invariants.

(6.6)

x=\frac{\Re\left(e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}T\right)}{\cos\psi}\ ,\quad y=-\frac{\Re\left(e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}T_{D}\right)}{\cos\psi}

It is useful to introduce the dual coordinates

(6.7)

\tilde{x}=\frac{\Im(e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}T)}{\cos\psi},\quad\tilde{y}=-\frac{\Im(e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}T_{D})}{\cos\psi}

such that the geometric ray
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}_{\psi}(\gamma)
, oriented along the direction of
decreasing
\Im[e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}Z(\gamma)]=r\tilde{y}+d\tilde{x}
, are given by oriented straight lines

(6.8)

ry+dx-\textnormal{ch}_{2}=0,\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}(r\tilde{y}+d\tilde{x})<0

Notice that for the large volume central charge
Z^{\rm LV}_{(s,t)}
, the
coordinates
(x,y)
coincide with
(s_{\psi},\frac{1}{2}(t_{\psi}^{2}-s_{\psi}^{2}))

defined in (
4.26
) and generalize the coordinates

(x,y)
of
[
26
]
to any
\psi\in(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2})
.
Notably, the image of the upper half-plane

t>0
lies above the parabola
y=-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}
.

In contrast, for the
exact central charge (
1.4
), the image of
the fundamental domain

\mathcal{F}_{C}
and its translates in the
(x,y)
-plane
extend below the parabola
y=-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}
,
while remaining above the parabola
y=-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}-\frac{5}{24}
passing through the images
of the orbifold points at
\tau=\tau_{o}+n
(see Figure
27
)

(6.9)

(x_{o(n)},y_{o(n)})=\left(n-\tfrac{1}{2},-\tfrac{1}{3}-\tfrac{1}{2}n(n-1)\right)

For
\psi=0
, the preimage of the region
y>-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}

includes the domain
\mathds{H}^{\rm LV}
defined by the condition
w>\frac{1}{2}s^{2}
(or equivalently
t>0
).
In fact, the curves
t=0
and
y=-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}
are tangent to the same point

\tau\simeq\frac{1}{2}+0.559926\mathrm{i}
corresponding to
(s,w)=(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{8})
or
(x,y)=(\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{8})
, and are nearly indistinguishable for any
\tau_{1}
,
with the former lying below the latter.
For general
\psi
, using (
A.25
) along the line
\tau_{1}=\frac{1}{2}

we get

(6.10)

y+\frac{1}{2}x=w-\frac{1}{2}s^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\Im T\tan\psi(1+\Im T\tan\psi)

so the relative position of the two curves depends on the
sign of
\Im T\tan\psi
.

Figure 27.
Image of the fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}_{C}
and its translates
\mathcal{F}_{C}(m)

in the
(x,y)
-plane for
\psi=0
(left) and
\psi=-0.9<-\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}
(right). The parabolas
y=-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}

and
y=-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}-\frac{5}{24}
are shown for reference. For any
\psi\in(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2})
,
the map
\tau\mapsto(x,y)
is injective in
\bigcup_{m\in\mathds{Z}}\mathcal{F}_{C}(m)
.

Geometrically, the coordinates
(x,y)
and
(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})
provide two systems of local Darboux
coordinates for the Kähler form

(6.11)

\mathrm{d}x\wedge\mathrm{d}y=\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\wedge\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}=-\frac{1}{4}(\mathrm{d}T\wedge\mathrm{d}\bar{T}_{D}+\mathrm{d}\bar{T}\wedge\mathrm{d}T_{D})

Hence, locally there exists a function
H_{\psi}(x,y)
such that
\tilde{x}=\partial_{y}H_{\psi},\tilde{y}=-\partial_{x}H_{\psi}
, satisfying the Monge-Ampère type equation

(6.12)

(\partial^{2}_{x}H_{\psi})(\partial^{2}_{y}H_{\psi})-(\partial_{x}\partial_{y}H_{\psi})^{2}=1

One easily checks that the Legendre transform to the imaginary part of the tree-level
prepotential (
A.30
),
sometimes known as the Hesse potential
[
63
]
, provides such a function,

(6.13)

H_{\psi}(x,y)=\langle\Im\left[-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{3}\frac{e^{-2\mathrm{i}\psi}}{\cos^{2}\psi}F_{0}\left(\cos\psi e^{\mathrm{i}\psi}(x+\mathrm{i}\tilde{x})\right)\right]+\tilde{x}y\rangle_{\tilde{x}}

where
F_{0}
is normalized such that
T_{D}=-\frac{3}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\partial_{T}F_{0}
(see (
A.30
)). In
the large volume limit, (
6.13
) evaluates to

(6.14)

\displaystyle H^{\rm LV}_{\psi}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3\cos\psi}(x^{2}+2y)^{\frac{3}{2}}-\frac{1}{3}x(x^{2}+3y)\tan\psi

\displaystyle=\frac{t^{3}}{3\cos\psi}+\frac{s}{6}(s^{2}-3t^{2})\tan\psi-\frac{t}{6}(t^{2}-3s^{2})\tan^{2}\psi

An explicit computation shows that the Hessian matrix of
H_{\psi}
with respect to
(x,y)
coincides with that of
H^{\rm LV}_{\psi}
(i.e. the Hessian is insensitive to the factor
C(u)
appearing in the Eichler integral (
1.5
)), namely

(6.15)

\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{xx}H_{\psi}&\partial_{xy}H_{\psi}\\
\partial_{xy}H_{\psi}&\partial_{yy}H_{\psi}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{\tau_{2}}\begin{pmatrix}|\tau|^{2}&\tau_{1}\\
\tau_{1}&1\end{pmatrix}

In particular it is positive definite. This observation allows to write the orientation condition
in (
6.8
) purely in terms of the coordinates
(x,y)
: indeed, it becomes

(6.16)

-\frac{1}{r}\left[r^{2}\partial_{x}^{2}H_{\psi}-2dr\partial_{x}\partial_{y}H_{\psi}+d^{2}\partial_{y}^{2}H_{\psi}\right]\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{d}\mu}<0

Thus we conclude that for
r\neq 0
the ray is oriented such that
r\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{d}\mu}>0
. In particular,
the electric potential
\varphi_{x}(\gamma)=2(d-xr)

(generalizing (
4.10
) for the exact central charge)
decreases along the ray. When
r=0
, we get instead
\frac{\mathrm{d}(d\tilde{x})}{\mathrm{d}\mu}=\frac{d}{\tau_{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}\mu}<0
, so the vertical ray is oriented towards decreasing
y
for
d>0
(or increasing

y
for
d<0
).

We now determine the images of the conifold points in the
(x,y)
plane. Using (
6.3
),
we find that the point
\tau=p/q
where the object of charge
\gamma_{C}=[q,p,pm-qm_{D}]
becomes massless maps to

(6.17)

(x_{C},y_{C})=(m+q\mathcal{V}_{\psi},-m_{D}-p\mathcal{V}_{\psi})

where
\mathcal{V}_{\psi}=\mathcal{V}\tan\psi
.
In particular, the conifold point
\tau=m
where
the object
\mathcal{O}(m)
becomes massless is mapped to the point

(6.18)

(x_{\mathcal{O}(m)},y_{\mathcal{O}(m)})=\Bigl(m+\mathcal{V}_{\psi},-\frac{1}{2}{m^{2}}-m\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\Bigr)

on the parabola
y=-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{V}_{\psi}^{2}
.
Moreover, the associated rays
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}_{\psi}(\pm\mathcal{O}(m))

are contained in the tangent to the parabola
y=-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}
at
x=m
, just as
for the large volume scattering diagram.
Similarly, the point
\tau=n-\frac{1}{2}
where
\Omega(n+1)
becomes massless is mapped to

(6.19)

(x_{\Omega(n+1)},y_{\Omega(n+1)})=\Bigl(n-\frac{1}{2}-2\mathcal{V}_{\psi},-\frac{1}{2}(n^{2}-n+1)+(2n-1)\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\Bigr)\quad

on the parabola
y=-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}+2\mathcal{V}_{\psi}^{2}-\frac{3}{8}
,
and the corresponding rays
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}_{\psi}(\pm\Omega(n+1))
are contained in the tangent to the parabola
y=-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}-\frac{3}{8}
at
x=n-\frac{1}{2}
.
The corresponding points are marked in Figure
28
.

Figure 28.
Images of the fundamental domains
\mathcal{F}_{o}
,
\mathcal{F}_{o^{\prime}}=\mathcal{F}_{o}(1)
and their
\mathds{Z}_{3}
images in the
(x,y)
plane for
\psi=0
. The vertical black lines are the images of the semi-infinite lines
n+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\sqrt{3}}+\mathrm{i}\mathds{R}^{+}
for
n=-1,0,1
, while the blue and purple lines correspond to their images under
\mathds{Z}_{3}
.

6.3.
Orbifold region

We now discuss the domain of validity of the orbifold quiver description inside the
space of
\Pi
-stability conditions, and how the two-dimensional scattering diagram constructed
in §
5.3
fits in the scattering diagram for the exact central charge.

As explained in §
5.1
,
the quiver description holds in the region
\mathds{H}^{o}\subset\Pi
where the central
charges
Z_{\tau}(\gamma_{i})
of the simple objects belong to a common half-plane.
In that region, the heart
\mathcal{A}(\tau)
is equivalent under
\widetilde{GL^{+}}(2,\mathds{R})
to the Abelian category
of representations of
J(Q,W)
, and the moduli space of semi-stable objects of phase

\arg Z_{\tau}(\gamma)=\psi+\frac{\pi}{2}
in
\mathcal{A}(\tau)
coincides with the
moduli space of semi-stable representations for King stability parameters

\theta_{i}\propto\Re[e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}Z_{\tau}(\gamma_{i})]
. Choosing the scale
such that
\theta_{i}(\gamma)=\Im[Z(\gamma_{i})\bar{Z}(\gamma)]=\tilde{W}(\gamma,\gamma_{i})
,
we can apply the relation (
6.2
) to obtain the variation of
\theta_{i}
along
the attractor flow
{\rm AF}(\gamma)
,

(6.20)

\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta_{i}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}=-g^{\tau\bar{\tau}}|C|^{2}\left[2\tau_{2}\langle\gamma,\gamma_{i}\rangle|Z(\gamma)|^{2}+|d-r\tau|^{2}\theta_{i}\right]

The second term simply amounts to a rescaling of
\theta_{i}
, while the first term shows that

\theta_{i}
flows towards the self-stability condition
\langle\gamma_{i},\gamma\rangle
.
On the other hand,
since
Z_{\tau}(\gamma_{1})+Z_{\tau}(\gamma_{2})+Z_{\tau}(\gamma_{3})=Z_{\tau}(\delta)=1

for any
\tau\in\mathds{H}
,
it makes more sense to fix the scale by requiring

(6.21)

\theta_{i}=\frac{\Re[e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}Z_{\tau}(\gamma_{i})]}{\cos\psi}

such that
\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}+\theta_{3}=1
. The King stability parameters (
6.21
) are
then linear combinations
of the affine coordinates defined in (
6.6
), such that the lines of gradient flow (
6.8
)
corresponds to straight lines
n_{1}\theta_{1}+n_{2}\theta_{2}+n_{3}\theta_{1}=0
.

Since the central charges
Z_{\tau}(\gamma_{i})
are all equal at the orbifold point
\tau_{o}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}e^{5\pi\mathrm{i}/6}
, the region
\mathds{H}^{o}\subset\Pi
includes an open set around
\tau=\tau_{o}
.
Using the Eichler integral representation (
1.5
), it is immediate to compute
the central charge
to first order in
\tau-\tau_{o}
,

(6.22)

Z_{\tau}(\gamma)\simeq Z_{\tau}^{o}(\gamma)\coloneqq-\frac{r}{3}-\frac{d}{2}-\textnormal{ch}_{2}+C(\tau_{0})(d-r\tau_{o})\,(\tau-\tau_{o})

leading to the central charges for the simple objects

(6.23)

\begin{cases}Z_{\tau}(\gamma_{1})\simeq\tfrac{1}{3}+\tau_{o}\,C(\tau_{o})\,(\tau-\tau_{o})\\
Z_{\tau}(\gamma_{2})\simeq\tfrac{1}{3}-(2\tau_{o}+1)\,C(\tau_{o})\,(\tau-\tau_{o})\\
Z_{\tau}(\gamma_{3})\simeq\tfrac{1}{3}+(\tau_{o}+1)\,C(\tau_{o})\,(\tau-\tau_{o})\\
\end{cases}

Computing the stability parameters via (
6.21
), one finds that the coordinates
(u,v)

on the two-dimensional section (
5.12
) are related to the distance away from the
orbifold point via

(6.24)

\tau-\tau_{o}\simeq\frac{2\mathrm{i}\sqrt{3}}{C(\tau_{o})}e^{\mathrm{i}\psi}(u+\mathrm{i}v)\cos\psi

In particular, as
\psi\to\pm\frac{\pi}{2}
, the scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}_{o}
is mapped to an infinitesimal
neighbourhood of
\tau_{o}
(see Figure
11
).

Going away from the orbifold point, the region
\mathds{H}^{o}
is bounded in the
fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}_{o}

by the condition

(6.25)

\Im(\overline{Z_{\tau}(E_{1})}Z_{\tau}(E_{3}))>0

and similarly by conditions

\Im(\overline{Z_{\tau}(E_{2})}Z_{\tau}(E_{1}))>0
,
\Im(\overline{Z_{\tau}(E_{3})}Z_{\tau}(E_{2}))>0
in the images

M_{o}\mathcal{F}_{o}
and
M_{o}^{2}\mathcal{F}_{o}
under the
\mathds{Z}_{3}
symmetry.
In terms of the variables
(s,w)
defined in (
1.7
), where walls of (anti)marginal stability
are straight lines (see (
2.29
)),
the inequality (
6.25
) in
\mathcal{F}_{o}
amounts to

(6.26)

w+\frac{1}{2}s<0

In that region, the coordinates
(u,v)
on the two-dimensional section of the quiver scattering diagram
can be expressed in terms of the affine coordinates (
6.6
),

(6.27)

u=-\frac{1}{2}x+y+\frac{1}{12},\quad v=-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{3}}\left(x+\frac{1}{2}\right)

such that the rays in the exact scattering diagram map to segments contained in the geometric
rays (
5.13
).

However, while the initial rays
\mathcal{R}_{o}(\gamma_{i})
in the quiver scattering diagram extend to infinity
inside the space of King stability conditions, they actually originate
from conifold points at
\tau=0,-\frac{1}{2},-1
in the slice of
\Pi
-stability conditions.
Using the values of the periods in Table
3
, one finds that
these conifold points are mapped
to points at finite distance in the coordinates defined by (
6.27
).
To make this precise, let us parametrize the initial rays
\mathcal{R}_{o}(\gamma_{i})
in the quiver scattering diagram as

(6.28)

p_{1}(\lambda)=\Bigl(\frac{1}{12}-\frac{\lambda}{2},\frac{-2\lambda-1}{4\sqrt{3}}\Bigr),\quad p_{2}(\lambda)=\Bigl(-\frac{1}{6},\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{3}}\Bigr),\quad p_{3}(\lambda)=\Bigl(\frac{1}{12}+\frac{\lambda}{2},\frac{-2\lambda+1}{4\sqrt{3}}\Bigr)

with
\lambda\in\mathds{R}
.
In this parametrization, the ray
\mathcal{R}_{o}(\gamma_{i})
starts from
\lambda=-\infty

and intersects
\mathcal{R}_{o}(\gamma_{i-1})
and
\mathcal{R}_{o}(\gamma_{i+1})
at
p_{i}(-1/2)

and
p_{i}(1/2)
, respectively, reproducing the intersection points in (
5.14
).

In contrast to the rays
\mathcal{R}_{o}(\gamma_{i})
in the orbifold scattering diagram, the rays

\mathcal{R}_{\psi}(\gamma_{i})
in the slice of
\Pi
-stability conditions start from the images of the conifold points at
\tau=0,-\frac{1}{2},-1
in the
(u,v)
-plane, given by the points
p_{i}(\mathcal{V}_{\psi})

with
\lambda=\mathcal{V}_{\psi}
.
For
\psi=-\frac{\pi}{2}+\epsilon
with
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, these initial points recess to infinity, and one recovers the
infinite two-dimensional scattering diagram constructed above.
Similarly, for
\psi=\frac{\pi}{2}-\epsilon
with
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, the initial points of the rays
\mathcal{R}_{\psi}(-\gamma_{i})

associated to the exceptional collection
E_{i}[1]
recess to infinity.
On the contrary, for
\psi
small enough such that
|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|<\frac{1}{2}
, the initial points are such that the initial rays
\mathcal{R}_{\psi}(\gamma_{i})

can no longer interact among themselves (see Figure
29
).

Figure 29.
Initial rays around the orbifold point in
(u,v)
plane for
\psi=0
(left),
\psi=-\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}
(middle) and
\psi<-\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}
(right). The dashed lines represent the
u=0
and
v=0
axis.
The green curves correspond to the boundary of the region
\mathds{H}^{o}
defined around (
6.25
).
The blue triangle is the face
\Delta_{\psi}
in a tesselation of
\mathds{H}
defined in §
6.4.1
.
As
\psi\to-\frac{\pi}{2}
, the initial points recess to infinity,
and the scattering diagram reduces to Figure
5
.

In between these two regimes, there are critical phases
\psi
such that some ray in the
scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}_{o}
passes through one of the initial points

p_{i}(\mathcal{V}_{\psi})
(see Figure
25
).
The first of these critical values arise from the discrete series of rays

\mathcal{R}_{\psi}(F_{2k}\gamma_{i}+F_{2k+2}\gamma_{i+1})

emitted in the scattering of
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{i+1}
, where
F_{k}

are the Fibonacci numbers. As mentioned above (
1.13
) in the introduction,
this leads to jumps in the topology of the scattering sequence contributing to the
index
\Omega_{\infty}(\gamma)
along the ray
\mathcal{R}_{\psi}(\gamma)
as a function of the phase
\psi
.

6.4.
Exact scattering diagram

Having identified the image of the large volume and orbifold region in affine coordinates,
we are ready to describe the exact scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}_{\psi}^{\Pi}
in the same coordinates.
We prove the Split Attractor Flow Conjecture on the slice of
\Pi
-stability conditions, and give an algorithm to list the finite set of trees that contribute to a given (rational) DT
invariant.
Depending on the phase
\psi
, we find that
\mathcal{D}_{\psi}^{\Pi}
has the following structure,
modulo the action of
\Gamma_{1}(3)
:

•

For small phases
|\psi|<\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}
(where
\psi^{\rm cr}_{\alpha}
was defined in (
1.13
)),

\mathcal{D}_{\psi}^{\Pi}
coincides with the large volume diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}_{0}

in the
(x,y)
plane, except for a shift in the origin of the initial rays.

•

For large phases
\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}<|\psi|<\pi/2
that are not critical (as defined at the end of §
6.3
),
\mathcal{D}^{\rm\Pi}_{\psi}
includes a triangular portion of the orbifold diagram, whose outbound rays seed scattering diagrams in the large volume region.

•

For
\psi=\pi/2
,
\mathcal{D}^{\rm\Pi}_{\psi}
coincides with the
\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}+\theta_{3}=0
slice
of the quiver scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}_{Q}
concentrated at the orbifold point

\tau_{o}
in the
\tau
-plane, supplemented with a vertical ray
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{O}[1])
.

We refrain from discussing critical phases, since additional rays emanating from the conifold points complicate the analysis.
25
25

25

Our proof of the SAFC should go through for critical
\psi
, except that one must prove that the additional initial rays have positive values of the potential
\tilde{\varphi}_{\tau}(\gamma)
defined in (
6.34
), a point which we have not investigated.

6.4.1.
Initial rays and a tessellation

A basic constraint on the scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}_{\psi}^{\Pi}
is that it is invariant under the group
\Gamma_{1}(3)
of auto-equivalences of
D^{b}\Coh(K_{\mathds{P}^{2}})
, generated by tensor product by
\mathcal{O}_{X}(1)
(acting by
T\colon\tau\mapsto\tau+1
) and by the spherical twist
\ST_{\mathcal{O}}
(acting by
V\colon\tau\mapsto\frac{\tau}{1-3\tau}
).
The quotient
\Pi/\Gamma_{1}(3)
has two boundary points from which initial rays could emanate: the large volume point, which is ruled out because central charges grow without bound in this limit, and the conifold point. Without loss of generality, we can thus restrict to initial rays emanating from the point
\tau=0
in
\Pi
. The analysis in (
6.4
) (with
\tau=p/q=0/1
hence
m=m_{D}=0
)
shows a ray with charge
\gamma=[r,d,\textnormal{ch}_{2}]
can only emanate from
\tau=0
if
d\mathcal{V}_{\psi}=\textnormal{ch}_{2}
.
If
\mathcal{V}_{\psi}
is irrational, this is only possible when
d=0=\textnormal{ch}_{2}
. In order to determine
the associated attractor invariant, we use the fact that the orbifold and large volume descriptions are valid in regions
\mathds{H}^{o}
and
\mathds{H}^{\rm LV}
that cover a neighborhood of
\tau=0
.
The outcome of this analysis in §
C.3
is that the initial rays of the exact scattering diagram are
26
26

26

To lighten the notation, we omit the
\psi
-dependence of the rays.

(6.29)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O})\colon

\displaystyle\Omega([k,0,0])

\displaystyle=\delta_{k,1},\quad

\displaystyle\text{and its }\Gamma_{1}(3)\text{ images,}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}[1])\colon

\displaystyle\Omega([-k,0,0])

\displaystyle=\delta_{k,1},\quad

\displaystyle\text{and its }\Gamma_{1}(3)\text{ images,}

provided
\psi
is
not critical
in a sense of Definition
2
.
In particular this holds for all small phases
|\psi|<\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}
.
Notably, since
V\in\Gamma_{1}(3)
maps
\mathcal{O}(0)[n]
to
\mathcal{O}(0)[n+2]
, the initial rays in
\mathcal{D}^{\Pi}_{\psi}
include infinitely many effective rays starting from
\tau=0
, which lie in images of
\mathcal{F}_{C}
that border this point. By a mild abuse of notation we will denote by
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}(0)[n])
these distinct rays, even though their charge vector only depends on the parity of
n
.
More generally, there are an infinite set of rays emanating from every
rational number
\tau=\frac{p}{q}
with
q\neq 0\mod 3
, corresponding to the images of

\mathcal{O}(0)[n]
under
\Gamma_{1}(3)
. We denote by
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}(p/q)[n])
the corresponding effective ray.
For non-critical
\psi
, these images exhaust all initial rays. In particular, there are no rays emanating from the large volume points
\tau=\frac{p}{q}
with
q=0\mod 3
, nor from points with
\tau\notin\mathds{Q}
.

For any
\psi\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
we introduce a
\Gamma_{1}(3)
-invariant tessellation of
\Pi
.
Edges of the tiles consist of all
\Gamma_{1}(3)
images of the straight line joining the conifold points
(x_{\mathcal{O}(-1)},y_{\mathcal{O}(-1)})
to
(x_{\mathcal{O}(0)},y_{\mathcal{O}(0)})
in the
(x,y)
plane.
There are two types of tiles: an orbifold region around each
\Gamma_{1}(3)
image of
\tau_{o}
and a large volume region around each image of
\tau=\mathrm{i}\infty
.
The orbifold region around
\tau_{o}
is the aforementioned triangle
\Delta_{\psi}
, whose corners are images of
\tau=0,-1/2,-1
.
The large volume region
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
around
\tau=\mathrm{i}\infty
maps in
(x,y)
coordinates to the region above the piecewise linear function (or ‘jagged parabola’, depicted in Figure
9
on page
9
)

(6.30)

y\geq\mathsf{p}(x)\coloneqq\Bigl(m+\mathcal{V}_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\Bigr)(\mathcal{V}_{\psi}-x)+\frac{m(m+1)}{2}\quad\text{with }m=\lfloor x-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\rfloor

zigzaging between
27
27

27

To see this,
rewrite
\mathsf{p}(x)=\frac{(\mathcal{V}_{\psi})^{2}-x^{2}}{2}+\frac{(2\{x-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\}-1)^{2}-1}{8}

where
\{x\}=x-\lfloor x\rfloor
denotes the fractional part.

the parabolas
y+\frac{1}{2}x^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{V}_{\psi})^{2}\in\{0,\frac{1}{8}\}
.
The boundary of
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
joins consecutive conifold points
(x_{\mathcal{O}(m)},y_{\mathcal{O}(m)})
,
m\in\mathds{Z}
, so that
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
borders all translates of
\Delta_{\psi}
, while
\Delta_{\psi}
borders three large volume regions.
The initial rays (
6.29
) emanate from vertices of the tessellation by construction, and they enter different regions depending on
\psi
.

6.4.2.
Small phases
|\psi|<\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}

Within
\Delta_{\psi}
, the exact diagram

\mathcal{D}_{\psi}^{\Pi}
coincides with a triangular region of the orbifold diagram
\mathcal{D}_{o}
.
For our first case of interest,
|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|<1/2
, this region lies inside the empty central triangle of
\mathcal{D}_{o}
, thus
\Delta_{\psi}
does not contain any ray, as depicted in Figure
29
.
The large volume regions are thus disconnected from each other in
\mathcal{D}^{\Pi}
, so that we can focus on
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
.

The initial rays in that region are
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}(m))
and
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}(m)[1])
, emanating from integer conifold points
\tau=m\in\mathds{Z}
.
In
(x,y)
coordinates, the geometric ray
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}(\pm[1,m,m^{2}/2])
is contained in the line
y+mx=m^{2}/2
tangent to the parabola
y+x^{2}/2=0
at
x=m
.
The active rays
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}(m))
and
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}(m)[1])
emanate leftwards/rightwards from the point
(x_{\mathcal{O}(m)},y_{\mathcal{O}(m)})=(m+\mathcal{V}_{\psi},-m^{2}/2-m\mathcal{V}_{\psi})
on this line: for
\psi=0
this coincides with the point of tangency with the parabola, while for
\psi\lessgtr 0
it is moved left/right along the tangent line.

For
\psi=0
, the initial rays
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}(m))
and
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}(m)[1])
for all
m\in\mathds{Z}
coincide with the initial rays for
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}_{0}
. Under conditions that are fulfilled here, consistent scattering diagrams are uniquely determined by the initial rays, so that
\mathcal{D}^{\Pi}_{0}\cap\diamondsuit_{\psi}
coincides with
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}_{0}
up to the map
\tau\mapsto(x,y)
.
The rest of the exact diagram is completed by
\Gamma_{1}(3)
invariance, namely
\mathcal{D}^{\Pi}_{\psi=0}
consists of all images of
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}
under
\Gamma_{1}(3)
, in which translations simply map
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}
to itself.

In the large volume diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}
, the first intersection along each initial ray is the intersection with the neighboring initial ray, with intersection points

(6.31)

\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}(\mathcal{O}(m))\cap\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}(\mathcal{O}(m-1))=\Bigl\{(x,y)=\Bigl(m-\tfrac{1}{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}m(m-1)\Bigr)\Bigr\}

In other words, along each geometric ray there is a neighborhood of
(x,y)=(m,-m^{2}/2)
in which there is no intersection.
When
\psi
is varied away from zero, the initial point
(m+\mathcal{V}_{\psi},-m^{2}/2-m\mathcal{V}_{\psi})
remains in this intersection-free portion as long as
|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|<1/2
.
We conclude that for small phases
|\psi|<\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}
, the exact diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\Pi}_{\psi}
consists of disjoint copies (images under
\Gamma_{1}(3)
) of
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}
with initial points shifted horizontally by
\mathcal{V}_{\psi}
along the geometric rays.

To prove the (strong) SAFC for
\mathcal{D}^{\Pi}_{\psi}
with
|\psi|<\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}
, we cannot rely on the absence of active rays in
\Delta_{\psi}
and its
\Gamma_{1}(3)
images, as only the leaves of attractor flow trees are required to be active.
We must instead prove that edges of attractor flow trees cannot enter
\Delta_{\psi}
.
Recall that tree edges are straight line segment in
(x,y)
coordinates.
We call such an edge ‘outbound’ if it lies in a large volume region
g\cdot\diamondsuit_{\psi}
and if
the line segment, prolonged in the attractor flow direction, intersects the boundary
\partial(g\cdot\diamondsuit_{\psi})=g\cdot\mathfrak{p}
.
In other words, an edge is outbound if it moves away from that boundary when following the scattering diagram direction.
For instance, the initial rays
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O})
and
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}[1])
, supported on the line
y=0
, are outbound: indeed, apart from the initial point
(x_{\mathcal{O}(0)},y_{\mathcal{O}(0)})=(\mathcal{V}_{\psi},0)
of these rays, all other vertices
(x_{\mathcal{O}(m)},y_{\mathcal{O}(m)})
of the polygonal curve
\mathfrak{p}
are below
y=0
since

(6.32)

y_{\mathcal{O}(m)}=-\tfrac{1}{2}m(m+2\mathcal{V}_{\psi})\leq 0

thanks to
m
and
m+2\mathcal{V}_{\psi}
having the same sign for
m\in\mathds{Z}
and
|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|<1/2
.
The leaves of an attractor flow tree are among the initial rays, which are
\Gamma_{1}(3)
images of
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O})
and
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}[1])
, hence are outbound.
Two outbound rays
\mathcal{R}(\gamma^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{R}(\gamma^{\prime\prime})
can only intersect if they lie in the same large volume region, and any bound state
\mathcal{R}(n^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}+n^{\prime\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime})
lies in the angular sector between
\mathcal{R}(\gamma^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{R}(\gamma^{\prime\prime})
hence is outbound. We deduce that in an attractor flow tree all rays are outbound and must lie in the same large volume region.
The SAFC for
\mathcal{D}^{\Pi}_{\psi}
is then equivalent to the SAFC for
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}
since trees contributing to a given
\bar{\Omega}_{\tau}(\gamma)
are simply trees in one of the copies of
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}
.
Assuming without loss of generality (up to
\Gamma_{1}(3)
transformation of
\tau
and
\gamma
) that
\tau\in\diamondsuit_{\psi}
, the electric potential
\varphi_{\tau}(\gamma)=2(d-rx)
bounds the number of leaves of the tree as in §
4.2
, while a causality argument (
4.14
) bounds the slope

m\in\mathds{Z}
of the initial rays
which can contribute.
This establishes Theorem
3
for
|\psi|<\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}
.

6.4.3.
Large phases
\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}<|\psi|<\pi/2

More generally, the exact diagram combines aspects of the orbifold and large volume diagrams.
For definiteness, we consider
-\pi/2<\psi<-\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}
, namely
\mathcal{V}_{\psi}<-1/2
.
Then, the
\Gamma_{1}(3)
images of
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O})
enter large volume regions while those of
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}[1])
enter orbifold regions. Specifically,
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O})
enters
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
while its homological shift
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}[-1])
enters
\Delta_{\psi}
.
28
28

28

For
\mathcal{V}_{\psi}>1/2
, the situation is analogous:
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}[1])
enters
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
while
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O})
enters
\Delta_{\psi}
.

In the region
\Delta_{\psi}
the exact scattering diagram coincides with the corresponding portion of
\mathcal{D}_{o}
, as argued in §
6.3
on general grounds.
29
29

29

An alternative proof of the matching is that the three
\mathds{Z}_{3}
images of
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}[-1])
carry the same initial data as in the orbifold diagram and consistency allows for a unique diagram with that initial data.

The initial ray
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O})
, together with rays exiting from
\Delta_{\psi}
to
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
, and all of their translates, then serve as incoming rays for the scattering diagram in the large volume region
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
.

By the same argument as for small
\psi
, all active rays in
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
are outbound (point away from
\mathfrak{p}
in the
(x,y)
plane), and more generally, the
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
portion of any attractor flow tree consists only of outbound rays.
In particular, none of these rays can ever re-enter
\Delta_{\psi}
.
The resulting picture for
\mathcal{D}^{\Pi}
is that three initial rays scatter within
\Delta_{\psi}
, producing some rays that leave
\Delta_{\psi}
towards
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
; these rays further scatter with
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O})
and with translates of all of these rays so as to produce an ever denser set of outbound rays, similarly to the
|\psi|<\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}
case but with additional incoming rays from the orbifold regions.
Throughout this process, only a limited set of initial rays participate in the construction of the scattering diagram in
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
:

(6.33)

\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}(m)[-1]),\ \mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\Omega(m+1)),\ \mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}(m-1)[1])\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}(m))

for each
m\in\mathds{Z}
, which amount to one initial ray from each conifold point
\tau=m-1/2
and three from each integer conifold point.

Let us now prove the Split Attractor Flow Conjecture.
As we have shown, edges of an attractor flow tree cannot exit the orbifold region
\Delta_{\psi}
(in the attractor flow direction).
Thus, for
\tau\in\Delta_{\psi}
the problem reduces to the orbifold diagram, where the dimension vector

\gamma=(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
has a finite number of decompositions into elementary charges of the form
k\gamma_{i}
, and each decomposition leads to a finite number of trees with those leaves.
This proves the conjecture for
\tau\in\Delta_{\psi}
.

For
\tau\in\diamondsuit_{\psi}
, infinitely many leaf rays (
6.33
) could in principle contribute.
Since edges cannot exit orbifold regions, attractor flow trees ending at
\tau
can be sawed into a
trunk
within
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
rooted at
\tau
, with leaves that are initial rays
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{O}(m))
,
and various
shrubs
in
orbifold regions
\Delta_{\psi}(m)
.
This is exemplified in Figure
10
on page
10
.
The trunk must lie in
H\cap\diamondsuit_{\psi}
, where
H
is the convex hull of
\mathfrak{p}=\partial\diamondsuit_{\psi}
and of
\tau
: this is easily seen by induction starting from the root, using the fact that all rays of the tree within
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
are outbound.
Since
H
is contained in a vertical strip of finite width in the
(x,y)
plane, we learn that the set of orbifold regions that can be reached by the split attractor flow starting from
\tau
is finite, therefore attractor flow trees have finitely many possible constituents.

This does not suffice for establishing finiteness however: as in the large volume diagram, some charges of possible constituents are opposite to each other, hence there are still infinitely many decompositions of
\gamma
.
To deal with this, we introduce a variant of the electric potential of the large volume diagram, defined piecewise by

(6.34)

\tilde{\varphi}_{\tau}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}2(d-r\lfloor x-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\rfloor)&\text{in }\diamondsuit_{\psi}\\
c_{\psi}(n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3})&\text{in }\Delta_{\psi}\end{cases}\qquad\text{with }c_{\psi}\coloneqq\frac{4(-2\mathcal{V}_{\psi}-1)}{12\mathcal{V}_{\psi}^{2}+1}

extended to all of
\mathds{H}
by the action of
\Gamma_{1}(3)
.
30
30

30

The definition in
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
is consistent with
\Gamma_{1}(3)
covariance:
\tau\to\tau+1
maps
x\to x+1
and
d\to d+r
.

We shall now prove that the potential is monotonically decreasing from the root to the (sum of) leaves of any attractor flow tree, as in the large volume diagram.
It is manifestly additive in the charge
\gamma
, so that (the sum over all branches of)
\tilde{\varphi}_{\tau}(\gamma)
does not jump at nodes of the tree.
For a given edge in
\Delta_{\psi}
, the potential
\tilde{\varphi}_{\tau}(\gamma)
is constant.
Next, we note that
\tilde{\varphi}_{\tau}(\gamma)
is non-increasing along edges in
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
: if
r=0
this is immediate, while if
r\gtrless 0

it follows from the monotonicity of
x
hence of
\lfloor x-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\rfloor
from (
6.16
).

It remains to check that
\tilde{\varphi}_{\tau}(\gamma)
decreases when an edge
\{ry+dx=\textnormal{ch}_{2}\}
of the tree crosses from
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
to
\Delta_{\psi}
in the attractor flow direction.
The boundary between these regions is the line segment between conifold points
(\mathcal{V}_{\psi}-1,\mathcal{V}_{\psi}-1/2)
and
(\mathcal{V}_{\psi},0)
, namely
\{x-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}=y/(\frac{1}{2}-\mathcal{V}_{\psi})\in(-1,0)\}
.
The intersection point of the two lines is easy to find, and must satisfy
x-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\in(-1,0)
.
In addition, the tangents
(r,-d)
and
(1,1/2-\mathcal{V}_{\psi})
must have the correct relative orientation for the attractor flow to enter
\Delta_{\psi}
:

(6.35)

x-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}=\frac{\textnormal{ch}_{2}-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}d}{d+r(\frac{1}{2}-\mathcal{V}_{\psi})}\in(-1,0),\qquad\left|\begin{matrix}r&-d\\
1&\frac{1}{2}-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\end{matrix}\right|=d+r\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\Bigr)>0

Combining these inequalities yields
\mathcal{V}_{\psi}d-\textnormal{ch}_{2}<d+r(\frac{1}{2}-\mathcal{V}_{\psi})
, or
equivalently
-\frac{d}{2}-\textnormal{ch}_{2}<(d+r)(\frac{1}{2}-\mathcal{V}_{\psi})
.
We deduce

(6.36)

\displaystyle n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}=-r-\frac{3}{2}d-3\textnormal{ch}_{2}

\displaystyle<-r+3(d+r)\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\Bigr)

\displaystyle<\Bigl[\frac{1}{-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}-1/2}+3\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\Bigr)\Bigr](d+r)

In terms of the constant
c_{\psi}
spelled out in (
6.34
), this inequality reads
c_{\psi}(n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3})<2(d+r)
, namely the potential
\tilde{\varphi}_{\tau}(\gamma)
evaluated on the
\Delta_{\psi}
side of the boundary is less than the potential evaluated on the
\diamondsuit_{\psi}
side (to evaluate the latter we have used (
6.35
) to see that
\lfloor x-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\rfloor=-1
).
This establishes that the total electric potential is monotonically decreasing in any tree
in the attractor flow direction.

The final step is to prove that the potential
\tilde{\varphi}_{\tau}(\gamma)
is positive for all initial rays (
6.33
) at their starting point.
Up to translations we only need to consider
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{O})
and the three initial rays of the orbifold diagram.
The ray
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{O})
starts at
(\mathcal{V}_{\psi}-\epsilon,0)
with
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, thus has a positive potential

(6.37)

\tilde{\varphi}(\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{O}))=2(0-\lfloor-\epsilon\rfloor)=2>0

The three initial rays of the quiver diagram have
(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})=(1,0,0)
and permutations thereof, hence
\tilde{\varphi}=c_{\psi}>0
.
We conclude that the number of constituents participating in an attractor flow tree rooted at
\tau
with total charge
\gamma
is at most
\tilde{\varphi}_{\tau}(\gamma)/\min(2,c_{\psi})
.

The SAFC now follows by noting that trees contributing to
\bar{\Omega}_{\tau}(\gamma)
are made of a bounded number of constituents taken among a finite set, hence there are finitely many possible lists of constituents.
For each such list there are finitely many ways that these constituents can be arranged into a topological tree.
For each topology there is at most one attractor flow tree in
\mathds{H}
, constructed by following the attractor flow starting from the root.
There is no ambiguity about where the flow should split: if some edge with charge
\gamma^{\prime}
splits (in the topological tree) into
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}+\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
, then the attractor flow must split when hitting the wall of marginal stability of
\gamma^{\prime}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
, which can only be crossed once according to (
6.2
).
The flow may fail to hit that wall, in which case the topological tree does not correspond to any attractor flow tree.
This establishes Theorem
3
for
\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}<|\psi|<\pi/2
.

6.4.4.
Behavior in the limits
\psi\to\pm\pi/2

For
\psi=\pi/2
, the scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\Pi}_{\psi}
drastically simplifies.
In that case, geometric rays
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}(\gamma)_{\psi}
satisfy

(6.38)

\Im Z_{\tau}(\gamma)=-r\Im T_{D}+d\Im T

thus are included
in contours of constant slope
s=\frac{\Im T_{D}}{\Im T}=\frac{d}{r}
, independent of
the value of
\textnormal{ch}_{2}
(see Figure
11
). The contours only
intersect at points where
s
is ill-defined, i.e. when
\Im T=\Im T_{D}=0
.
These two curves intersect at the orbifold point
\tau_{o}
and
\Gamma_{1}(3)
images thereof.
At
\tau_{o}
, there are three incoming rays

(6.39)

\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}),\ \mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\Omega(1)[1]),\ \mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}(-1)[2])

The first and last emanate from
\tau=0
and
\tau=-1
and lie along the boundary of the fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}_{o}
, where
s=0
and
s=-1
, respectively while
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\Omega(1)[1])
is a vertical line emanating from
\tau=-1/2
, with slope
s=-1/2
.
In addition, there are vertical rays
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}(m)[1])

for any
m\in\mathds{Z}
, which do not interact with the rays (
6.39
).
Consistency at
\tau_{o}
fixes uniquely the outgoing rays, which then continue on towards a large volume limit, either at
\tau\to-\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}\infty
,
\tau\to-\frac{2}{3}+\mathrm{i}0
or
\tau\to-\frac{1}{3}+\mathrm{i}0
.
The rays (
6.39
) are associated to the homological shifts
E_{i}[1]
of the objects
E_{i}
in the exceptional collection (
1.8
). Their scattering diagram around
\tau_{o}
, which we denote by

\mathcal{D}^{\vee}_{o}
, is simply obtained from
\mathcal{D}_{o}

by sending
(u,v)\mapsto(-u,-v)
and reversing the direction of the arrows. Equivalently, we can send

(u,v)\mapsto(-u,v)
and exchange
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{3}
, which amounts to applying the
derived duality (
2.32
) and a suitable translation.
Thus, the scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\Pi}_{\pi/2}
consists of the scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\vee}_{o}

concentrated at the point
\tau_{o}
, the vertical ray
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}[1])
and all
\Gamma_{1}(3)
images
thereof.

Similarly, for
\psi=-\pi/2+\epsilon
with
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, the rays associated to the
objects
E_{i}
in the exceptional collection (
1.8
).

(6.40)

\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}[-1]),\ \mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\Omega(1)),\ \mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O}(-1)[1])

intersect in a region of size
\epsilon
around
\tau_{o}
. As
\epsilon\to 0
, the scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\Pi}_{-\pi/2+\epsilon}
reduces to the orbifold scattering diagram
{\mathcal{D}}_{o}
concentrated in a region of size
\epsilon
at the point
\tau_{o}
, the vertical ray
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}(\mathcal{O})
and all their
\Gamma_{1}(3)
images.

In either case, the SAFC on
\mathcal{D}^{\Pi}_{\pi/2}
reduces to the flow tree formula for quivers, which is proven in
[
22
]
. Moreover, the fact that rays do not intersect away from orbifold points
makes it clear that the index
\Omega_{c}(\gamma)
computed using the quiver
associated to the exceptional collection

E_{i}(k)
will coincide with the Gieseker index

\Omega_{\infty}(\gamma)
along the ray
\mathcal{R}_{\pi/2}(\gamma)
provided

k-1\leq\frac{d}{r}\leq k
for
r\neq 0
, as observed in
[
40
,
23
]
.
This concludes the proof of Theorem
3
for all non-critical phases
\psi\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2]
.

6.5.
Case studies

In this final section, we study the scattering sequences which contribute to the Gieseker index

\Omega_{\infty}(\gamma)
for two simple choices of charges, as function of the phase
\psi
.
The initial rays in these sequences turn out to jump at a subset of the critical
phases of Definition
2
, while the index remains unaffected. This is reminiscent of the ‘fake walls’ encountered in earlier studies
[
64
,
65
,
14
]
,
where the structure of the bound state changes while the index remains unchanged.

We first consider
\gamma=[0,1,1)
, corresponding to the Chern vector of
the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{C}
of a rational curve in the hyperplane class. As noted below (
2.14
), the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\gamma)
is
\mathds{P}^{2}
itself,
so
\Omega_{\tau}(\gamma)
should equal

y^{2}+1+1/y^{2}
for
\tau_{2}\gg 1
and arbitrary values of
\tau_{1}
.
Thus, for any
\psi\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
the scattering diagram should contain a ray
\mathcal{R}^{\rm eff}_{\psi}(\gamma)
reaching

\tau_{2}=\mathrm{i}\infty
with the above index. The scattering sequences which contribute to this index however depend sensitively on the value of
\psi
(which we can assume to be negative,
using the reflection symmetry of the scattering diagram):

•

-\psi^{\rm cr}_{\frac{1}{2}}<\psi<\psi^{\rm cr}_{\frac{1}{2}}\simeq 0.82406
:

T_{0}=\{\mathcal{O}(-1)[1],\mathcal{O}(0)\}

contributing
K_{3}(1,1)
;

•

-\psi^{\rm cr}_{\frac{3}{2}}\simeq-1.27155<\psi<-\psi^{\rm cr}_{\frac{1}{2}}
:

T_{1}=\{2\mathcal{O}(0)[1],\Omega(2)\}

contributing
K_{3}(1,2)
;

•

-\psi^{\rm cr}_{\frac{5}{2}}\simeq-1.38766<\psi<-\psi^{\rm cr}_{\frac{3}{2}}
:

T_{2}=\{3\mathcal{O}(1)[1],\{2\Omega(3),\mathcal{O}(2)[-1]\}\}

contributing
K_{3}(1,2)K_{3}(1,3)
;

•

-\psi^{\rm cr}_{\frac{7}{2}}\simeq-1.43934<\psi<-\psi^{\rm cr}_{\frac{5}{2}}
:

T_{3}=\{3\mathcal{O}(2)[1],\{3\Omega(4),\{2\mathcal{O}(3)[-1],\mathcal{O}(2)[1]\}\}\}

contributing
K_{3}(1,2)K_{3}(1,3)
;

In all cases, the tree produces the desired index
y^{2}+1+1/y^{2}
. More generally, when

(6.41)

-\psi^{\rm cr}_{n+1/2}<\psi<-\psi^{\rm cr}_{n-1/2}\ ,

we find a single tree
T_{n}
with constituents in the positive cone spanned by the exceptional
collection
\langle\mathcal{O}(n)[-1],\Omega(n+1),\mathcal{O}(n-1)[1]\rangle
with
Chern vectors
\gamma_{1}(n),\gamma_{2}(n),\gamma_{3}(n)
, obtained
from (
1.8
) by
n
units
of spectral flow (and homology shift
[-1]
).
The tree
T_{n}
has
n+1
constituents and is of the iterated form

(6.42)

T_{n}=\{k_{1}\gamma_{3}(n),\{k_{2}\gamma_{2}(n),\{k_{3}\gamma_{1}(n),\{k_{4}\gamma_{3}(n),\{k_{5}\gamma_{2}(n),\dots\}\}\}\},

where
k_{i}=3
for
i=1,\dots n-1
and
k_{n}=2,k_{n+1}=1
. Equivalently,
T_{n}
is defined inductively by

(6.43)

T_{n}=\{3\gamma_{3}(n),\sigma_{n}^{-1}\cdot T_{n-1}(1)\}\ ,\quad T_{1}=\{2\gamma_{3}(1),\gamma_{2}(1)\}

where
T(1)
is the tree
T
shifted by one unit of spectral flow, and
\sigma_{n}
acts by cyclic permutation

(6.44)

\sigma_{n}\colon\gamma_{1}(n)\mapsto\gamma_{2}(n)\ ,\quad\gamma_{2}(n)\mapsto\gamma_{3}(n)\ ,\quad\gamma_{3}(n)\mapsto\gamma_{1}(n)

The charges indeed
add up to
(n-1)\gamma_{1}(n)+n\gamma_{2}(n)+(n+1)\gamma_{3}(n)=[0,1,1)
, and the index evaluates to
K_{3}(1,2)K_{3}(1,3)^{n-1}=y^{2}+1+1/y^{2}
. In Figure
30
we plot the scattering sequences for the
first few values of
n
.

Figure 30.
Attractor flow trees contributing to
\gamma=[0,1,-\frac{1}{2}]=[0,1,1)
for
\psi
varying from
-\frac{\pi}{2}
(right) to
\frac{\pi}{2}
(left). The dashed lines corresponding to the incoming rays for
\psi=\psi^{\rm cr}_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\{\pm\frac{1}{2},\pm\frac{3}{2},\pm\frac{5}{2},\dots\}
.

We now consider
\gamma=[1,0,1)
, the Chern vector of the structure sheaf

\mathcal{O}
. This object is spherical and stable throughout the large volume region.
In particular, for large
\tau_{2}
and any
\tau_{1}<0
, the index

\Omega_{\tau}(\gamma)
should equal
1
.

•

In the range
-\frac{\pi}{2}<\psi<\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}
,
there is a single ray originating from the conifold point
\mathcal{O}(0)=\gamma_{1}(0)[1]
;

•

In the range
\psi^{\rm cr}_{\frac{1}{2}}<\psi<\psi^{\rm cr}_{1}
, we find a single sequence

\{3\mathcal{O}(-1),\Omega(0)[1]\}

contributing
K_{3}(1,3)=1
;

•

In the range
\psi^{\rm cr}_{1}<\psi<\psi^{\rm cr}_{\frac{3}{2}}
, we find a single sequence

\{6\mathcal{O}(-2),\{3\Omega(-1)[1],\mathcal{O}(-3)[2]\}\}

contributing
K_{3}(1,3)K_{6}(1,6)=1
;

More generally, for
\psi^{\rm cr}_{\frac{n}{2}}<\psi<\psi^{\rm cr}_{\frac{n+1}{2}}

there is a sequence
T_{n}
obtained inductively through

(6.45)

T_{n}=\{(3n)\gamma_{1}(-n)[1],\sigma_{-n}\cdot T_{n-1}(-1)\}\ ,\quad T_{0}=\{\gamma_{1}(0)[1]\}

with total charge
[1,0,1)
contributing 1 to the index.
In Figure
31
we plot the scattering sequences for the first few values of
n
.

for
