(right) to
\frac{\pi}{2}
(left). The dashed lines corresponding to the incoming rays for
\psi=\psi^{\rm cr}_{\alpha}

with
\alpha\in\{\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2},\dots\}
.

Appendix A
Periods as Eichler integrals

In this section, we review the modular description of the Kähler moduli space of
K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}
,
derive the Eichler integral representation (
1.5
) of the periods
(T,T_{D})
, and use it to obtain asymptotic expansions around the large volume, conifold and orbifold points and the behavior under monodromies. We refer to
[
66
,
67
,
47
,
68
,
69
,
31
,
70
]
for earlier studies in the literature.
31
31

31

We are grateful to Thorsten Schimannek for his help
about the material in this section.

A.1.
Kähler moduli space as the modular curve
X_{1}(3)

Recall that the mirror of
K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}
is a family of genus-one curves
\Sigma(z)=x_{1}^{3}+x_{2}^{3}+x_{3}^{3}-z^{-1/3}x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}=0
in
\mathds{P}^{2}
parametrized by the complex structure modulus
z\in\mathcal{M}_{K}=\mathds{C}\backslash\{0,-\frac{1}{27}\}
, which is identified as the complexified Kähler moduli space of
K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}
. The points
z=0,-\frac{1}{27},\infty
then correspond
to the large radius, conifold and orbifold points, respectively.
Alternatively, we can consider the family of genus-one curves
\Sigma^{\prime}(z^{\prime})\colon x+y+1-z^{\prime}x^{3}/y=0

in
\mathds{C}^{\times}_{x}\times\mathds{C}^{\times}_{y}
,
related to
\Sigma(z)
with
z^{\prime}=-z-\frac{1}{27}
by a 3-isogeny.
The points
z^{\prime}=0,-\frac{1}{27},\infty
then correspond to
the conifold, large radius and orbifold points, respectively.

Figure 32.
Fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}_{o^{\prime}}

centered around the orbifold point
\tau_{o^{\prime}}=e^{\mathrm{i}\pi/6}/\sqrt{3}
, and some of its images. Here
g=ST^{-2}STS\colon\tau\mapsto\frac{\tau-1}{3\tau-2}

and
g^{-1}=TST^{3}S\colon\tau\mapsto\frac{2\tau-1}{3\tau-1}
. The fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}_{o}

is the same domain translated to the interval
[-1,0]
.

The space
\mathcal{M}_{K}
is isomorphic to the modular curve
X_{1}(3)=\mathds{H}/\Gamma_{1}(3)
, where

\Gamma_{1}(3)
is the group of integer matrices
(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix})

such that
a,d=1\mod 3
and
c=0\mod 3
. This is an index 4 subgroup of
PSL(2,\mathds{Z})
, with two cusps of width 1 and 3, corresponding to the large volume and conifold points, respectively, and one elliptic point of order 3 corresponding to the orbifold point.
A convenient choice of fundamental domain is
the domain centered
around the orbifold point at
\tau_{o}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}e^{5\pi\mathrm{i}/6}=(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2\sqrt{3}})
,
shifted horizontally by
-1/2
compared to (
A.2
),

(A.1)

\mathcal{F}_{o}\coloneqq\Bigl\{\tau:\,-1\leq\tau_{1}<0,(\tau_{1}+\frac{2}{3})^{2}+\tau_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{9},(\tau_{1}+\frac{1}{3})^{2}+\tau_{2}^{2}>\frac{1}{9}\Bigr\}

This domain (or rather its translate
\mathcal{F}_{o^{\prime}}\coloneqq\mathcal{F}_{o}(1)
under
\tau\mapsto\tau+1
) is depicted in Figure
32
,
along with several of its images under
\Gamma_{1}(3)
.
Alternatively, one may choose a fundamental domain centered around
\tau=0
,

(A.2)

\mathcal{F}_{C}\coloneqq\Bigl\{\tau:\,-\frac{1}{2}\leq\tau_{1}<\frac{1}{2},(\tau_{1}+\frac{1}{3})^{2}+\tau_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{9},(\tau_{1}-\frac{1}{3})^{2}+\tau_{2}^{2}>\frac{1}{9}\Bigr\}

shown in Figure
6
. It has the nice property of being invariant under the
Fricke involution
\tau\to-1/(3\tau)
.

The isomorphism
\mathcal{M}_{K}\simeq X_{1}(3)
is given explicitly by

(A.3)

J_{3}(\tau)=-15-\frac{1}{z}=\frac{12-405z^{\prime}}{1+27z^{\prime}},

where
J_{3}(\tau)
is the normalized Hauptmodul of
\Gamma_{1}(3)
,

(A.4)

J_{3}\coloneqq\left(\frac{\eta(\tau)}{\eta(3\tau)}\right)^{12}+12=\frac{1}{q}+54q-76q^{2}-243q^{3}+1188q^{4}-1384q^{5}-2916q^{6}+\dots

where
q=e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\tau}
.
The latter maps the points
\tau=\mathrm{i}\infty,0
and
\tau_{o}
to
J_{3}=\infty,12
and
-15
, corresponding to the large volume, conifold and orbifold points, respectively. One easily checks that the Fricke involution maps
J_{3}\mapsto\frac{729}{J_{3}-12}+12
and
z\mapsto z^{\prime}
. Plugging the
q
-expansion
(
A.4
) into (
A.3
), we get

(A.5)

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=-z+15z^{2}-279z^{3}+5729z^{4}-124554z^{5}+2810718z^{6}+\dots

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=-q+15q^{2}-171q^{3}+1679q^{4}-15054q^{5}+126981q^{6}+\dots

The Klein invariant is expressed in terms
of
J_{3}
via

(A.6)

J\coloneqq\frac{E_{4}^{3}(\tau)}{\eta^{24}(\tau)}=J_{3}+744+\frac{196830}{J_{3}-12}+\frac{19131876}{(J_{3}-12)^{2}}+\frac{387420489}{(J_{3}-12)^{3}}

leading to

(A.7)

J=\frac{(216z-1)^{3}}{z(1+27z)^{3}}=-\frac{(1+24z^{\prime})^{3}}{z^{\prime 3}(1+27z^{\prime})}

Using the isomorphism
\mathcal{M}_{K}\simeq X_{1}(3)
, the universal cover of
\mathcal{M}_{K}
is therefore identified with the Poincaré upper half plane, tesselated by an infinite number of copies of the fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}_{C}
.

A.2.
Periods as Eichler integrals

Under mirror symmetry, the central charges
(T(\tau),T_{D}(\tau))
of the D2-brane and D4-brane are identified with periods
(\varpi,\varpi_{D})=(\int_{\ell}\lambda,\int_{\ell_{D}}\lambda)
of a suitable meromorphic differential
\lambda
over a basis of one cycles
(\ell,\ell_{D})
on the mirror curve. Each of these
periods satisfies the degree
3

Picard-Fuchs equation

(A.8)

\bigl[\Theta^{2}+3z(3\Theta+2)(3\Theta+1)\bigr]\Theta\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\varpi\\
\varpi_{D}\end{pmatrix}=0

where
\Theta=z\partial_{z}
. On the other hands, the periods
(\varpi^{\prime},\varpi^{\prime}_{D})=(\int_{\ell}\omega,\int_{\ell_{D}}\omega)
of the unique (up to scale) holomorphic differential
\omega
on the mirror curve
satisfy the degree
2

Picard-Fuchs equation

(A.9)

\left[\Theta^{2}+3z(3\Theta+2)(3\Theta+1)\right]\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\varpi^{\prime}\\
\varpi^{\prime}_{D}\end{pmatrix}=0

It follows that

(A.10)

\Theta\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\varpi\\
\varpi_{D}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\varpi^{\prime}\\
\varpi^{\prime}_{D}\end{pmatrix}

Identifying the modular parameter as the ratio
\tau=\frac{\varpi^{\prime}_{D}}{\varpi^{\prime}}
, we find that
\varpi^{\prime}_{D}
transforms as a modular form of
weight 1 under
\Gamma_{1}(3)
, hence it is given (up to normalization) by the
weight 1 Eisenstein series (denoted by
A
in
[
71
]
),

(A.11)

\varpi^{\prime}(\tau)=1+6\sum_{m\geq 1}\biggl(\sum_{n|m}\chi(n)\biggr)q^{m}=1+6q+6q^{3}+6q^{4}+12q^{7}+\dots

where
\chi(n)=\binom{n}{-3}
is the Dirichlet character equal to
+1
for
n=1\mod 3
,
-1
for
n=2\mod 3
and 0 otherwise. Let
C
be the weight
3
Eisenstein series with the same character,

(A.12)

C(\tau)=1-9\sum_{m\geq 1}\biggl(\sum_{n|m}n^{2}\chi(n)\biggr)q^{m}=1-9\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{n^{2}\chi(n)q^{n}}{1-q^{n}}

This modular form can also be written as an eta product

(A.13)

C(\tau)\coloneqq\frac{\eta(\tau)^{9}}{\eta(3\tau)^{3}}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{n}q^{n}=1-9q+27q^{2}-9q^{3}-117q^{4}+\dots

which makes it clear that it does not vanish anywhere in
\mathds{H}
. The ratio
\frac{C-\varpi^{\prime 3}}{27\varpi^{\prime 3}}
is a meromorphic function on
X_{1}(3)
, which can be shown coincides with
z
. Using the differential identities in
\frac{C-\varpi^{\prime 3}}{27\varpi^{\prime 3}}
, we obtain immediately that
z\partial_{z}=\frac{\varpi^{\prime}}{C}\partial_{\tau}
. Substituting into (
A.10
), we get

(A.14)

\frac{d}{d\tau}\begin{pmatrix}T\\
T_{D}\end{pmatrix}=C\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\tau\end{pmatrix}

Using the values
(T,T_{D})=(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{3})
at the orbifold point
\tau_{o}

[
67
]
we
can write the periods as a holomorphic Eichler integral

(A.15)

\begin{pmatrix}T\\
T_{D}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2}\\
\frac{1}{3}\end{pmatrix}+\int_{\tau_{o}}^{\tau}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
u\end{pmatrix}\,C(u)\mathrm{d}u

Equivalently, using the identity

(A.16)

\int_{\tau_{o}}^{\tau_{o^{\prime}}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
u\end{pmatrix}\,\,C(u)\mathrm{d}u=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}

proven at the end of §
A.6
,
one can also take
\tau_{o^{\prime}}=\tau_{o}+1
as a base point and write

(A.17)

\begin{pmatrix}T\\
T_{D}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\\
\frac{1}{3}\end{pmatrix}+\int_{\tau_{o^{\prime}}}^{\tau}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
u\end{pmatrix}\,\,C(u)\mathrm{d}u

This representation (or equivalently (
A.15
)) provides a global formula for the analytic continuation of
T
and
T_{D}
throughout
the upper half-plane, which gives immediate access to the asymptotic expansions near all singular points and monodromies around them. It also proves to be very efficient for numerical evaluations.
In Table
3
we record the values of the periods at some special points.
Using

\overline{C(\tau)}=C(-\bar{\tau})
and (
A.16
), it is easy to establish the reality properties

(A.18)

\overline{T(\tau)}=-T(-\bar{\tau})\ ,\quad\overline{T_{D}(\tau)}=T_{D}(-\bar{\tau})\ ,

At the end of §
A.6
, we use (
A.18
) to conclude that

\Im T_{D}=0
on the semi-circle
\mathcal{C}(-\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3})
passing through the orbifold point
\tau_{o}
.
As a result, the slope
s=\Im T_{D}/\Im T
vanishes on that semi-circle.

A.3.
Expansion around large radius

In the large radius limit, integrating term-by-term the
q
-expansion of
C
, we get

(A.19)

\int_{\tau_{o}}^{\tau}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
u\end{pmatrix}\,C(u)\mathrm{d}u=\begin{pmatrix}\tau-\tau_{o}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\left(\bar{f}_{1}(\tau)-\bar{f}_{1}(\tau_{o})\right)\\
\frac{1}{2}\tau^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\tau_{o}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\left(\tau\,\bar{f}_{1}(\tau)-\tau_{o}\bar{f}_{1}(\tau_{o})\right)+\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\left(\bar{f}_{2}(\tau)-\bar{f}_{2}(\tau_{o})\right)\end{pmatrix}

where

(A.20)

\bar{f}_{1}(\tau)\coloneqq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}}{n}q^{n}\ ,\quad\bar{f}_{2}(\tau)\coloneqq-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}}{n^{2}}q^{n}

We observe numerically that for
\tau=\tau_{o}
,

(A.21)

\tau_{o}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\bar{f}_{1}(\tau_{o})=-\frac{1}{2}\ ,\quad-\frac{1}{2}\tau_{o}^{2}-\frac{\tau_{o}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\bar{f}_{1}(\tau_{o})+\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\bar{f}_{2}(\tau_{o})=-\frac{1}{8}

Thus we find that

(A.22)

T=\frac{f_{1}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}-\frac{1}{2}\ ,\quad T_{D}=\frac{f_{2}}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}-\frac{f_{1}}{4\pi\mathrm{i}}+\frac{1}{4}

with

(A.23)

f_{1}\coloneqq\log(-q)+\bar{f}_{1}\ ,\quad f_{2}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}[\log(-q)]^{2}+\bar{f}_{1}\log(-q)+\bar{f}_{2}\ ,\quad

Consequently, we have

(A.24)

T=\tau+\frac{\bar{f}_{1}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\ ,\quad T_{D}=\frac{1}{2}\tau^{2}+\frac{1}{8}+\frac{\tau\,\bar{f}_{1}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}+\frac{\bar{f}_{2}}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}

In particular, on any vertical line with
2\tau_{1}\in\mathds{Z}
, we have

(A.25)

\Re T=\frac{\Im T_{D}}{\Im T}=\tau_{1}

since
\bar{f}_{1},\bar{f}_{2}
are real.
In the limit
\tau\to\mathrm{i}\infty
,
\bar{f}_{1}(\tau),\bar{f}_{2}(\tau)
are exponentially suppressed
hence
(T,T_{D})\sim(\tau,\frac{1}{2}\tau^{2})
. Substituting
q
in terms of
z
via (
A.5
),
we find agreement with the usual representations in terms of Meijer G-functions
[
67
]

(A.26)

\displaystyle f_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{3})\Gamma(\frac{2}{3})}G_{3,3}^{2,2}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\frac{1}{3}&\frac{2}{3}&1\\
0&0&0\end{smallmatrix}\middle|27z\right)

(A.27)

\displaystyle f_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[G_{3,3}^{3,1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\frac{1}{3}&\frac{2}{3}&1\\
0&0&0\end{smallmatrix}\middle|27z\right)+G_{3,3}^{3,1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\frac{2}{3}&\frac{1}{3}&1\\
0&0&0\end{smallmatrix}\middle|27z\right)\right]-\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}

Expressing
\tau
and
T_{D}
in terms of the flat coordinate
T
by inverting the
q
-expansions, we
recover the usual expansion in terms of Gromov-Witten invariants,

(A.28)

T_{D}=\frac{T^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{8}+\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\left(-9Q+\frac{135}{4}Q^{2}+244Q^{3}+\frac{36999}{16}Q^{4}+\frac{635634}{25}Q^{5}+307095Q^{6}+\dots\right)

where

(A.29)

Q\coloneqq e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}T}=q+9q^{2}+54q^{3}+246q^{4}+909q^{5}+2808q^{6}+\dots

The expansion (
A.28
) can be integrated term-by-term to obtain the tree-level
prepotential

(A.30)

F_{0}=-\frac{T^{3}}{18}-\frac{T}{24}-\frac{1}{36}+\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}}\left(3Q-\frac{45}{8}Q^{2}+\frac{244}{9}Q^{3}-\frac{12333Q^{4}}{64}+\frac{211878Q^{5}}{125}-\frac{102365Q^{6}}{6}+\dots\right)

such that
T_{D}=-3\partial_{T}F_{0}
.

A.4.
Expansion around conifold point

The expansion near the conifold can be obtained by applying the Fricke involution
\tau\mapsto\tau^{\prime}=-1/(3\tau)
which maps
\tau_{o}
to
\tau_{o^{\prime}}=\tau_{o}+1
and
C
to

(A.31)

C^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})\coloneqq\frac{\eta(3\tau^{\prime})^{9}}{\eta(\tau^{\prime})^{3}}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c^{\prime}_{n}q^{\prime n}=q^{\prime}+3q^{\prime 2}+9q^{\prime 3}+13q^{\prime 4}+24q^{\prime 5}+27q^{\prime 6}+\dots

This is again recognized as an Eisenstein series,

(A.32)

C^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})=\sum_{m\geq 1}\big(\sum_{n|m}n^{2}\chi(m/n)\big)q^{\prime m}=\sum_{n\geq 1}\chi(n)\frac{q^{\prime n}(1+q^{\prime n})}{(1-q^{\prime n})^{2}}

Changing variables
u\to u^{\prime}=-1/(3u)
in (
A.15
) and using
\eta(-1/\tau)=\sqrt{-\mathrm{i}\tau}\eta(\tau)
, we get

(A.33)

\begin{pmatrix}T\\
T_{D}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2}\\
\frac{1}{3}\end{pmatrix}+\mathrm{i}\,3^{\frac{5}{2}}\int_{\tau_{o^{\prime}}}^{\tau^{\prime}}C^{\prime}(u^{\prime})\begin{pmatrix}3u^{\prime}\\
-1\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}

Integrating terms by terms we get

(A.34)

\int_{\tau_{o^{\prime}}}^{\tau}C^{\prime}(u)\begin{pmatrix}3u^{\prime}\\
-1\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}u=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{3}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\left(\tau^{\prime}f^{c}_{1}(\tau^{\prime})-\tau_{o^{\prime}}f^{c}_{1}(\tau_{o^{\prime}})\right)+\frac{3}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\left(\bar{f}^{c}_{2}(\tau^{\prime})-\bar{f}^{c}_{2}(\tau_{o^{\prime}})\right)\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\left(f^{c}_{1}(\tau^{\prime})-f^{c}_{1}(\tau_{o^{\prime}})\right)\end{pmatrix}

where we defined

(A.35)

\displaystyle f^{c}_{1}(\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{c^{\prime}_{n}}{n}q^{\prime n}=q^{\prime}+\frac{3}{2}q^{\prime 2}+3q^{\prime 3}+\frac{13}{4}q^{\prime 4}+\dots

\displaystyle f^{c}_{2}(\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneqq f_{1}^{c}\log q^{\prime}+\bar{f}_{2}^{c}(\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{2}^{c}(\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{c^{\prime}_{n}}{n^{2}}q^{\prime n}=-q^{\prime}-\frac{3}{4}q^{\prime 2}-q^{\prime 3}-\frac{13}{16}q^{\prime 4}-\dots

We observe numerically that for
\tau=\tau_{o}
,

(A.36)

\kappa\,f_{1}^{c}(\tau_{o})=-1,\quad\frac{\kappa}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}f_{2}^{c}(\tau_{o})=\tfrac{1}{2}-\mathrm{i}\,\mathcal{V}

where

(A.37)

\kappa\coloneqq\frac{27\sqrt{3}}{2\pi}\,,\quad\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\frac{27}{4\pi^{2}}\Im\Li_{2}\left(e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}/3}\right)\simeq 0.462758

Thus we find that
T,T_{D}
can be expressed as

(A.38)

T=\frac{\kappa}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}f_{2}^{c}+\mathrm{i}\,\mathcal{V}\ ,\quad T_{D}=-\frac{\kappa}{3}f_{1}^{c}

In particular,
(T,T_{D})=(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{V},0)
at the conifold point. Substituting
q^{\prime}
in terms of

z^{\prime}
using (
A.5
) (with
q\to q^{\prime},z\to z^{\prime})
, we recover the usual expression in
terms of Meijer G-functions, and arrive at an alternative representation for
the ‘quantum volume’
[
66
]
,

(A.39)

\mathcal{V}=\frac{G_{3,3}^{2,2}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\frac{1}{3}&\frac{2}{3}&1\\
0&0&0\end{smallmatrix}\middle|-1\right)}{2\pi\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{3})\Gamma(\frac{2}{3})}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}

The value for
\mathcal{V}
observed numerically in (
A.37
)
can be determined exactly by evaluating (
A.24
) at
\tau=0

using zeta function regularization.
Indeed, the
L
-series of
\bar{f}_{1},\bar{f}_{2}

easily evaluate to

(A.40)

L_{\bar{f}_{1}}(s)\coloneqq\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{c_{m}}{m^{1+s}}=-9\zeta(s+1)\,L(s-1)\ ,\quad L_{\bar{f}_{2}}(s)\coloneqq-\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{c_{m}}{m^{2+s}}=9\zeta(s+2)\,L(s)\

where
L(s)\coloneqq\sum_{m\geq 1}\chi(m)m^{-s}=3^{-s}\left(\zeta(s,\frac{1}{3})-\zeta(s,\frac{2}{3})\right)
is the Dirichlet
L
-series. Its completion

(A.41)

L^{\star}(s)=\left(\tfrac{3}{\pi}\right)^{\frac{s+1}{2}}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{s+1}{2}\right)L_{s}(s)

is analytic for
\Re(s)>1
and invariant under
s\mapsto 1-s
, and so is the completed Riemann zeta function
\zeta^{\star}(s)=\pi^{-s/2}\Gamma(s/2)\zeta(s)
. Using this, one can evaluate the limit as
s\to 0
,

(A.42)

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{1}(0)

\displaystyle=-9\lim_{s\to 0}\zeta(s+1)\,L(s-1)=-\frac{27\sqrt{3}L(2)}{8\pi^{2}}

(A.43)

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{2}(0)

\displaystyle=9\lim_{s\to 0}\zeta(s+2)\,L(s)=\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}

where we used
L(0)=1/3
. This reproduces the expected value
(T,T_{D})=(\mathrm{i}\,\mathcal{V},0)
at
\tau=0
.

A.5.
Orbifold point

Near the orbifold point, integrating the Taylor expansion of
C
term by term we get

(A.44)

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}+C(\tau_{o})(\tau-\tau_{o})+\frac{1}{2}C^{\prime}(\tau_{o})(\tau-\tau_{o})^{2}+\dots

(A.45)

\displaystyle T_{D}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}+\frac{1}{2}C(\tau_{o})(\tau^{2}-\tau_{o}^{2})+\frac{1}{6}C^{\prime}(\tau_{o})(\tau-\tau_{o})^{2}(2\tau+\tau_{o})+\dots

We observe numerically that

(A.46)

\displaystyle C(\tau_{o})

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{3})^{3}}{\Gamma(\frac{2}{3})^{6}}\simeq 3.1185\ ,\quad C^{\prime}(\tau_{o})=-\mathrm{i}\frac{729}{4\pi^{\frac{9}{2}}}\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{3})^{3}\Gamma(\tfrac{7}{6})^{3}\simeq 16.2043\,\mathrm{i}\ ,\quad

(A.47)

\displaystyle J_{3}^{\prime\prime\prime}(\tau_{o})

\displaystyle=\frac{18\sqrt{3}\Gamma(\frac{1}{3})^{9}}{\mathrm{i}\Gamma(\frac{2}{3})^{9}}\simeq-14474\,\mathrm{i}

with the same values for
\tau=\tau_{o^{\prime}}
.
The flat coordinate
w=1/z
is obtained by expanding (
A.3
),

(A.48)

w=-J_{3}-15=\frac{3\sqrt{3}\Gamma(\frac{1}{3})^{9}}{\mathrm{i}\,\Gamma(\frac{2}{3})^{9}}(\tau-\tau_{o})^{3}+\mathcal{O}((\tau-\tau_{o})^{6})

A.6.
Monodromies

The monodromies around the three singular points can be computed by using the transformation property of the Eichler integral,

(A.49)

\begin{pmatrix}T+\frac{1}{2}\\
T_{D}-\frac{1}{3}\end{pmatrix}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}d&c\\
b&a\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}T+\frac{1}{2}\\
T_{D}-\frac{1}{3}\end{pmatrix}\left(\tau\right)+\int_{\frac{d\tau_{o}-b}{a-c\tau_{o}}}^{\tau_{o}}\begin{pmatrix}cu+d\\
au+b\end{pmatrix}\,\,C(u)\mathrm{d}u

whenever
ad-bc=1
,
c=0\mod 3
. The last term is independent of
\tau
, and is a degree 1 period polynomial for the weight 3 modular form
C
.
It follows from (
A.15
) and (
A.49
) that the period vector
\Pi=(1,T,T_{D})

transforms as

(A.50)

\Pi^{t}\mapsto M\Pi^{t}\,\qquad M=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
m&d&c\\
m_{D}&b&a\end{pmatrix}\ ,\quad

where

(A.51)

\begin{pmatrix}m\\
m_{D}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}(d-1)-\frac{c}{3}\\
\frac{1}{3}(1-a)+\frac{b}{2}\end{pmatrix}+\int_{\frac{d\tau_{o}-b}{a-c\tau_{o}}}^{\tau_{o}}\begin{pmatrix}cu+d\\
au+b\end{pmatrix}\,\,C(u)\mathrm{d}u

Consequently, the coordinates
(s,w)
defined in (
1.7
) transform
as

(A.52)

s\mapsto\frac{as+b}{cs+d},\quad w\mapsto\frac{w}{cs+d}+\frac{as+b}{cs+d}m-m_{D}

Under the
\Gamma_{1}(3)
transformations

(A.53)

\tau\mapsto\tau+1\ ,\quad\tau\mapsto-\frac{\tau}{3\tau-1}\ ,\quad\tau\mapsto-\frac{\tau+1}{3\tau+2}

corresponding to monodromies around
\mathrm{i}\infty
,
0
and
\tau_{o}
, we
find, in agreement with
[
40
]
32
32

32

This also agrees with

[
67
]
upon conjugating the matrices in
by
\Bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&0\\
\!\!1/2&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\Bigr)
, due to a shift
T\to T+\frac{1}{2}
.

(A.54)

M_{\rm LV}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
1&1&0\\
\frac{1}{2}&1&1\end{pmatrix}\,\quad M_{C}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&-3\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\,\quad M_{o}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
-\frac{1}{2}&-2&-3\\
\frac{1}{2}&1&1\end{pmatrix}

satisfying
M_{o}=M_{C}M_{\rm LV},M_{o}^{3}=\mathds{1}
.

In the remainder of this section, we prove the identity (
A.16
) and the statement
below (
2.32
) by studying the action of the monodromy
M_{C}
. First, we observe that

M_{C}
maps
\tau_{o}
to
\tau_{o^{\prime}}=\tau_{o}+1
, while preserving
T_{D}
. Thus, (
A.15
)
implies the second equation in (
A.16
), namely

(A.55)

\int_{\tau_{o}}^{\tau_{o^{\prime}}}u\,C(u)\mathrm{d}u=0

As a result,
T_{D}(\tau_{o^{\prime}})=T_{D}(\tau_{o})=1/3
.
Similarly,
M_{C}
maps
T
to
T-3T_{D}
, therefore
T(\tau_{o})=T(\tau_{o^{\prime}})-3T_{D}(\tau_{o^{\prime}})
,
which implies the first equation in (
A.16
),

(A.56)

\int_{\tau_{o}}^{\tau_{o^{\prime}}}C(u)\mathrm{d}u=T(\tau_{o^{\prime}})-T(\tau_{o})=3T_{D}(\tau_{o^{\prime}})=1

Secondly, since
M_{C}
sends
\tau=\tau_{1}+\mathrm{i}\tau_{2}
to

(A.57)

-\frac{\tau}{3\tau-1}=-\frac{3\tau_{1}^{2}+3\tau_{2}^{2}-\tau_{1}}{3(3\tau_{1}^{2}+3\tau_{2}^{2}-2\tau_{1})+1}+\mathrm{i}\frac{\tau_{2}}{3(3\tau_{1}^{2}+3\tau_{2}^{2}-2\tau_{1})+1}

we see that on the half circle
\mathcal{C}(-1/3,1/3)
defined by
3\tau_{1}^{2}+3\tau_{2}^{2}-2\tau_{1}=0
,
the action of
M_{C}
restricts to
\tau\mapsto-\bar{\tau}
.
Since
T_{D}
is invariant under
M_{C}
, it follows that
for any
\tau\in\mathcal{C}(-1/3,1/3)

(A.58)

T_{D}(\tau)=T_{D}(-\bar{\tau})

Since
\overline{T_{D}(\tau)}=T_{D}(-\bar{\tau})
by (
2.32
), it follows that

\Im T_{D}=0
on the half circle
\mathcal{C}(-1/3,1/3)
.
\square

Appendix B
Massless objects at conifold points

The structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}
of
\mathds{P}^{2}
is a spherical object in the derived category
D^{b}(\Coh_{c}K_{\mathds{P}^{2}})
, whose central charge vanishes at the conifold point

\tau=0
. The action of the group

\Gamma_{1}(3)
on the
\tau
upper half-plane lifts to an action by auto-equivalences on the derived category of
K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}
. Thus, for every

g\in\Gamma_{1}(3)
,
E=g(\mathcal{O})
is a spherical object whose central charge vanishes at the conifold point
\tau=g(0)=p/q
with
q\neq 0\mod 3
. In this section we compute the object
E

for low values of
p,q
. The results are summarized in Table
1
on page
1
.

For example, the element

V=(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
-3&1\end{smallmatrix})

(equal to the monodromy around the conifold point
\tau=0
) acts on

\tau
via
V\colon\tau\mapsto-\frac{\tau}{3\tau-1}
, and
on the derived category via the spherical twist
\ST_{\mathcal{O}}
around the spherical object

\mathcal{O}
. The latter is given by the exact triangle (see (
2.20
) and
[
31
, §9.1]
)

(B.1)

\mathrm{Hom}^{\bullet}_{K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}}(\mathcal{O},E)\otimes\mathcal{O}\xrightarrow{\rm ev}E\to\ST_{\mathcal{O}}(E)\xrightarrow{+1}

Similarly for
U=V^{-1}
the action on the derived category is the inverse of the spherical twist
\ST_{\mathcal{O}}
around the spherical object
\mathcal{O}
, which is given, following
Proposition 2.10 of
[
50
]
, by the exact triangle

(B.2)

\ST^{-1}_{\mathcal{O}}(E)\to E\xrightarrow{\rm coev}\Hom(\Hom^{\bullet}_{K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}}(E,\mathcal{O}),\mathcal{O})\xrightarrow{+1}

At the point
\tau=-1/2
, obtained by acting with
VT
on
\tau=0
, the spherical object becoming massless is
\ST_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}(1))
.
We have
\Hom^{0}_{\mathds{P}^{2}}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(1))=\mathds{C}^{3}
,

\Hom^{k}_{\mathds{P}^{2}}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(1))=0
for
k>0
, and

\Hom^{k}_{\mathds{P}^{2}}(\mathcal{O}(1),\mathcal{O})=0
for
k\geq 0
.
Hence, using (
2.18
),

(B.3)

\Hom^{0}_{K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(1))=\Hom^{0}_{\mathds{P}^{2}}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(1))=\mathds{C}^{3}\ ,\quad\Hom^{k\neq 0}_{K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(1))=0

Finally, from the Euler exact sequence

(B.4)

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(1)^{\oplus 3}\rightarrow T\rightarrow 0

where
T
is the tangent bundle of
\mathds{P}^{2}
,
we obtain the exact sequence

(B.5)

0\rightarrow\Omega(1)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 3}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(1)\rightarrow 0

and so an exact triangle

\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 3}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(1)\rightarrow\Omega(1)[1]\xrightarrow{+1}
.
Hence the massless object at
\tau=-\frac{1}{2}
is

(B.6)

\ST_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}(1))=\Omega(1)[1]

For the point
\tau=4/5
, obtained by acting by
TV
on
\tau=-1/2
, the spherical object becoming massless is
\ST_{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega(1)[1])(1)
.
Using (
B.5
) and the Bott vanishing theorem, we obtain

\Hom^{0}_{\mathds{P}^{2}}(\Omega(1),\mathcal{O})=\mathds{C}^{3}
.
On the other hand, using the Bott vanishing theorem and Riemann–Roch formula, we find that

\Hom^{k}_{\mathds{P}^{2}}(\Omega(1),\mathcal{O})=0
for all

k\neq 0
, and

\Hom^{k}_{\mathds{P}^{2}}(\mathcal{O},\Omega(1))=0
for all
k
.
Hence, by (
2.18
),

(B.7)

\displaystyle\Hom^{\bullet}_{K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}}(\mathcal{O},\Omega(1))=\mathds{C}^{3}[-3]

(B.8)

\displaystyle\Hom^{3}_{K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}}(\mathcal{O},\Omega(1))=\mathds{C}^{3}\ ,\quad\Hom^{k\neq 3}_{K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}}(\mathcal{O},\Omega(1))

It follows that the exact triangle defining

E=\ST_{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega(1)[1])(1)
is of the form

(B.9)

\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 3}(1)[-2]\rightarrow\Omega(2)[1]\rightarrow E\xrightarrow{+1}

Note in particular that
E
has rank
-5
and degree
-4
, as expected.

It is important to remark that the map
\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 3}(1)[-2]\rightarrow\Omega(2)[1]
in the derived
category of
K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}
does not come from maps in the derived category of
\mathds{P}^{2}
: indeed all extension groups
\Hom^{3}_{\mathds{P}^{2}}
between sheaves are zero since

\mathds{P}^{2}
is of dimension
2
. As mentioned at the beginning of §
2.2
,
in general, an object
E
in the derived category of coherent sheaves
on
K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}
supported set-theoretically on the zero section can be viewed as a pair
(F,\phi)
with
F
an object in the derived category of
\mathds{P}^{2}
and
\phi\colon F\rightarrow F\otimes K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}

a nilpotent Higgs field. For the object
E
defined by (
B.9
),
since every map
\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 3}(1)[-2]\rightarrow\Omega(2)[1]
in the derived category
of
\mathds{P}^{2}
is zero, the underlying object
F

is simply the direct sum

(B.10)

F=\Omega(2)[1]\oplus\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 3}(1)[-1]

but there is a non-trivial nilpotent Higgs field on
F
coming from a map

\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 3}(1)[-1]\rightarrow\Omega(-1)[1]

in
D^{b}(\Coh\mathds{P}^{2})
.
Note that by Serre duality, we have

(B.11)

\Hom^{2}_{\mathds{P}^{2}}(\mathcal{O}(1),\Omega(-1))=\Hom_{\mathds{P}^{2}}(\Omega(2),\mathcal{O}(1))^{\vee}

which is indeed non-zero.

The same type of analysis provides the exact triangles giving the following
massless objects at other conifold points.

•

\tau=1/5
,
g=U^{2}T^{-1}
.
Because of the relations
(VT)^{3}=1
and
U=V^{-1}
,

(B.12)

UT^{-1}(\mathcal{O})=TVTV(\mathcal{O})=TVT(\mathcal{O}[-2])=\Omega(2)[-1]

In the quiver associated to the exceptional collection
(\mathcal{O},\Omega(2)[-1],\mathcal{O}(1)[-2])
there are 3 relations and no arrows from the second to the first node, so we deduce successively

(B.13)

\displaystyle\Hom^{\bullet}_{K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}}(\Omega(2)[-1],\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle=\mathds{C}^{3}[-2]

(B.14)

\displaystyle\Hom(\Hom^{\bullet}_{K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}}(\Omega(2)[-1],\mathcal{O}),\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 3}[2]

The object
E=U(\Omega(2)[-1])
is then given by the exact triangle

(B.15)

\displaystyle E\to\Omega(2)[-1]\to\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 3}[2]\xrightarrow{+1}

•

\tau=1/4
,
g=UT
. One has
\Hom(\Hom^{\bullet}_{K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}}(\mathcal{O}(1),\mathcal{O}),\mathcal{O})=\Hom(\mathds{C}^{3}[-3],\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 3}[3]
,
thus the object
E=U(\mathcal{O}(1))
is given by the exact triangle

(B.16)

E\to\mathcal{O}(1)\to\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 3}[3]\xrightarrow{+1}

•

\tau=2/5
,
g=UT^{-2}
. One has
\Hom(\Hom^{\bullet}_{K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}}(\mathcal{O}(-2),\mathcal{O}),\mathcal{O})=\Hom(\mathds{C}^{6},\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 6}
,
thus
E=U(\mathcal{O}(-2))
is given by the exact triangle

(B.17)

E\to\mathcal{O}(-2)\to\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 6}\xrightarrow{+1}

•

\tau=1/2
,
g=TVT
. This is a translate of (
B.6
), namely
E=\Omega(2)[1]
.

•

\tau=3/5
,
g=TVT^{2}
. One has
\Hom^{\bullet}_{K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(2))=\mathds{C}^{6}
, hence
E=TV(\mathcal{O}(2))
is given by the exact triangle

(B.18)

\mathcal{O}(1)^{\oplus 6}\to\mathcal{O}(3)\to E\xrightarrow{+1}

•

\tau=3/4
,
g=TVT^{-1}
. One has
\Hom^{\bullet}_{K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(-1))=\mathds{C}^{3}[-3]
, hence
E=TV(\mathcal{O}(-1))
is given by the exact triangle

(B.19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(1)^{\oplus 3}[-3]\to\mathcal{O}\to E\xrightarrow{+1}

•

\tau=1
,
g=T
. Trivially,
E=\mathcal{O}(1)
.

These results are summarized in Table
1
on page

.

Appendix C
Endpoints of attractor flows for local
\mathds{P}^{2}

In this section we derive several bounds on the behaviour of the attractor flow on the slice of
\Pi
-stability conditions in the case
\mathfrak{Y}=K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}
, which we used in §
6.1
.
As explained there, the central charge
Z_{\tau}(\gamma)
is a holomorphic function of
\tau\in\mathds{H}
with no critical point, so that the attractor flow can either end at a marginal stability wall, end at a conifold point, end at a large volume point, or continue indefinitely.
In §
C.1
and §
C.2
we rule out the last two cases in turn.
In §
C.3
, §
C.4
and §
C.5
we determine conditions for an attractor flow to end or start at a conifold point, which leads to the equivalent definitions of the critical phase
\psi
in Definition
2
.

C.1.
Attractor flows avoid large volume points

Let us assume that an attractor flow associated to some
\gamma\in\Gamma
ends at a large volume point, which we take without loss of generality to be
\tau=\mathrm{i}\infty
.
Such a flow
\mu\mapsto\tau(\mu)\in\mathds{H}
would at late times lie in the fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}_{C}
centered around the conifold point
\tau=0
, or its translates centered around
\tau=n
.
By applying a transformation
g(\mu)\in\Gamma_{1}(3)
(which depends discretely on the flow parameter
\mu
), we can map
\tau(\mu)
into
\tilde{\tau}=g\cdot\tau\in\mathcal{F}_{C}
, at the cost of also mapping
\gamma
to
\tilde{\gamma}=g\cdot\gamma
.
The central charge is unchanged, and in particular the mass
|Z_{\tilde{\tau}(\mu)}(\tilde{\gamma}(\mu))|=|Z_{\tau(\mu)}(\gamma)|
is monotonically decreasing, hence lower than its initial value.
We now exclude such attractor flows by proving that this upper bound on
|Z_{\tilde{\tau}}(\tilde{\gamma})|
would translate into an upper bound on
\Im\tau
, regardless of the charge vector
\tilde{\gamma}
(provided the corresponding DT invariant is nonzero).
Passing to the contrapositive statement and dropping tildes,
33
33

33

Importantly, images of
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\mathds{Z}\delta
under
\Gamma_{1}(3)
are in
\Gamma\setminus\mathds{Z}\delta
because
\delta
is invariant.
we shall prove the following statement (recall that

\delta
denotes the D0-brane charge vector).

Proposition 4
.

For every
M>0
, there exists
D>0
such that for every
\tau\in\mathcal{F}_{C}
with
\Im(\tau)>D
, and every
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\mathds{Z}\delta
with
\Omega_{\tau}(\gamma)\neq 0
, one has
|Z_{\tau}(\gamma)|>M
.

The proof relies on approximating the exact central charge
Z
by the large volume central charge (
1.6
)
Z_{(s,t)}^{\rm LV}(\gamma)=-\frac{r}{2}(s+\mathrm{i}t)^{2}+d(s+\mathrm{i}t)-\textnormal{ch}_{2}
, and we begin by proving bounds on it. We denote

(C.1)

Y=\frac{1}{2}(t^{2}-s^{2})

Lemma 5
.

For every
(s,t)
with
Y\geq 0
, and every
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
r\geq 0
and
d^{2}-2r\textnormal{ch}_{2}\geq 0
, one has

(C.2)

|Z_{(s,t)}^{\rm LV}(\gamma)|^{2}\geq r^{2}Y^{2}+\frac{d^{2}}{2}Y

Proof.

We have

(C.3)

\displaystyle|Z_{(s,t)}^{\rm LV}(\gamma)|^{2}

\displaystyle=(rY+ds-\textnormal{ch}_{2})^{2}+(2Y+s^{2})(d-rs)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq r^{2}Y^{2}+2rY(ds-\textnormal{ch}_{2})+2Y(d-rs)^{2}

If
r=0
, this gives
|Z_{(s,t)}^{\rm LV}(\gamma)|^{2}\geq 2d^{2}Y
and so in particular (
C.2
). If
r>0
, we rephrase the inequality in terms of the slope
\mu=d/r
and use the assumption
-\textnormal{ch}_{2}/r\geq-\mu^{2}/2
to obtain

(C.4)

|Z_{(s,t)}^{\rm LV}(\gamma)|^{2}\geq r^{2}\bigl(Y^{2}+Y(2\mu s-\mu^{2})+2Y(\mu-s)^{2}\bigr)

The coefficient of
Y
is
\mu^{2}-2s\mu+2s^{2}\geq\mu^{2}/2
, which yields (
C.2
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
4
.

We write
\tau=\tau_{1}+i\tau_{2}
with
\tau_{1}=\Re\tau
and
\tau_{2}=\Im\tau
.
We denote by
\mathcal{O}(1)
any function on
\mathcal{F}_{C}
which is bounded for
\tau_{2}
large enough, uniformly in
r
and
d
.
For instance,
1/\tau_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\tau_{1}=\mathcal{O}(1)
, as
-\frac{1}{2}\leq\tau_{1}\leq\frac{1}{2}
on
\mathcal{F}_{C}
.
According to the large volume expansion of the periods in §
A.3
,

T=\tau+\mathcal{O}(1)
and
T_{D}=\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(1)
, hence

(C.5)

\displaystyle\Re T

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(1),

\displaystyle\quad\Re T_{D}

\displaystyle=-\tau_{2}^{2}/2+\mathcal{O}(1)

\displaystyle\Im T

\displaystyle=\tau_{2}+\mathcal{O}(1),

\displaystyle\quad\Im T_{D}

\displaystyle=\tau_{1}\tau_{2}+\mathcal{O}(1)=\mathcal{O}(1)\tau_{2}

As a result

(C.6)

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\frac{\Im T_{D}}{\Im T}=\tau_{1}+\mathcal{O}(1)/\tau_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=-\Re T_{D}+s\Re T=\tau_{2}^{2}/2+\mathcal{O}(1)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2w-s^{2}}=\sqrt{\tau_{2}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(1)}=\tau_{2}+\mathcal{O}(1)/\tau_{2}

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=w-s^{2}=\tau_{2}^{2}/2+\mathcal{O}(1)

In particular, large enough
\tau_{2}
,
t
or
Y
are synonymous within
\mathcal{F}_{C}
.
Another consequence is

(C.7)

\tau=s+\mathrm{i}t+\mathcal{O}(1)/\tau_{2},\qquad\tau^{2}=(s+\mathrm{i}t)^{2}+\mathcal{O}(1)

which implies that the large volume central charge is a good approximation of the central charge in the sense that

(C.8)

\displaystyle Z_{\tau}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=-rT_{D}+dT-\textnormal{ch}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{r}{2}(s+\mathrm{i}t)^{2}+d(s+\mathrm{i}t)-\textnormal{ch}_{2}+|r|\mathcal{O}(1)+|d|\mathcal{O}(1)

\displaystyle=Z_{(s,t)}^{\rm LV}(\gamma)+|r|\mathcal{O}(1)+|d|\mathcal{O}(1)

Next, we seek to apply Lemma
5
.
For large enough
\tau_{2}
, the point
\tau\in\mathcal{F}_{C}
does not belong to
the lower boundary of
\mathcal{F}_{C}
, thus
\tau
defines a geometric stability condition.
Hence, by
[
51
]
(Corollary 1.33 in published version, or Corollary 1.30 in arXiv version), if

\Omega_{\tau}(\gamma)\neq 0
, then

\gamma=n\gamma^{\prime}
is a multiple
n\in\mathds{Z}\setminus\{0\}
of the class
\gamma^{\prime}
of a Gieseker semistable sheaf.
The latter obeys
r^{\prime}\geq 0
and
d^{\prime 2}-2r^{\prime}\operatorname{ch}^{\prime}_{2}\geq 0
(see for example
[
41
, Lemma 3.4]
).
Up to replacing
\gamma
by
-\gamma
, which does not change the mass
|Z_{\tau}(\gamma)|
, one can assume that
n>0
, so that
r\geq 0
and
d^{2}-2r\textnormal{ch}_{2}\geq 0
.
Lemma
5
thus applies (for large enough
\tau_{2}
to ensure
Y\geq 0
): for every

\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\mathds{Z}\delta
such that

\Omega_{\tau}(\gamma)\neq 0
, we have

(C.9)

|Z_{(s,t)}^{\rm LV}(\gamma)|\geq\sqrt{r^{2}Y^{2}+\frac{1}{2}d^{2}Y}\geq\frac{1}{2}|r|Y+\frac{1}{2}|d|\sqrt{Y}

where we did not try to optimize the constants.
As a result,

(C.10)

|Z_{\tau}(\gamma)|\geq|Z_{(s,t)}^{\rm LV}(\gamma)|-|r\mathcal{O}(1)|-|d\mathcal{O}(1)|\geq\frac{1}{3}|r|Y/+\frac{1}{3}|d|\sqrt{Y}

for large enough
Y
.
Since we restrict to
\gamma\notin\mathds{Z}\delta
, one has
(r,d)\neq(0,0)
, so that we have proven
|Z_{\tau}(\gamma)|\geq\sqrt{Y}/3
for large enough
Y
, or equivalently for large enough
\tau_{2}
.
This ends the proof of Proposition
4
, which confines any attractor flow away from all large volume points.
∎

C.2.
Attractor flows end at walls or conifold points

We are now ready to prove that an attractor flow cannot continue indefinitely. We denote by
\overline{\mathds{H}}=\mathds{H}\cup\mathds{R}
the closed upper half plane.

Proposition 6
.

For a charge vector
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\mathds{Z}\delta
and a starting point
\tau(\mu_{0})\in\mathds{H}
, consider the attractor flow
[\mu_{0},\mu_{\infty})\ni\mu\mapsto\tau(\mu)
that is maximally extended subject to the condition
\Omega_{\tau(\mu)}(\gamma)\neq 0
.
Then the limit
\tau(\mu_{\infty})=\lim_{\mu\to\mu_{\infty}}\tau(\mu)\in\overline{\mathds{H}}
exists and lies either at a conifold point or on a wall of marginal stability of
\gamma
.

Proof.

The modular curve
X_{1}(3)=\Gamma_{1}(3)\backslash\mathds{H}
has a natural compactification

\overline{X_{1}(3)}\simeq\mathds{P}^{1}
obtained by adding the large volume point
z_{\rm LV}
and the conifold point
z_{C}
.
Let
\pi\colon\mathds{H}\rightarrow X_{1}(3)
be the quotient map.
Then
\pi\circ\tau\colon[\mu_{0},\mu_{\infty})\to X_{1}(3)
takes values in the compact space
\overline{X_{1}(3)}
hence admits at least one limit point
z_{\infty}\in\overline{X_{1}(3)}
. In other words there exists a sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n=1,2,\dots}
that tends to
\mu_{\infty}
and such that
\pi(\tau(\mu_{n}))\to z_{\infty}
.
For later purposes, it is useful to recall that the mass
|Z_{\tau(\mu)}(\gamma)|
is monotonically decreasing hence has a limit as
\mu\to\mu_{\infty}
, which necessarily coincides with the limit of its subsequence
|Z_{\tau(\mu_{n})}(\gamma)|
.

Consider the unique element
g_{n}\in\Gamma_{1}(3)
that maps
\tau(\mu_{n})
to a point
\tau^{\prime}_{n}=g_{n}\cdot\tau(\mu_{n})
in the fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}_{C}
centered on the conifold point, and consider the corresponding charge
\gamma^{\prime}_{n}=g_{n}\cdot\gamma
.
By
\Gamma_{1}(3)
-equivariance,

(C.11)

\Omega_{\tau^{\prime}_{n}}(\gamma^{\prime}_{n})=\Omega_{\tau(\mu_{n})}(\gamma)\neq 0,\qquad|Z_{\tau^{\prime}_{n}}(\gamma^{\prime}_{n})|=|Z_{\tau(\mu)}(\gamma)|\leq|Z_{\tau(\mu_{0})}(\gamma)|

Proposition
4
applied with
M=|Z_{\tau(\mu_{0})}(\gamma)|
implies
that the imaginary parts
\Im\tau^{\prime}_{n}\leq D
must be bounded above by some constant
D
.
This bound excludes
\pi(\tau^{\prime}_{n})=\pi(\tau(\mu_{n}))
from a neighborhood of the large volume point in
\overline{X_{1}(3)}
.
Therefore, the large volume point cannot be a limit point
z_{\infty}
of
\pi\circ\tau
.

Next, assume that the limit point
z_{\infty}
lies in
X_{1}(3)
.
Consider its lift
\tau_{\infty}\in\mathcal{F}_{C}
, and note that
34
34

34

Strictly speaking, if
\tau_{\infty}
lies on the boundary of
\mathcal{F}_{C}
, it can have multiple
\Gamma_{1}(3)
images in the closure
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{C}}
. Then
\tau^{\prime}_{n}
has a subsequence that converges to either of these images, which we then denote
\tau_{\infty}
.

\tau^{\prime}_{n}\to\tau_{\infty}
.
By the support property,
\tau_{\infty}
admits an open neighbourhood
U
on which there are finitely many classes
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma\setminus\mathds{Z}\delta
with non-zero DT invariants and with central charge less than the upper bound
|Z_{\tau(\mu_{0})}(\gamma)|
.
The point
\tau^{\prime}_{n}
lies in
U
for large enough
n
, hence
\gamma^{\prime}_{n}
takes finitely many values. Up to passing to a subsequence we can assume that all
\gamma^{\prime}_{n}=\gamma^{\prime}
are equal to the same charge vector.
Fix an arbitrary (Euclidean) norm
\|\ \|\colon\Gamma\to[0,+\infty)
.
The support property ensures that
\|\gamma^{\prime}\|\lesssim|Z_{\tau}(\gamma^{\prime})|
whenever
\tau\in U
and
\Omega_{\tau}(\gamma^{\prime})\neq 0
, with an implied constant that is uniform in
\tau\in U
. This gives a positive lower bound on
|Z_{\tau^{\prime}_{n}}(\gamma^{\prime})|
, hence on its limit

(C.12)

m_{\infty}\coloneqq\lim_{\mu\to\mu_{\infty}}|Z_{\tau(\mu)}(\gamma)|=\lim_{n\to+\infty}|Z_{\tau(\mu_{n})}(\gamma)|=\lim_{n\to+\infty}|Z_{\tau^{\prime}_{n}}(\gamma^{\prime})|=|Z_{\tau_{\infty}}(\gamma^{\prime})|>0

We learn that the point
\tau_{\infty}\in\mathds{H}
is not a critical point of
Z_{\tau}(\gamma^{\prime})
and the gradient flow is smooth near
\tau_{\infty}
.
Therefore, near
\tau_{\infty}
there exists local coordinates
m=|Z_{\tau}(\gamma^{\prime})|
along attractor flow lines and
\ell
parametrizing the different flow lines: the attractor flow keeps
\ell
constant and decreases
m
.
Consider a neighborhood that is rectangular in these coordinates,

(C.13)

V=(\ell_{-},\ell_{+})\times(m_{-},m_{+})\ni(\ell_{\infty},m_{\infty})=\tau_{\infty}

For large enough
n
we have
\tau^{\prime}_{n}=(\ell_{n},m_{n})\in V
. The gradient flow of
|Z_{\tau}(\gamma^{\prime})|
starting from this point is
(\ell_{n},m)
with
m
decreasing from
m_{n}
all the way to
m_{-}
at the boundary of
V
.
Since
m_{-}<m_{\infty}
, the attractor flow must stop before, and specifically (
C.12
) requires the attractor flow to stop precisely at
m=m_{\infty}
.
Recall now that
\tau^{\prime}_{n}=g_{n}\cdot\tau(\mu_{n})
.
The image
\{g_{n}^{-1}\cdot(\ell_{n},m),m_{\infty}<m\leq m_{n}\}
of the gradient flow of
|Z_{\tau}(\gamma^{\prime})|
is the gradient flow of
|Z_{\tau}(\gamma)|
starting from
\tau(\mu_{n})
, which is precisely the attractor flow.
We have thus fully determined the end segment of the attractor flow: the end point
\tau(\mu_{\infty})=g_{n}^{-1}\cdot(\ell_{n},m_{\infty})
of the attractor flow exists.
The gradient flow of
|Z_{\tau}(\gamma)|
could continue unimpeded beyond
m=m_{\infty}
, hence what stops the attractor flow must be that
\Omega_{\tau}(\gamma)=0
for
\tau=g_{n}^{-1}\cdot(\ell_{n},m)
with
m<m_{\infty}
(at least, close to
m_{\infty}
).
This means that
\tau(\mu_{\infty})
is on a wall of marginal stability.

It remains to treat the case where none of the limit points of
\pi\circ\tau
are of the above type, in which case the only remaining possibility for the limit point is the conifold point
z_{\infty}=z_{C}\in\overline{X_{1}(3)}
.
Since this is a unique limit point, we have
\lim_{\mu\to\mu_{\infty}}\pi(\tau(\mu))=z_{C}
.
For a constant
0<D<\Im\tau_{o}
, consider the (connected) set
W_{D}=\{\tau\in\mathcal{F}_{C},\Im\tau<D\}
, its projection
V_{D}=\pi(W_{D})\subset X_{1}(3)
, and the union
U_{D}=\pi^{-1}(V_{D})\subset\mathds{H}
of all of its
\Gamma_{1}(3)
images.
The sets
V_{D}
and
U_{D}
are manifestly open.
In fact,
V_{D}\cup\{z_{C}\}
is a neighborhood of
z_{C}
in
\overline{X_{1}(3)}
, hence for large enough
\mu
, one has
\pi(\tau(\mu))\in V_{D}
thus
\tau(\mu)\in U_{D}
.
We learn that
\tau(\mu)
remains in a fixed connected component of
U_{D}
for large enough
\mu
.
For instance, the connected component of
U_{D}
containing
W_{D}
consists of the union of images
g\cdot W_{D}
for all elements
g\in\Gamma_{1}(3)
that leave
\tau=0
invariant, so if
\tau(\mu)
lies in this connected component, it can only tend to the conifold point
\tau=0
.
Other connected components are
\Gamma_{1}(3)
images of this one, which implies that
\tau(\mu)
tends to a conifold point
\tau_{C}\in\mathds{Q}\subset\partial\overline{\mathds{H}}
.
∎

C.3.
Initial data of the exact diagram from the large volume diagram

A key ingredient when building scattering diagrams is the initial data: DT invariants along the initial rays, which are rays that do not arise from the scattering of other rays. Initial rays correspond to attractor flows that do not end on a wall of marginal stability, hence that end at a conifold point by Proposition
6
.
In this subsection we identify the DT invariants of such a flow (near a conifold point) to some DT invariants in the large volume diagram. This establishes the equivalence of the characterizations (
2
) and (
3
) of critical phases in Definition
2
.
Using
\Gamma_{1}(3)
invariance we take the conifold point to be
\tau=0
.
We assume
\psi\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
in this section as the scattering diagram for
\psi=\pi/2
is highly degenerate and best treated separately in §
6.4.4
.
We recall
\mathcal{V}_{\psi}=\mathcal{V}\tan\psi
.

Proposition 7
.

Consider an attractor flow that ends at the conifold point
\tau=0
and has
Z_{\tau}(\gamma)\in\mathrm{i}e^{\mathrm{i}\psi}[0,+\infty)
with
\psi\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
.
Then the point
(s,t)=(\mathcal{V}_{\psi},|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
lies on (the closure of) an active ray
\mathcal{R}^{\rm LV}_{0}(\gamma)
of charge
\gamma
in the large volume scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}_{0}
.
Furthermore, each DT invariant
\Omega_{\tau}(k\gamma)
,
k\geq 1
, is eventually constant along the flow close to the conifold point, and coincides with the limit of
\Omega^{\rm LV}_{(s,t)}(k\gamma)
as
(s,t)\to(\mathcal{V}_{\psi},|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
along
\mathcal{R}^{\rm LV}_{0}(\gamma)
in
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}_{0}
.
In particular, either
\gamma=[r,0,0]
with DT invariants
\Omega_{\tau}([k,0,0])=\delta_{k,\sign r}
, or
\psi
is a critical phase in the sense that
(\mathcal{V}_{\psi},|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
is an intersection of active rays in
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}_{0}
.

Proof.

While the statement is expressed in a uniform way, we shall distinguish
d=0
from
d\neq 0
momentarily as they require very different approaches.

There is a
\mathds{Z}
-worth of fundamental domains meeting at this conifold point, acted upon by the monodromy
V\colon\tau\mapsto\frac{\tau}{1-3\tau}
around
\tau=0
.
We use the coordinate
\tau^{\prime}=-1/(3\tau)
, in which the conifold point lies at
\tau^{\prime}\to+\mathrm{i}\infty
. and the monodromy acts as
V\colon\tau^{\prime}\mapsto\tau^{\prime}+1
.
In terms of
q^{\prime}=e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\tau^{\prime}}
(which vanishes at the conifold point), the expansion (
A.38
) reads

(C.14)

T=\mathrm{i}\mathcal{V}+\kappa\tau^{\prime}q^{\prime}(1+o(1)),\qquad T_{D}=-\frac{\kappa}{3}q^{\prime}(1+o(1))

where

o(1)
denotes any function of
\tau^{\prime}
(such as
1/\tau^{\prime}
or
q^{\prime}
) that vanishes as
q^{\prime}\to 0
.
Within an attractor flow, the phase of
Z_{\tau}(\gamma)
is fixed and its modulus is monotonically decreasing, hence
Z_{\tau}(\gamma)=-rT_{D}+dT-\textnormal{ch}_{2}
has a limit as
q^{\prime}\to 0
.

If
d=0
then
Z_{\tau}(\gamma)\to-\textnormal{ch}_{2}
, which does not belong to the half-line
\mathrm{i}e^{\mathrm{i}\psi}[0,+\infty)
unless
\textnormal{ch}_{2}=0
, which corresponds to a charge
\gamma=[r,0,0]
, with
r\neq 0
since
\gamma\neq 0
.
Such a charge is a multiple of the class of sheaves
\mathcal{O}(0)[k]
becoming massless at the conifold point.
It is easy to see that
Z_{\tau}(\gamma)=-rT_{D}=\frac{\kappa}{3}rq^{\prime}(1+o(1))\in\mathrm{i}e^{\mathrm{i}\psi}(0,+\infty)
requires

(C.15)

\tau^{\prime}_{1}=\Re\tau^{\prime}=-n+\frac{1}{4}\sign r+\frac{\psi}{2\pi}+o(1)

for some integer
n\in\mathds{Z}
. Close enough to the conifold point, the
o(1)
term is less than
1/2
, so along a given attractor flow
n
is eventually constant.
Applying a
\Gamma_{1}(3)
translation
V^{n}\colon\tau^{\prime}\mapsto\tau^{\prime}+n
reduces the problem to the case
n=0
, for which
|\tau^{\prime}_{1}|<1/2
close enough to the conifold point.
As the Fricke involution
\tau^{\prime}=-1/(3\tau)
maps the fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}_{C}
to itself, we deduce that
\tau\in\mathcal{F}_{C}
at late enough times along the flow.
In addition,

(C.16)

\sign(\Re\tau)=\sign\Bigl(\frac{-\tau^{\prime}_{1}}{3|\tau^{\prime}|^{2}}\Bigr)=-\sign(\tau^{\prime}_{1})=-\sign r

Let us determine the DT invariants along this attractor flow that eventually lies in
\mathcal{F}_{C}
and ends at
\tau=0
.
It follows from
[
51
, Corollary 1.24]
(Corollary 1.21 of the arXiv version) that
\Omega_{\tau}(\gamma)
does not jump for
\gamma
proportional to
\gamma(\mathcal{O})
and
\tau
geometric (apart from
\gamma\mapsto-\gamma
across the vertical axis).
So it is enough to work at large volume, namely with Gieseker stability.
Let
E
be a Gieseker-stable sheaf of class
\gamma=k\gamma(\mathcal{O})
with
k\geq 1
. It is of slope
\mu=0
and of discriminant
\Delta=0
. Hence,
E
is exceptional in the sense of
[
41
, Section 4.2]
. By
[
41
, Lemma 4.3]
, there is a unique exceptional sheaf of given slope. As
\mathcal{O}
is exceptional of slope
0
, we obtain
E=\mathcal{O}
. The moduli space of stable objects is a point for
k=1
and empty for
k>1
.
So

(C.17)

\Omega_{\tau}(k\gamma(\mathcal{O}))=\delta_{k,1}

for
k\in\mathds{Z}
and for every
\tau\in\mathcal{F}_{C}
with
\Re\tau<0
, and likewise
\Omega_{\tau}(k\gamma(\mathcal{O}))=\delta_{k,-1}
for
\Re\tau>0
.
Thanks to (
C.16
) this translates to the sign condition in the statement of the Proposition.

We henceforth assume that
d\neq 0
.

After proving that the phase must obey
\mathcal{V}_{\psi}=\textnormal{ch}_{2}/d
, our strategy is to show that the attractor flow (near
\tau=0
) lies in the large volume region
\tau\in\mathds{H}^{\rm LV}
, map this point to large volume coordinates
(s,t)
, and finally use that the large volume scattering diagram for non-zero phase
\psi^{\rm LV}=\arg(-\mathrm{i}Z^{\rm LV}_{(s,t)}(\gamma))
coincides with the diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}_{0}
with zero phase up to a further change of coordinates (
4.26
),

(C.18)

\Omega_{\tau}(\gamma)=\Omega^{\rm LV}_{(s,t)}(\gamma)=\Omega^{\rm LV}_{(\tilde{s},\tilde{t})}(\gamma),\qquad\tilde{s}=s+t\tan\psi^{\rm LV},\qquad\tilde{t}^{2}=t^{2}+\bigl(t\tan\psi^{\rm LV}\bigr)^{2}

Our calculations show that
(\tilde{s},\tilde{t})\to(\mathcal{V}_{\psi},|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
as
\tau\to 0
along the flow, which suffices to conclude.

We start by determining how the conifold point is approached.
The central charge has a limit, hence
T
has a limit, and equivalently
\tau^{\prime}q^{\prime}\to c
for some
c\in\mathds{C}
.
Decomposing
\tau^{\prime}=\tau^{\prime}_{1}+\mathrm{i}\tau^{\prime}_{2}
, we see that
\tau^{\prime}_{2}q^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}(\tau^{\prime}_{2}e^{-2\pi\tau^{\prime}_{2}})
vanishes at the conifold point so
\tau^{\prime}_{1}q^{\prime}\to c
.
If
c\neq 0
then
|\tau^{\prime}_{1}|\sim|c|/|q^{\prime}|=|c|e^{2\pi\tau^{\prime}_{2}}
, which means that the phase of
\tau^{\prime}_{1}q^{\prime}
diverges, contradicting
\tau^{\prime}_{1}q^{\prime}\to c
.
Thus
c=0
and altogether
T\to\mathrm{i}\mathcal{V}
.
We learn that

(C.19)

Z_{\tau}(\gamma)=\mathrm{i}\mathcal{V}d-\textnormal{ch}_{2}+\kappa\tau^{\prime}q^{\prime}(d+o(1))

with
\tau^{\prime}q^{\prime}\to 0
.
Along the flow, the central charge is fixed to lie in
Z_{\tau}(\gamma)\in\mathrm{i}e^{\mathrm{i}\psi}[0,+\infty)
and to move towards
0
along this half-line, hence
Z_{\tau}(\gamma)-(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{V}d-\textnormal{ch}_{2})
lies in the same half-line.
This implies (using
-\pi/2<\psi<\pi/2
hence
\cos\psi>0
)

(C.20)

d>0,\qquad\frac{\textnormal{ch}_{2}}{d}=\mathcal{V}_{\psi},\qquad 2\pi\tau^{\prime}_{1}=-2\pi n+\psi+o(1)

where
\mathcal{V}_{\psi}=\mathcal{V}\tan\psi
and
n\in\mathds{Z}
is eventually constant (as in (
C.15
),
\tau\in\mathcal{F}_{C}
if and only if
n=0
).
We then evaluate the large-volume coordinates
s,t
,

(C.21)

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\frac{\Im T_{D}}{\Im T}=-\frac{\kappa}{3\mathcal{V}}e^{-2\pi\tau^{\prime}_{2}}\bigl(\sin\psi+o(1)\bigr)

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=-\Re T_{D}+s\Re T=\frac{\kappa}{3}e^{-2\pi\tau^{\prime}_{2}}\bigl(\cos\psi+o(1)\bigr)

\displaystyle t^{2}

\displaystyle=2w-s^{2}=\frac{2\kappa}{3}e^{-2\pi\tau^{\prime}_{2}}\bigl(\cos\psi+o(1)\bigr)>0

Therefore, close enough to the conifold point, the ray lies in the large volume region
2w>s^{2}
, and one has
\Omega_{\tau}(\gamma)=\Omega^{\rm LV}_{(s,t)}(\gamma)
.

Next we consider the phase
\psi^{\rm LV}=\arg(-\mathrm{i}Z^{\rm LV}_{(s,t)}(\gamma))
of the large volume central charge at
(s,t)
, and evaluate its tangent since this is what appears in the change of coordinates (
C.18
):

(C.22)

t\tan\psi^{\rm LV}=-t\frac{\Re Z^{\rm LV}_{(s,t)}(\gamma)}{\Im Z^{\rm LV}_{(s,t)}(\gamma)}=\frac{\textnormal{ch}_{2}-ds+\frac{r}{2}(s^{2}-t^{2})}{d-rs}=\mathcal{V}_{\psi}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\pi\tau^{\prime}_{2}})

Thus

(C.23)

\displaystyle\tilde{s}

\displaystyle=s+t\tan\psi^{\rm LV}=\mathcal{V}_{\psi}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\pi\tau^{\prime}_{2}}),

\displaystyle\tilde{t}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{t^{2}+\bigl(t\tan\psi^{\rm LV}\bigr)^{2}}=|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\pi\tau^{\prime}_{2}})

As announced, we learn that
(\tilde{s},\tilde{t})
tends to the point
(\mathcal{V}_{\psi},|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
.
By construction,
(\tilde{s},\tilde{t})
moves along the geometric ray
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo,LV}_{0}(\gamma)
of the zero-phase large volume scattering diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\rm LV}_{0}
.
DT invariants
\Omega_{\tau}(\gamma)
near the end of the attractor flow are thus given by DT invariants along the ray
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo,LV}_{0}(\gamma)
near its intersection
(\mathcal{V}_{\psi},|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
with the initial ray
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{\rm LV}([\sign\mathcal{V}_{\psi},0,0])
.
The ray
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo}_{\psi}(\gamma)
is thus active close to
\tau=0
precisely when the ray
\mathcal{R}^{\rm geo,LV}_{0}(\gamma)
is active close to
(\mathcal{V}_{\psi},|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
.
We conclude that there are non-trivial initial rays at
\tau=0
if and only if
(\mathcal{V}_{\psi},|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
is an intersection of active rays.
∎

C.4.
Initial data of the exact diagram from the orbifold diagram

In the previous section we have mapped DT invariants along an attractor flow ending at
\tau=0
to DT invariants in the large volume scattering diagram.
We now map the DT invariants to the orbifold diagram, thus proving the equivalence of the criteria (
2
) and (
4
) in Definition
2
, in terms of attractor flows and of the orbifold diagram.
The latter criterion involves the point
\theta=(0,\frac{1}{2}+|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|,\frac{1}{2}-|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
whose
(u,v)
coordinates are determined from (
5.12
) to be

(C.24)

u=\frac{1}{12}+\frac{1}{2}|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|,\qquad v=\frac{1}{4\sqrt{3}}(-1+2|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)

Recall the functions
p_{j}\colon\mathds{R}\to\mathds{R}^{2}
given in (
6.28
) for
j=1,2,3
parametrizing the three initial rays of the orbifold diagram.
The point (
C.24
) involved in Definition
2
is
p_{1}(-|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
. By
\mathds{Z}_{3}
-invariance (cyclic permutations of the
\theta_{j}
) one can replace this point by
p_{3}(-|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
, which will appear more naturally in this section.
Rather than repeating what can already be learned about
\gamma=[k,0,0)
from Proposition
7
, we restrict our attention immediately to attractor flows with
\gamma\notin[1,0,0)
.

Proposition 8
.

Consider an attractor flow that ends at the conifold point
\tau=0
and has
Z_{\tau}(\gamma)\in\mathrm{i}e^{\mathrm{i}\psi}[0,+\infty)
with
-\pi/2<\psi<\pi/2
and with
\gamma\notin[1,0,0)\mathds{Z}
. Then the point
p_{3}(-|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
is a ray intersection in
\mathcal{D}_{o}
.

Proof.

For
|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|<1/2
there are no such ray intersections in
\mathcal{D}_{o}
, and we have shown as part of Proposition
7
that there is no such attractor flow.
We thus concentrate on
\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\leq-1/2
, fixing the sign to be negative by using the
\psi\mapsto-\psi
symmetry.
In other words,
-\pi/2<\psi\leq-\psi^{\rm cr}_{1/2}
.

A translation
V^{n}\colon\tau^{\prime}\mapsto\tau^{\prime}+n
maps the attractor flow (
C.20
) to that with
n=0
,

(C.25)

\tau^{\prime}_{1}=\psi/(2\pi)+o(1)\in(-1/2,0)

As discussed below (
C.15
), this condition on
\tau^{\prime}
implies that
\tau\in\mathcal{F}_{C}
. Furthermore, the sign
\sign(\Re\tau)=-\sign(\tau^{\prime}_{1})=1
implies that
\tau\in\mathcal{F}_{o^{\prime}}=\mathcal{F}_{o}(1)
.
Within the orbifold fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}_{o}
, the region of validity
\mathds{H}^{o}
of the quiver description is described by the inequality (
6.26
)
2w+s<0
, namely
t^{2}<-s(1+s)
for the point
\tau-1\in\mathcal{F}_{o}
.
Since
s(\tau-1)=s(\tau)-1
and
t^{2}
is invariant under translations, this condition reduces to
t^{2}<(1-s)s
, namely
2w<s
, for the point
\tau\in\mathcal{F}_{o^{\prime}}
.
Then, thanks to the asymptotics (
C.21
), we evaluate

(C.26)

s-2w=\frac{\kappa\cos\psi}{3\mathcal{V}^{2}}e^{-2\pi\tau^{\prime}_{2}}\bigl(-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}-2\mathcal{V}^{2}+o(1)\bigr)

Since
-\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\geq 1/2>2\mathcal{V}^{2}\simeq 0.4283
, this is positive, which ensures that the attractor flow lies in
\mathds{H}^{o^{\prime}}=\mathds{H}^{o}(1)
and its DT invariants are correctly given by the quiver scattering diagram.

The translated attractor flow
\mu\mapsto\tau(\mu)-1\in\mathcal{F}_{o}
tends to the
\tau=-1
conifold point, hence

(C.27)

(x,y)\longrightarrow(x_{\mathcal{O}(-1)},y_{\mathcal{O}(-1)})=(\mathcal{V}_{\psi}-1,\mathcal{V}_{\psi}-1/2)

where coordinates of the conifold point were calculated in (
6.18
). The corresponding
(u,v)
coordinates are

(C.28)

(u,v)=\biggl(\frac{1}{12}-\frac{1}{2}x+y,-\frac{2x+1}{4\sqrt{3}}\biggr)\longrightarrow\biggl(\frac{1}{12}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{V}_{\psi},\frac{1-2\mathcal{V}_{\psi}}{4\sqrt{3}}\biggr)=p_{3}(\mathcal{V}_{\psi})=p_{3}(-|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)

where
p_{3}
was defined in (
6.28
) and we used
\psi<0
.
Along the attractor flow, the quiver description is valid in a neighborhood of this point, so DT invariants of the exact ray
\mathcal{R}_{\psi}(\gamma)
starting at
\tau=0
coincide with those of the ray
\mathcal{R}_{o}(\gamma)
starting at
p_{3}(-|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
in the orbifold diagram.
In particular, there exists an active ray (with
\gamma\notin[1,0,0]\mathds{Z}
) ending at
\tau=0
if and only if
p_{3}(-|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
is a ray intersection.
∎

C.5.
Attractor flows starting at conifold points

To complete our description of the neighborhood of the conifold point, we now also study the attractor flows that emanate from
\tau=0
, namely
\mu\mapsto\tau(\mu)
with
\mu\in(\mu_{0},\mu_{\infty})
such that
\lim_{\mu\to\mu_{0}}\tau(\mu)=0
.
We establish the equivalence of the characterizations (
1
) and (
4
) in Definition
2
by mapping such flows to rays passing through
p_{1}(-|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
in the quiver.

As before, the central charge must have a limit as
\mu\to\mu_{0}
, but now this limit must be non-zero (as its modulus should decrease along the flow).
This rules out the case
d=0
because
Z_{\tau=0}(\gamma)=-\textnormal{ch}_{2}
cannot be in the open half-line
\mathrm{i}e^{\mathrm{i}\psi}(0,+\infty)
.
Thus,
d\neq 0
. By symmetry under
\psi\mapsto-\psi
, we focus on
\psi\in(-\pi/2,0]
.

Returning to the expansion (
C.19
) of the central charge, we again find that
\tau^{\prime}q^{\prime}
has a limit and that this limit must vanish to avoid a divergent phase.
Thus,
Z_{\tau}(\gamma)\to\mathrm{i}\mathcal{V}d-\textnormal{ch}_{2}
as
\mu\to\mu_{0}
.
Along the flow, the central charge is fixed to lie in
Z_{\tau}(\gamma)\in\mathrm{i}e^{\mathrm{i}\psi}[0,+\infty)
and to move towards
0
along this half-line, hence
(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{V}d-\textnormal{ch}_{2})-Z_{\tau}(\gamma)
lies in the same half-line.
This implies (
C.20
) with a constant shift of
\tau^{\prime}_{1}
,

(C.29)

d>0,\qquad\frac{\textnormal{ch}_{2}}{d}=\mathcal{V}_{\psi},\qquad 2\pi\tau^{\prime}_{1}=-2\pi n+\psi+\pi+o(1)

The integer
n\in\mathds{Z}
can be eliminated by a
\Gamma_{1}(3)
transformation
V^{n}\colon\tau^{\prime}\to\tau^{\prime}+n
.
Then
\tau^{\prime}_{1}\in(1/4,1/2]
, namely
\tau^{\prime}
is in the closure
\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
, which is stable under the Fricke involution, so
\tau\in\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
.
In addition, the sign of
\tau^{\prime}_{1}
yields
\Re\tau<0
, so that
\tau
lies in the closure
\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{o}
of the orbifold fundamental domain.

The expansions of
s
and
w
are then the opposites of (
C.21
), so that for
\psi\in(-\pi/2,0]
we have
w,s\leq 0
close to the conifold point, hence the inequality
2w\leq-s
defining the orbifold region
\mathds{H}^{o}
within
\mathcal{F}_{o}
is satisfied.
DT invariants along the flow are thus correctly given by those of the orbifold diagram
\mathcal{D}_{o}
at a suitable point
(u,v)
.
The affine coordinates (
6.6
) are

(C.30)

x=\frac{\Re(e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}T)}{\cos\psi}=\mathcal{V}_{\psi}+o(1)=-|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|+o(1),\quad y=-\frac{\Re(e^{-\mathrm{i}\psi}T_{D})}{\cos\psi}=o(1).

Thus, (
C.28
) holds as well, and the attractor flow starts (in the conifold limit
\mu\to\mu_{0}
) at the point
p_{1}(-|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
lying on the initial ray
\mathcal{R}_{o}(\gamma_{1})
of the quiver scattering diagram.

As in the previous section, we find that flows with
\gamma\notin[1,0,0]\mathds{Z}
that start (rather than end) at
\tau=0
are given by rays of the quiver scattering diagram that end
35
35

35

We recall the opposite orientation of rays and of the attractor flow.
(rather than start) at
(u,v)=p_{1}(-|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
.
By consistency of the orbifold scattering diagram, the points
p_{1}(-|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
of
\mathcal{R}_{o}
with incoming rays or with outgoing rays are the same, and correspond by Proposition
8
to critical phases.
This situation, in which both incoming and outgoing rays at
\tau=0
occur for a critical phase, is illustrated in Figure
8
for
\mathcal{V}_{\psi}\simeq-1/2
.

Since both incoming and outgoing rays at
\tau=0
are seen in the orbifold diagram, it should be interesting to translate the consistency of the orbifold scattering diagram at
p_{1}(-|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}|)
into a notion of consistency of the exact diagram
\mathcal{D}^{\Pi}_{\psi}
at the conifold point, which is a singular point in the moduli space.

Appendix D
On the mathematical definition of DT invariants

Here we provide mathematical details on the definition of the DT
invariants
\Omega_{\sigma}(\gamma)
. These invariants are a direct generalisation of the integer BPS invariants of
[
72
]
. From
[
73
]
, the objects of a smooth CY3 dg category
\mathcal{C}
(like the dg category of perfect complexes with compact support on a smooth CY3-fold) form a

-1
-shifted symplectic derived stack
\mathcal{M}
in the sense of
[
74
]
. We suppose that
\mathcal{M}
admits an orientation, i.e. a square root of the line bundle
\,{\rm det}\,(\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{M}})
given by the determinant of the cotangent complex of
\mathcal{M}
(a canonical orientation was constructed in the case of sheaves with compact support on noncompact CY3-fold in
[
75
, Theorem 4.9]
). For
\sigma
a stability condition on
\mathcal{C}
,
\sigma
-semistability is a Zariski open condition, hence from
[
76
, Proposition 2.1]
there is an open
-1
-shifted symplectic substack
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}
of
\sigma
-semistable objects. From the definition of semistability,
\Ext^{i}(E,E)=0
for any
E\in\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}
and
i<0
, hence by
[
73
, Proposition 3.3]

\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}}|_{E}=\Ext(E,E)[1]
,
\mathcal{M}
is a
-1
-shifted symplectic Artin–1 stack. We then define
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\gamma)
as the component of
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}
of objects of class
\gamma
in the Grothendieck group of
\mathcal{C}
. Suppose now that
\sigma
is generic, i.e. that two
\sigma
-semistable objects
E,E^{\prime}
of the same phase have collinear charges. For
\gamma
primitive, we define the DT invariants
\Omega_{\sigma}(k\gamma),k\geq 1
by

(D.1)

\displaystyle\Exp\biggl(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\Omega_{\sigma}(k\gamma)}{y^{-1}-y}x^{k}\biggr)\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}H_{c}(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(k\gamma),P_{\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(k\gamma)})x^{k}

where
\Exp
denotes the plethystic exponential,
P_{\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(k\gamma)}
the monodromic mixed Hodge module on
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(k\gamma)
constructed in
[
77
, Theorem 4.4]
using the orientation data, and
H_{c}(M,P)
the Hodge polynomial of the cohomology with compact support on
M
with values in
P
. In the case of quiver with potentials and King stability conditions, the invariants
\Omega_{\sigma}(k\gamma)
are integer for any
\gamma
and
k\geq 1
by
[
72
]
, and we conjecture that this remains true in this more general framework. In particular, we conjecture that, as in
[
72
]
,

(D.2)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\sigma}(k\gamma)=H_{c}\bigl(M_{\sigma}(k\gamma),\mathcal{H}^{1}(\JH_{!}P_{\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(k\gamma)})\bigr)

where
\JH\colon\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(k\gamma)\to M_{\sigma}(k\gamma)
denotes the Jordan-Hölder map to the coarse moduli space,
\JH_{!}
denotes the proper pushforward for the derived categories of monodromic mixed Hodge modules, and
\mathcal{H}^{1}
denotes the first cohomology of a complex of monodromic mixed Hodge modules.

Appendix E
Gieseker indices for higher rank sheaves

In this section, we extend the list of examples in §
4
and determine the trees
contributing to the Gieseker index for some examples with higher rank. As explained in

[
78
,
56
]
, for

(r,d)
coprime and discriminant
\Delta(\gamma)\geq\Delta_{1}(r,d)

large enough, the Gieseker wall is

\mathcal{W}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
due to a subobject with Chern vector
\gamma^{\prime}=[r^{\prime},d^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})

uniquely determined by the following conditions:

•

0<r^{\prime}\leq r
,
\mu(\gamma^{\prime})<\mu(\gamma)

•

Every rational number in the interval
(\mu(\gamma^{\prime}),\mu(\gamma))
has denominator greater than
r

•

The discriminant of any stable bundle of slope
\mu(\gamma^{\prime})
and rank
\leq r
is
\geq\Delta(\gamma^{\prime})

•

the rank of any stable bundle of slope
\mu(\gamma^{\prime})
and discriminant
\Delta(\gamma^{\prime})

is
\geq r^{\prime}

The minimal value
\Delta_{1}(r,d)
for which conditions are applicable and the rightmost point
x_{+}=s_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}+R_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}
of the Gieseker wall
for the lowest discriminant
\Delta_{0}\geq\Delta_{1}
are tabulated in
[
78
, Table 3]
for
r\leq 6
and
0<\mu(\gamma)\leq 1
.

E.1.
Rank 2

We consider rank 2 sheaves with
\gamma=[2,-1,1-n)
, discriminant
\Delta=\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{8}
.
The condition (
2.10
) gives
\Delta\geq\delta_{\rm LP}(-\frac{1}{2})=\frac{5}{8}
for non-exceptional sheaves.
The generating function of Gieseker indices is given by
[
79
]

[

, (A.38)]

\displaystyle+\left(y^{12}+2y^{10}+6y^{8}+13y^{6}+24y^{4}+35y^{2}+41+\dots\right)q^{4}+\dots

\displaystyle\rightarrow q+9q^{2}+48q^{3}+203q^{4}+729q^{5}+2346q^{6}+\dots

Appendix F
Mathematica package
P2Scattering.m

The
Mathematica
package
P2Scattering.m
, available from

https://github.com/bpioline/P2Scattering

provides a suite of routines for analyzing the scattering diagrams considered in this work,
both at large volume, around the orbifold and along the
\Pi
-stability slice. It was used
extensively in order to generate the figures and arrive at the global picture presented in this article.
A list of routines is provided in the documentation
P2Scattering.pdf
available in the
GitHub repository, along with several demonstration worksheets. Here we simply
give a taste of the package capabilities.

After copying file
P2Scattering.m
in the current directory, load the package via

In[1]:
=

SetDirectory[NotebookDirectory[]]; <​​​ < P2Scattering
`

Out[1]:
=

P2Scattering 1.4 - A package for evaluating DT invariants on
K_{\mathds{P}^{2}}

For a given charge
\gamma=[r,d,\chi)
and point
(s,t)
on the large volume slice, the scattering sequences contributing to the index
\Omega_{(s,t)}(\gamma)
can be found by using the routine
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.35,0.75}{\tt ScanAllTrees}}
,
for example for
\gamma=[3,0,0)
through the point
(s,t)=(-\frac{3}{2},2)
,

In[2]:
=

LiTrees = ScanAllTrees[{0, 3, 0}, {-3/2, 2}]

Out[2]:
=

{{-Ch[-3], Ch[0]}, {-3 Ch[-2], 3 Ch[-1]}}

In[3]:
=

ScattDiagLV[LiTrees, 0]

Out[3]:
=

In[4]:
=

Limit[EvaluateKronecker[ScattIndex[LiTrees]], y -> 1]

Out[4]:
=

{9,18}

reproducing the GV invariant
N_{3}^{(0)}=27
(compare with §
4.4
).
Note that the current implementation of the routine
ScattIndex

assumes that the index associated to each scattering sequence
is a product of Kronecker indices associated to each
vertex, and may give the wrong result if some of the edges carry non-primitive charges
(see (
4.23
) for an example).
In the case above, it does produce the correct results for both scattering sequences,
\Omega_{\infty}(\gamma)=K_{9}(1,1)+K_{3}(3,3)
. More generally, the routine
IndexFromSequences
[\{\text{\it trees}\},\{s,t\}]

computes the total rational index
\bar{\Omega}_{s,t}(\gamma)

by decomposing each scattering
sequence into attractor flow trees as explained at the end of §
4.2
, and perturbing
the charges of the constituents
\gamma_{i}\to\gamma_{i}+\epsilon_{i}\delta
such that only binary
splittings remain:

In[5]:
=

Limit[Plus@@Flatten[IndexFromSequences[LiTrees, {-3/2, 2}]],
y -> 1]

Out[5]:
=

82/3

consistent with
\bar{\Omega}_{s,t}(3\gamma)=\Omega_{s,t}(3\gamma)+\frac{1}{9}\Omega_{s,t}(\gamma)=27+\frac{1}{3}
.

Similarly, one can find the scattering sequences contributing near
the orbifold point using
McKayScanAllTrees
: for the same charge, corresponding to dimension vector
(0,3,6)
, a single scattering sequence contributes in the anti-attractor chamber, with index 18,

In[6]:
=

LiTrees = McKayScanAllTrees[chiton[{0, 3, 0}]];
LiTrees /. McKayrep

Out[6]:
=

\{\{3\gamma_{2},6\gamma_{3}\}\}

In[7]:
=

Limit[EvaluateKronecker[McKayScattIndex[LiTrees]], y ->1]

Out[7]:
=

{18}

In[8]:
=

Show[McKayInitialRays[2], McKayScattDiag[LiTrees]]

Out[8]:
=

In this example, the index in the anti-attractor chamber differs from the one at large volume,
due to wall-crossing along the circle
\mathcal{C}(-\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2})
.
